These questions test whether you can explain scarcity precisely (unlimited wants, finite resources) and the difference between economic and free goods; examiners reward exact economic definitions rather than everyday meanings.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports say weaker candidates use the everyday meaning of a ‘free good’ (something given away at no charge) instead of the economic one: a good that is not scarce, uses no resources and has no opportunity cost. Others explain the economic problem in general terms but never apply it to the group the question names, such as consumers.
0455/22/F/M/25 Q2(a) · February/March 2025 · [2 marks]

Source material: South Korea makes use of both economic goods and free goods.

Identify a difference between economic goods and free goods.

Economic goods are scarce because resources (factors of production) are used up to produce them, so they have an opportunity cost. Free goods are not scarce: they are in unlimited supply, need no resources to produce and so have no opportunity cost.

Why this scores
One mark for the economic-good half and one for the free-good half of the same difference, so the answer states both. Examples earn nothing here, and the examiner report warns against the everyday meaning of ‘free’: a free newspaper is still an economic good, because resources were used to make it.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/24 Q5(a) · May/June 2024 · [2 marks]

Source material: Free goods and economic goods are consumed in Poland.

Identify two free goods.

1. Air.

2. Sunlight.

Why this scores
One mark each. Air, sunlight, water and wind are all accepted. Avoid fish, wood or stone: resources are needed to catch, cut or extract them, so the mark scheme does not accept them.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/22 Q1(c) · October/November 2022 · [2 marks]

Source material: Tourism relies on natural resources including sunshine and beaches.

State why sunshine has no opportunity cost.

Sunshine is a free good: it occurs naturally and is in unlimited supply, so no resources have to be used to produce it. Using sunshine in Ecuador’s tourism industry therefore means nothing has to be given up, so there is no opportunity cost.

Why this scores
One mark for identifying sunshine as a free good and one for a reason (unlimited supply, not scarce, needs no resources so nothing is sacrificed). The examiner report says vague answers such as ‘sunshine is everywhere’, or answers that only defined opportunity cost, did not score.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/23 Q5(b) · February/March 2023 · [4 marks]

Source material: Consumers experience the economic problem when buying raspberries.

Explain how the economic problem results in consumers having to make choices.

The economic problem is that people’s wants are unlimited but the resources available to satisfy them are limited (finite). This creates scarcity: there is not enough to produce everything that everyone would like.

For consumers, the limited resource is their income. A household in Serbia has only a certain amount of money to spend each week, so it cannot buy everything it wants and has to choose, for example between buying raspberries and buying another fruit or a snack. Choosing one product means giving up the next best alternative, which is the opportunity cost of that choice. Scarcity therefore forces consumers to rank their wants and decide which ones to satisfy.

Why this scores
Marks for the economic problem itself (unlimited wants, limited resources, scarcity) and then for applying it to consumers: limited income means they cannot buy everything, so each purchase has an opportunity cost. The examiner report says many answers explained the economic problem well but did not relate it to consumers, which limited their marks.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

1.2 The factors of production

These questions test the four factors of production, their rewards, and how mobile each factor is; examiners reward precise terms such as ‘resource used to produce goods and services’ and the distinction between occupational and geographical mobility.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner report says a high proportion of candidates asked to define a factor of production simply listed land, labour, capital and enterprise, which is not a definition. Rewards are another trap: some candidates matched rewards to the wrong factor or named the factor itself (machinery, farmland) instead of its reward.
0455/22/F/M/25 Q3(a) · February/March 2025 · [2 marks]

Define factor of production.

A factor of production is a resource, or input, that is used to produce goods and services.

Why this scores
One mark for ‘resource’ or ‘input’ and one for ‘used to produce goods and services’. The examiner report says many candidates just named the four factors, which is not a definition, and the mark scheme gives no marks for examples.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/M/J/25 Q3(a) · May/June 2025 · [2 marks]

Source material: Since 2008, its capital has increased and its land has not changed significantly, but its labour force and its enterprise have decreased.

Identify the rewards to capital and land.

Capital: interest.

Land: rent.

Why this scores
One mark each. The examiner report says weaker answers gave the rewards for the wrong factors, or described the factor (machinery, farmland) instead of its reward. Learn the full set: rent for land, wages for labour, interest for capital and profit for enterprise.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/22 Q5(b) · February/March 2022 · [4 marks]

Source material: Ghana uses much of its agricultural land to grow cocoa.

Explain whether land is mobile.

Most land is geographically immobile. Land is fixed in one place: a cocoa farm in Ghana cannot be moved to another region or country where it might be more productive or where demand is higher. Some natural resources counted as land, such as fish or wild animals, can move from place to place, but they are the exception.

Land can be occupationally mobile. The same piece of land can usually be switched to a different use. Ghana’s farmland could change from growing cocoa to growing other crops, or be cleared for housing, factories or roads if those uses become more profitable. How occupationally mobile it is depends on the type of land: flat, fertile land has many possible uses, whereas a mountainside has very few.

Why this scores
Two marks for each type of mobility: identifying that most land is geographically immobile (1) and explaining that it is fixed in one place (1), then identifying that it is occupationally mobile (1) with an example of a change of use (1). The examiner report credited answers that separated the two kinds of mobility, as this one does.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/25 Q2(c) · May/June 2025 · [6 marks]

Source material: Three of the accountancy firms reduced the number of new workers they hired and the other accountancy firm announced job losses. The ability of workers to gain new jobs is influenced by their mobility.

Analyse the advantages of an increase in labour mobility.

Labour mobility is the ability of workers to change jobs. Occupational mobility is the ability to move from one type of job to another, and geographical mobility is the ability to move from one area to another to work.

Lower unemployment. If accountants who lose their jobs are occupationally mobile, for example because their numeracy and IT skills are transferable, they can move quickly into banking, finance or teaching. If they are geographically mobile, they can move to regions or countries where jobs are available. This reduces structural and frictional unemployment, which means the government spends less on unemployment benefits, receives more income tax and fewer households fall into poverty.

Higher incomes for workers. Mobile workers can move to the jobs that pay best and make the most of their skills, and changing jobs gives them new experience. As a result their productivity and earning power rise, so their standard of living improves.

Fewer labour shortages, so more output. Firms in expanding industries can fill vacancies more easily when workers are able to move to them. This stops shortages of workers pushing up wages and costs, so there is less cost-push inflation, and it allows these firms to expand. The economy’s resources are used more fully, so real GDP and economic growth increase.

A more flexible economy. When consumer demand changes, as it did for accountancy services in 2023, workers can move out of declining industries and into growing ones. Resources are reallocated more quickly to the products consumers want, which reduces surpluses and shortages.

Why this scores
Defines both types of mobility, then builds complete chains for workers (jobs, wages), the government (benefits, tax), firms (shortages, costs) and the economy (output, growth), matching the mark scheme’s points. The examiner report praised answers that distinguished geographical from occupational mobility and analysed the effects on unemployment, firms’ flexibility and output.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/22 Q1(g) · May/June 2022 · [6 marks]

Source material: To achieve this, it announced increased spending on some areas including mental health, child poverty and pollution. … It was, however, difficult to predict firms’ future profits.

Discuss whether or not enterprise is likely to have increased in New Zealand after 2019.

Enterprise is the willingness and ability of entrepreneurs to take risks and organise the other factors of production to set up and run businesses, in return for profit.

Why enterprise might have increased. New Zealand has high productivity, partly because of high investment in capital goods and education. A well-educated population has more people with the skills and confidence to start firms, and high productivity keeps costs down, raising the profits that reward entrepreneurs. The government’s extra spending on mental health, child poverty and pollution also raises total demand. Higher demand increases firms’ expected revenue and profits, giving people a stronger incentive to set up or expand businesses.

New Zealand’s high GDP per head also attracts net immigration. Some immigrants are likely to be entrepreneurs who set up new firms, and a larger population means more customers. If the government also followed the economists’ advice and cut income tax rates, households would have more disposable income, raising demand and profits further.

Why enterprise might not have increased. The source says it was difficult to predict firms’ future profits because of uncertainty about GDP and corporation tax rates. Entrepreneurs bear risk, so if they fear a recession could cut demand, the expected reward for taking that risk falls and fewer people start businesses.

The extra government spending also has to be paid for. If the government raised corporation tax, firms would keep less of their profit, reducing the incentive to be an entrepreneur. Measures to reduce pollution may also raise firms’ costs of production, for example by forcing them to use cleaner but more expensive methods, which cuts profits further.

Conclusion. Enterprise is likely to have increased in New Zealand after 2019, because an educated, productive labour force and rising demand give entrepreneurs good opportunities. This is most likely if corporation tax was not raised to fund the extra spending and confidence about future GDP stayed high; if taxes on profits rose during a period of uncertainty, enterprise may have stayed the same or fallen.

Why this scores
Up to 4 marks are available for each side, so the answer gives developed reasons both ways, each linked to the reward for enterprise (profit) or to risk, and uses the source material: education, government spending, immigration, uncertainty and corporation tax. The examiner report picked out exactly these points in strong answers, and the conclusion names the conditions the outcome depends on.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

1.3 Opportunity cost

These questions test whether you can identify the next best alternative given up in a real choice and explain how it shapes decisions; examiners reward a specific, relevant alternative rather than a general mention of ‘cost’.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports note that some candidates describe opportunity cost as the option that is chosen rather than the next best alternative that is given up, and others pick an alternative that does not fit the context, such as saying the opportunity cost of exporting is importing (it is selling the products in the home market).
0455/22/F/M/24 Q1(b) · February/March 2024 · [2 marks]

Source material: They spend this time in a variety of ways including cycling, jogging and playing golf.

Identify two possible opportunity costs of a Danish person using their leisure time to cycle.

1. Jogging.

2. Playing golf.

Why this scores
One mark each for the two other leisure activities named in the source, because these are the alternative uses of the same leisure time that are given up. “Identify” needs no explanation; the key is to pick alternatives from the source rather than a money cost.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/23 Q5(a) · May/June 2023 · [2 marks]

Source material: In 2019, China’s economic growth rate was 6.1% and Chinese households increased their spending. … China exported more despite a rise in tariffs on some of its products.

Identify the opportunity cost of households spending their income and the opportunity cost of Chinese firms exporting goods and services.

Households spending their income: the saving they could have done instead.

Chinese firms exporting: selling those goods and services to consumers in China’s home market instead.

Why this scores
One mark for saving and one for selling (consuming) the products at home. The examiner report says many candidates wrongly gave ‘importing’ as the opportunity cost of exporting; ask what else the firm could have done with the same products.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/25 Q2(b) · February/March 2025 · [4 marks]

Source material: The country is building the world’s first floating city, estimated to cost $180m.

Explain how opportunity cost will influence a government’s decision on whether to spend tax revenue on a large infrastructure project.

Opportunity cost is the next best alternative given up when a choice is made. A government’s tax revenue is limited, so if it spends $180m on a large infrastructure project such as South Korea’s floating city, that money cannot be used for something else, for example building new hospitals or schools.

The government therefore has to compare the benefits of the project with the benefits of the best alternative it would give up. It will go ahead only if it judges that the project gives the greater benefit, for example because protecting people and property from rising sea levels is a higher priority than extra healthcare. If the forgone hospitals would bring greater benefits, the opportunity cost is too high and the government may reject or delay the project.

Why this scores
Marks for defining opportunity cost (best alternative forgone), for explaining that limited tax revenue spent on the project cannot be spent elsewhere, with an example, and for showing how the government weighs its priorities to decide. The examiner report said strong answers explained that tax revenue is limited and identified which choice would be given up.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/25 Q2(c) · October/November 2025 · [6 marks]

Source material: Over this period, its decisions have always been influenced by opportunity cost.

Analyse how opportunity cost influences the decisions made by consumers, workers and producers.

Opportunity cost is the next best alternative forgone when a choice is made. Consumers, workers and producers all have limited resources, so every decision involves giving something up, and they compare what they would gain with what they would sacrifice.

Consumers. Consumers have limited incomes, so they cannot buy everything they want. If a family spends $50 on a meal out, it cannot spend that $50 on new clothes. A rational consumer chooses the option that gives more satisfaction than the alternative given up. If the price of a product rises, such as the 8% rise in the MNC’s food prices, its opportunity cost rises because buying it now means giving up more of other goods, so consumers may switch to cheaper brands.

Workers. Workers have limited time. An hour spent at leisure means the wage for that hour is given up, so if wages rise, leisure becomes more expensive and a worker may choose to work more hours. Workers also choose between jobs: accepting one job means giving up the pay and conditions of the next best job, so they compare wages and non-wage factors before deciding.

Producers. Firms have limited finance, land, labour and capital, so they must decide what to produce, how to produce it and where to locate. If a food manufacturer uses its factory to make more chocolate, the opportunity cost is the cereal or coffee it could have made instead. The firm therefore puts its resources into the products that earn the highest profit, and switches them when another product becomes more profitable.

Why this scores
Opens with a definition, then analyses all three groups, each with its limited resource (income, time, finance), a specific choice and the alternative forgone. The mark scheme caps answers at 4 marks if they cover fewer than two of consumers, workers and producers, so covering all three, each with a chain to the decision made, secures full marks.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/M/J/24 Q1(h) · May/June 2024 · [6 marks]

Source material: Nicaragua is the world’s twelfth largest producer of coffee. Global coffee consumption continues to increase.

Discuss whether or not Nicaragua should devote more of its resources to coffee production.

Devoting more resources to coffee means using more of Nicaragua’s land, labour, capital and enterprise to grow and process coffee. Every extra resource used this way has an opportunity cost: the other goods those resources could have produced.

Why Nicaragua should. Global coffee consumption continues to increase, so demand for Nicaragua’s coffee is likely to rise. Demand for coffee is price-inelastic (PED of 0.3), so as rising world demand pushes up the price, farmers’ revenue rises. More coffee exports would also improve the current account of the balance of payments, and as agricultural products already make up 60% of exports, Nicaragua has the land, skills and experience to produce coffee efficiently.

Expanding coffee production would also create jobs in rural areas, where wages are particularly low and unemployment rose in 2020. More employment would raise incomes, reduce poverty and increase tax revenue. Larger coffee firms could also gain economies of scale, lowering average costs and making Nicaragua more competitive against other producers.

Why Nicaragua should not. Coffee output is often affected by droughts and earthquakes. Putting even more resources into one crop makes the economy more vulnerable, because a single natural disaster could wipe out much of its output and export earnings. If world coffee prices fall, for example because other countries expand production, Nicaragua’s income would also fall sharply.

There is also a significant opportunity cost. The land and workers used for extra coffee could instead produce food for the domestic market or develop manufacturing and tourism, which may pay higher wages than low-paid coffee farming. Table 1.1 suggests that relying on agriculture keeps GDP per head low: only 3% of the Bahamas’ labour force works in agriculture and its GDP per head is $25 194, compared with 31% and $1905 in Nicaragua.

Conclusion. Nicaragua should devote somewhat more resources to coffee, because world demand is growing and it already produces coffee well. The case is strongest if the extra resources come from unemployed workers and are used for drought-resistant farming and high-quality coffee; it is weakest if they are taken from manufacturing or tourism, which would do more to raise GDP per head in the long run.

Why this scores
Up to 4 marks are available for each side, so the answer develops reasons both ways and uses the source data (rising world demand, PED of 0.3, natural disasters, low rural wages, Table 1.1). The opportunity-cost argument is credited directly by the mark scheme, and the conclusion makes a judgement that depends on where the extra resources come from.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

1.4 Production possibility curve (PPC) diagrams

These questions test what a PPC shows and the difference between a point on it, a point inside it and a shift of the whole curve; examiners reward fully labelled diagrams and linking every shift to a change in the quantity or quality of resources.

What examiners keep seeing
The 2022 examiner report says weaker answers treated a fall in actual output, such as a poor grape harvest, as a shift of the PPC. A PPC shows productive capacity, not actual output: only a change in the quantity or quality of resources shifts it, while unemployed resources put the economy inside the curve. Diagram marks are also lost when the curves do not touch both axes.
0455/22/M/J/22 Q3(a) · May/June 2022 · [2 marks]

Define a production possibility curve.

A production possibility curve is a diagram showing the maximum combinations of two types of products that an economy can produce with its given resources and technology.

Why this scores
One mark for ‘maximum output’ or ‘possible combinations’ and one for ‘two products’ or ‘given resources’. The examiner report notes that some candidates just drew a diagram: a diagram is not a definition and earns nothing here.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/25 Q1(c) · October/November 2025 · [2 marks]

Source material: In January 2022, Malawi was hit by Storm Ana. This damaged agricultural crops, factories, forests and power stations.

Explain what is likely to have happened to Malawi’s production possibility curve (PPC) in January 2022.

Malawi’s PPC is likely to have shifted to the left (inwards). Storm Ana damaged or destroyed resources such as factories, power stations and forests, reducing the quantity of capital and land, so Malawi’s productive capacity fell.

Why this scores
One mark for the inward shift and one for a reason from the source material (resources destroyed, so productive capacity fell). Naming the damaged resources, rather than just saying ‘output fell’, shows why the whole curve moves.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/25 Q2(b) · October/November 2025 · [4 marks]

Source material: The Belgian Government wants the economy to move to a production possibility point beyond its current production possibility curve (PPC).

Explain two ways an economy could reach a point outside its current PPC.

Increase investment in capital goods. If firms and the government devote more resources to building new machinery, factories and infrastructure, the quantity of capital increases. With more capital the economy can produce more of both types of goods, so the PPC shifts outwards and a point that was outside the old curve becomes attainable.

Improve the quality of labour through education and training. Spending on education and training makes workers more skilled, like the highly specialised workers in Belgium’s scientific industry. Their productivity rises, so the same number of workers can produce more output. Productive capacity increases and the PPC shifts outwards.

Why this scores
One mark for each way identified and one for each explanation of how it raises the quantity or quality of resources and so shifts the PPC outwards. Other creditworthy ways include immigration, land reclamation, new technology and discovering new natural resources.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/23 Q2(c) · October/November 2023 · [6 marks]

Source material: In 2019, Mozambique was hit by a serious storm which destroyed bridges, factories, roads and electricity lines.

Analyse, using a production possibility curve (PPC) diagram, the effect of a serious storm on an economy.

A production possibility curve (PPC) shows the maximum combinations of two types of goods, here capital goods and consumer goods, that an economy can produce with its existing resources and technology.

A serious storm, such as the one that hit Mozambique in 2019, destroys factors of production. Bridges, factories, roads and electricity lines are all capital, so the quantity of capital falls. Farmland may be flooded and workers may be injured or forced to leave the area, which reduces the quantity of land and labour as well.

With fewer resources, the economy’s productive capacity falls: it can no longer produce as much of either type of good as before. As shown in the diagram, the PPC shifts inwards from PPC to PPC1. A combination on the old curve, such as point A, is no longer attainable, and the most the economy can now produce is a combination on PPC1, such as B.

In the short run, damaged roads and power lines may also stop firms that still have their machinery from getting raw materials or electricity, so some resources become unemployed and the economy may produce inside even its new PPC. The economy can only move back out as the damaged capital is rebuilt.

Why this scores
Up to 4 marks are for the diagram: axes labelled with two types of output, both curves touching both axes, and the inward shift shown with an arrow and labels. The other 2 marks are for explaining that the storm destroys resources and so reduces productive capacity. A new curve that does not reach both axes loses the diagram marks.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/M/J/22 Q1(g) · May/June 2022 · [6 marks]

Source material: The interest rate in Cyprus was very low in 2019. … In 2019, two concerns for the future of Cyprus’s economy were that the quality of some of its agricultural land may decline and that some of its top entrepreneurs may emigrate.

Discuss whether or not, after 2019, Cyprus’s production possibility curve (PPC) is likely to shift to the right.

A PPC shows the maximum output of two types of goods that an economy can produce with its resources. It shifts to the right (outwards) when the quantity or quality of those resources increases, raising productive capacity.

Why it might shift to the right. The interest rate in Cyprus was very low in 2019. Cheaper borrowing encourages firms to invest in capital goods, and some firms have already become more capital-intensive. More and better machinery increases the quantity and quality of capital, so productive capacity rises and the PPC shifts outwards.

The labour force may also grow. The population rose from 1.1 million in 2015 to 1.2 million in 2019, life expectancy is rising and more workers are working past retirement age. If the retirement age is raised, people will stay in the labour force for longer, increasing the quantity of labour and shifting the PPC to the right.

Why it might not shift to the right. The quality of some agricultural land may decline, which would reduce the productivity of land, and some top entrepreneurs may emigrate. Enterprise organises the other factors and takes risks, so losing entrepreneurs means fewer new firms and less efficient use of resources. Both would reduce productive capacity and could shift the PPC to the left.

In the longer term, the birth rate fell from 11.0 to 10.2 per 1000, so fewer young people will enter the labour force in future. Unless immigration increases, the quantity of labour may eventually fall, which would also stop the PPC shifting outwards.

Conclusion. Cyprus’s PPC is likely to shift to the right in the next few years, because low interest rates and longer working lives should increase the quantity of capital and labour. This depends on how many entrepreneurs actually emigrate and how quickly land quality declines: if those losses are large, they could cancel out the gains and leave the PPC unchanged or shift it to the left.

Why this scores
Each side links evidence from the source to a change in the quantity or quality of a factor of production, and so to productive capacity, which is what the mark scheme rewards (up to 4 marks per side). The examiner report says weaker answers wrongly used the bad grape harvest, a fall in actual output, as a reason for a shift; this answer avoids that and ends with a conditional judgement.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

Past Paper Questions: The Allocation of Resources

48 real Cambridge IGCSE Economics Paper 2 questions from 2020 to 2025, sorted by sub-topic, each with its exact paper reference and a full-mark exemplar answer. The questions are quoted exactly; the model answers are my own, written to the published mark schemes and the advice in the examiner reports.

How to use this page
Cover the answer, write your own in timed conditions (about 1½ minutes per mark), then compare. 0455/22/M/J/25 Q2(d) means Paper 2, variant 2, May/June 2025, Question 2 part (d). F/M = February/March, M/J = May/June, O/N = October/November.

Jump to: 2.1 Microeconomics and macroeconomics · 2.2 The role of markets in allocating resources · 2.3 Demand · 2.4 Supply · 2.5 Price determination · 2.6 Price changes · 2.8 Price elasticity of supply (PES) · 2.9 Market economic system · 2.10 Market failure · 2.11 Mixed economic system
