2.1 Microeconomics and macroeconomics

These short questions test whether you can say precisely what microeconomics and macroeconomics study and classify examples of each; examiners reward the key phrases ‘individual markets and decision makers’ and ‘the economy as a whole’.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner report for October/November 2020 says weak answers described microeconomics as ‘a small part of the economy’ or only ‘how households behave’. Define it as the study of individual markets and decision makers (households, workers and firms), and macroeconomics as the study of the whole economy.
0455/21/M/J/20 Q4(a) · May/June 2020 · [2 marks]

Define macroeconomics.

Macroeconomics is the study of the economy as a whole, for example a country’s total output (GDP), unemployment, inflation and balance of payments.

Why this scores
‘The study of the whole economy’ earns both marks; adding an example of a macroeconomic topic, such as unemployment, makes the answer secure.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/20 Q2(a) · October/November 2020 · [2 marks]

Define microeconomics.

Microeconomics is the study of individual markets and the decisions of individual economic agents, such as households, workers and firms, for example how the price of cigarettes is determined in the market for cigarettes.

Why this scores
Both marks come from ‘individual markets’ or ‘individual economic agents’. The examiner report says ‘how households behave’ on its own was too narrow and ‘a small part of the economy’ was wrong; an example of one market, as here, makes the meaning clear.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/24 Q3(a) · May/June 2024 · [2 marks]

Identify the difference between microeconomics and macroeconomics.

Microeconomics studies individual decision makers and individual markets, such as households, firms or the market for housing, whereas macroeconomics studies the economy as a whole, such as total output, inflation and unemployment.

Why this scores
One mark for each side of the difference: individual markets or decision makers for microeconomics (1) and the whole economy for macroeconomics (1). Writing both halves in one comparative sentence makes the difference clear.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/25 Q1(b) · October/November 2025 · [2 marks]

Source material: Price levels are more stable because of a combination of microeconomic and macroeconomic policy measures. These include maximum prices, subsidies and a contractionary monetary policy.

Identify two microeconomic policy measures.

1. Maximum prices.

2. Subsidies (such as those given to solar energy producers).

Why this scores
One mark each. Both measures act on individual markets, so they are microeconomic; contractionary monetary policy affects the whole economy, so it is a macroeconomic measure and would not score. The mark scheme also accepts organising a merger, which the source mentions.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

2.2 The role of markets in allocating resources

These questions test the three key resource allocation questions (what, how and for whom to produce) and how the price mechanism answers them; examiners reward answers that explain price signals and the profit incentive, not just describe demand and supply.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports say this is often poorly answered: in 2021 many candidates wrote about the price mechanism in general without addressing what, how and for whom to produce, and in 2025 many read ‘a market’ as the whole economy and wrote about factors of production or sectors instead of buyers, sellers and prices.
0455/22/M/J/20 Q5(a) · May/June 2020 · [2 marks]

State two key questions about how resources are allocated.

1. What to produce?

2. How to produce it?

Why this scores
One mark each for any two of the three questions: what to produce, how to produce it and for whom to produce. State them as the syllabus phrases them; no explanation is needed.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/24 Q2(b) · May/June 2024 · [4 marks]

Source material: This transformation was possible as local villages shared resources to develop their area.

Explain two key resource allocation decisions.

What to produce. Because resources are scarce, an economy has to decide which goods and services to make and in what quantities, for example whether to use land in Shenzhen for factories or for housing for migrant workers, or whether to produce more capital goods or more consumer goods. Producing more of one product means producing less of another.

How to produce. Producers must decide which combination of factors of production to use, for example a capital-intensive method using machinery and robots or a labour-intensive method using many workers. The aim is to choose the most efficient method, which keeps the cost of production as low as possible and wastes the fewest resources.

Why this scores
One mark for each decision identified and one for each explanation of what it involves, with an example. The third decision, for whom to produce (those who can pay or those who need the product), would also be credited.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/25 Q5(b) · May/June 2025 · [4 marks]

Explain how a market allocates resources.

A market is where buyers (demand) and sellers (supply) come together to trade a product. The interaction of demand and supply sets the equilibrium price, and that price acts as a signal and an incentive to producers about how to use their resources.

If consumers’ demand for a product increases, its price rises. Producing it becomes more profitable, so firms are encouraged to move more land, labour and capital into making it. If demand falls, its price and profits fall, so firms move resources out of that product and into others. In this way the price mechanism shifts resources towards the products consumers want most, without the government having to decide.

Why this scores
Marks for explaining that a market is buyers and sellers whose demand and supply create an equilibrium price, that price signals and the profit incentive guide sellers, and that more resources go to products in higher demand and fewer to products in lower demand. The examiner report says many candidates misread ‘a market’ as the economy and wrote about sectors or factors of production, and very few mentioned price at all.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/21 Q2(c) · October/November 2021 · [6 marks]

Source material: In Russia, the role of the price mechanism to answer key resource allocation questions is increasing.

Analyse how the price mechanism answers the three key resource allocation questions.

The price mechanism is the way the forces of demand and supply determine prices in a market economy, and those prices then decide how resources are used. It answers the three key questions without government planning.

What to produce. Consumers signal what they want through their spending. If demand for a product rises, its price rises. A higher price means higher profits, so firms have an incentive to produce more and move resources into that industry. If demand for a product falls, its price and profits fall, so firms produce less and resources move elsewhere. Output therefore follows consumer demand.

How to produce. Firms aim to maximise profit, so they choose the cheapest combination of factors of production, and the prices of those factors guide the choice. If wages rise relative to the cost of machinery, firms switch to more capital-intensive methods; if labour is cheap, they use more labour-intensive methods. Resources are therefore used efficiently.

For whom to produce. Goods go to those who are willing and able to pay the market price. People’s ability to pay depends on their income, which is also set by the price mechanism: workers whose skills are in high demand and short supply earn high wages, so they can buy the most goods and services. When a product is scarce, its higher price rations it to those willing and able to pay most.

Why this scores
Defines the price mechanism and then takes each of the three questions in turn, explaining how prices act as a signal and an incentive to answer it, which matches the mark scheme. The examiner report says this question was poorly answered because many candidates described the price mechanism in general, or drew a demand and supply diagram, without addressing what, how and for whom.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

2.3 Demand

These questions test whether you can tell a movement along the demand curve (caused only by the product’s own price) from a shift of the whole curve (caused by any other condition of demand), and examiners reward answers that name the cause, explain why consumers’ willingness or ability to buy changes and state which way demand moves.

What examiners keep seeing
Students mix up an extension in demand with an increase in demand: the examiner report notes some even thought an extension means a rise in demand that lasts a long time. An extension is a movement down the curve caused by a fall in the product’s own price; an increase is a shift of the whole curve to the right caused by something else, such as higher incomes.
0455/22/O/N/24 Q4(a) · October/November 2024 · [2 marks]

Source material: In 2021, there were extensions and contractions in demand for a number of South African products.

Define an extension in demand.

An extension in demand is a movement down along the demand curve, showing a rise in the quantity demanded, which is caused by a fall in the price of the product itself. The demand curve does not shift.

Why this scores
The mark scheme rewards a movement along the demand curve, a higher quantity demanded and the cause, a fall in price. Writing “an increase in demand” scores nothing, because that means a shift of the whole curve.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/22 Q1(b) · May/June 2022 · [2 marks]

Source material: Demand has increased because of increases in incomes and decreases in the price of the technology needed to play or watch eSports.

Identify two conditions of demand for eSports.

1. Rising incomes.

2. A fall in the price of the technology needed to play or watch eSports.

Why this scores
One mark for each condition, both taken straight from the source material. “Identify” needs no explanation. The examiner report says most candidates found these two in the text, so read the source rather than listing textbook factors such as advertising.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/24 Q4(b) · October/November 2024 · [4 marks]

Source material: However, the markets for coffee substitutes and complements do not have such levels of government intervention.

Explain how the price of coffee substitutes and complements can affect the demand for coffee.

Substitutes. A substitute, such as tea, can be used instead of coffee. If the price of tea rises, coffee becomes relatively cheaper, so some consumers switch from tea to coffee. Demand for coffee increases and its demand curve shifts to the right; a fall in the price of tea would do the opposite. The price of a substitute and the demand for coffee therefore move in the same direction.

Complements. A complement, such as sugar or milk, is used together with coffee (joint demand). If the price of sugar rises, consumers buy less sugar and, because the two are used together, they also buy less coffee, so demand for coffee falls and its curve shifts to the left. The price of a complement and the demand for coffee therefore move in opposite directions.

Why this scores
The mark scheme rewards the direction of each relationship (positive for substitutes, negative for complements), the reason it happens and an example. An answer that covers only substitutes or only complements is capped at 3 marks, so always deal with both.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/24 Q5(c) · May/June 2024 · [6 marks]

Source material: In 2020, demand for both economic goods and factors of production increased in Poland. For example, Polish consumers demanded more football shirts …

Analyse the effect of an increase in demand for football shirts on demand for football shorts and demand for basketball shirts.

Demand is the willingness and ability of consumers to buy a product at a given price. When demand for one product changes, demand for related products changes too, depending on whether they are complements or substitutes.

Football shorts: complements. Football shirts and football shorts are in joint demand, because players and clubs buy them together to make up a full kit. If more Polish consumers buy football shirts, many of them will also buy shorts to go with them. Demand for football shorts therefore increases and the demand curve for shorts shifts to the right. At the original price there is now a shortage of shorts, so their price is likely to rise and more shorts are bought and sold.

Basketball shirts: substitutes. Football shirts and basketball shirts can be substitutes: both are sports shirts that people buy to wear casually or to support a sport. If consumers’ tastes switch towards football shirts, some of the money they spend on sportswear moves away from basketball shirts. Demand for basketball shirts falls and its demand curve shifts to the left, which tends to lower their price and the quantity sold.

However, the effect on basketball shirts may be small. If most buyers are basketball players who need the shirts to play, the two are not close substitutes, so demand for basketball shirts may hardly change. And if the rise in demand for football shirts was caused by higher incomes, demand for basketball shirts could rise as well, since both are normal goods.

Why this scores
It identifies the direction of each change, the relationship (complements, substitutes) and why consumers behave that way, which is exactly what the mark scheme credits, then takes the chain on to price and quantity. Pointing out that the substitute effect depends on how close a substitute basketball shirts really are shows deeper understanding.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/23 Q3(c) · May/June 2023 · [6 marks]

Source material: Reasons for this include falling demand for Chinese food and increasing competition from other types of food.

Analyse two causes of a fall in demand for a product such as Chinese food.

Demand is the willingness and ability of consumers to buy a product at a given price. A fall in demand means less Chinese food is demanded at every price, so the demand curve shifts to the left. It is caused by a change in a condition of demand other than the price of Chinese food itself.

Cause 1: a fall in the price of substitutes. Other types of food, such as pizza, Indian food or burgers, are substitutes for Chinese food. If the price of pizza falls, for example because a new pizza chain enters the market with special offers, pizza becomes relatively cheaper. Some consumers switch from Chinese takeaways to pizza, so demand for Chinese food falls. This fits the UK case, where the source mentions increasing competition from other types of food.

Cause 2: a fall in incomes. Eating at a Chinese restaurant or ordering a takeaway is a normal good, and for many households it is a luxury rather than a necessity. If incomes fall, for example because unemployment rises, households have less disposable income and cut non-essential spending first. They cook at home instead, so demand for Chinese food falls at every price.

In both cases the leftward shift creates a surplus at the original price, so restaurants cut prices and sell fewer meals. Lower revenue may force some of them to close, which is consistent with the fall in the number of Chinese restaurants in the UK.

Why this scores
Exactly two causes, each built into a full chain: the cause, why it changes consumers’ willingness or ability to buy, and the result (demand shifts left). The examiner report says weaker answers wrote generally about food, or confused Chinese food with food imported from China; the best applied simple demand theory (price of substitutes, income, tastes) to Chinese food.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

2.4 Supply

Supply questions test whether you can explain why firms become willing and able to offer more or less at every price, and examiners reward answers that name a condition of supply, link it to costs or profitability and state which way the supply curve shifts.

What examiners keep seeing
Many candidates turn a supply question into a demand question: the examiner reports on the chocolate question (2022) and on causes of an increase in supply (2025) both say weaker answers analysed why consumers want more instead of why firms supply more. Keep the producer as the subject of every sentence.
0455/23/O/N/24 Q4(a) · October/November 2024 · [2 marks]

Source material: Hospitality firms in Davos depend on seasonal migrant labour, especially when they want to increase the supply of goods and services quickly.

Define supply.

Supply is the willingness and ability of producers to sell a good or service at a given price over a given period of time.

Why this scores
One mark for “willingness” and one for “ability” to sell. “The amount of a good produced by a firm” on its own earns only one mark, because it leaves out willingness and ability.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/22 Q2(a) · October/November 2022 · [2 marks]

Source material: This has led to an increase in the market supply of industries such as cafes and clothes shops.

Identify the difference between individual supply and market supply.

Individual supply is the quantity that one firm is willing and able to sell at each price, whereas market supply is the total of all the individual firms’ supply in the market at each price, for example all the cafes in Medan added together.

Why this scores
One mark for each side of the contrast. The examiner report says weaker answers described individual supply as coming from one person and market supply as “lots of people” supplying: talk about firms.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/25 Q4(b) · May/June 2025 · [4 marks]

Source material: Changes in the conditions of supply have also increased the number of tall buildings being constructed.

Explain two causes of an increase in the supply of a product.

Lower costs of production. If the price of raw materials falls, for example cheaper steel and cement for construction firms, each unit costs less to produce, so producing becomes more profitable. Firms are willing and able to supply more at every price, so the supply curve shifts to the right.

A government subsidy. A subsidy is a payment from the government to producers for each unit they make. It covers part of their costs and adds to the revenue they receive, so firms can afford to produce more with the same money and have a bigger incentive to do so. Supply therefore increases at every price.

Why this scores
Each cause is identified (1) and explained (1) by linking it to costs or revenue, and so to producers’ willingness and ability to supply more at every price. The 2025 examiner report says many candidates wrote about consumers wanting more, which is demand, not supply.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/M/J/25 Q3(c) · May/June 2025 · [6 marks]

Source material: Enterprise and conditions of supply can affect firms’ prices. In 2022, the Latvian Government increased the country’s national minimum wage (NMW).

Analyse how changes in conditions of supply can affect a firm’s prices.

Conditions of supply are the factors other than the product’s own price that affect how much firms are willing and able to supply, such as costs of production, technology, taxes, subsidies and the weather. A change in any of them shifts the supply curve and so changes the price a firm can charge.

Higher wage costs raise prices. In 2022 Latvia raised its national minimum wage. For a firm employing low-paid workers, this increases wage costs, so each unit costs more to produce and the firm is willing to supply less at each price. Supply shifts to the left, creating a shortage at the old price, so the price rises. The firm may also raise its price on purpose to protect its profit margin, which is easier if demand for its product is price-inelastic.

Advances in technology lower prices. If a firm invests in new machinery, its productivity rises and its average cost falls. It is willing to supply more at every price, so supply shifts to the right; at the old price there is a surplus, so the price falls. A government subsidy works in a similar way, because it covers part of the firm’s costs and lets it charge a lower price while earning the same profit.

Weather and natural disasters. For agricultural firms, good weather creates a large harvest, so supply rises and prices fall. A drought or flood destroys crops, cutting supply and creating a shortage, so prices rise.

Why this scores
Every point names a specific condition of supply, explains how it changes costs and supply, and only then gives the price effect. The mark scheme caps answers at 4 marks if they do not identify the condition, and the examiner report says weaker answers just described supply rising or falling without saying what caused it. Using Latvia’s minimum wage applies the stimulus.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/22 Q5(c) · February/March 2022 · [6 marks]

Source material: Ghana uses much of its agricultural land to grow cocoa. Cocoa is sold to chocolate producers.

Analyse the causes of a shift to the right in the supply curve of chocolate.

A shift to the right in the supply curve means producers are willing and able to supply more chocolate at each and every price. It is caused by a change in a condition of supply, not by a change in the price of chocolate.

A good cocoa harvest. Cocoa is the main raw material in chocolate, and much of it is grown in countries such as Ghana. If the weather is good, or farmers use better fertilisers, the cocoa harvest is larger, which increases the supply of cocoa and lowers its price. Chocolate producers’ costs of production fall, so each bar is more profitable to make and they supply more chocolate at every price.

Advances in technology. New machinery in chocolate factories, such as automated moulding and packing lines, raises productivity. More bars can be made from the same resources, so the average cost of each bar falls and firms can produce more at each price.

A subsidy or a lower tax. A subsidy to chocolate producers is a payment for each unit produced, which effectively lowers their costs. A cut in an indirect tax on chocolate has a similar effect. Either way producers keep more of the revenue from each bar, which increases the incentive to supply.

Lower profitability of other products. If the price of another product chocolate firms could make, such as biscuits, falls, firms may move factory space and workers into chocolate production, so the supply of chocolate rises.

Why this scores
It opens with what a rightward shift means (more supplied at each and every price), which the mark scheme credits and the examiner report picked out in strong answers, then gives several causes, each a chain from the cause to lower costs or higher profitability to more supply. There are no marks for a diagram here, and the report warns against drawing a demand shift and analysing demand instead.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

2.5 Price determination

Price determination questions test the precise language of equilibrium, shortage and surplus, and examiners reward answers that compare demand with supply and explain how the price moves to close the gap.

What examiners keep seeing
Students often describe what happened (“production fell by 35%”) without saying what it did to the market: the 2022 examiner report notes many did not go on to explain why this meant disequilibrium. Always finish with the relationship between demand and supply, such as “demand exceeded supply, so there was a shortage”.
0455/22/O/N/25 Q2(a) · October/November 2025 · [2 marks]

Source material: Some markets in Belgium are in equilibrium.

Define market equilibrium.

Market equilibrium is the situation where the quantity demanded equals the quantity supplied at the current price, so there is no shortage and no surplus and no pressure for the price to change.

Why this scores
“Demand equals supply” earns both marks on its own; adding “no shortage and no surplus” makes the answer secure. Avoid vague wording such as “the market is stable”, which does not mention demand and supply.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/24 Q4(a) · February/March 2024 · [2 marks]

Source material: In 2021, the Suez Canal was blocked by one of the world’s biggest container ships. This affected some firms’ profits and caused a shortage in a number of products.

Define a shortage.

A shortage (excess demand) is when the quantity demanded of a product is greater than the quantity supplied at the current price.

Why this scores
Both marks are for the idea that demand exceeds supply. Vague answers such as “not enough of the product” or “demand is high” get only one mark, because they do not compare demand with supply.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/M/J/22 Q1(c) · May/June 2022 · [2 marks]

Source material: In 2019, the global grape harvest was affected by bad weather. Global grape production fell by 35% which caused a shortage.

State why the global market for grapes was in disequilibrium in 2019.

Bad weather caused the global grape harvest to fail, so supply fell sharply. At the existing price, demand for grapes was greater than supply, so there was a shortage.

Why this scores
One mark for the cause (bad weather and the failed harvest) and one for why that is disequilibrium: a shortage, with demand exceeding supply. The examiner report says many candidates just repeated that production fell by 35% without explaining why this put the market in disequilibrium.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/23 Q5(b) · October/November 2023 · [4 marks]

Source material: 2020 was a year of great change in a number of Singaporean markets. Some moved from disequilibrium to equilibrium.

Explain how a market moves from disequilibrium to equilibrium.

Disequilibrium is when demand and supply are not equal at the current price. If there is a shortage (demand greater than supply), consumers compete for the limited products and firms realise they can charge more, so the price rises. The higher price causes a contraction in demand and an extension in supply until demand again equals supply.

If there is a surplus (supply greater than demand), firms are left with unsold stock, so they cut the price to sell it. The lower price causes an extension in demand and a contraction in supply until the two are equal again, and the market is back in equilibrium.

Why this scores
The mark scheme gives marks for identifying each type of disequilibrium, for the price change that follows and for demand again equalling supply. Using “extension” and “contraction” correctly shows that the curves do not shift: the price change alone restores equilibrium.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

2.6 Price changes

These questions test whether you can trace a change through a market (what shifts, which way, and what happens to equilibrium price and quantity), and examiners reward a fully labelled diagram backed by a written explanation of why the curve shifts.

What examiners keep seeing
Marks are often lost on the diagram itself: the 2022 examiner report says candidates who understood the economics still missed marks by not labelling the axes, curves or equilibrium points. It also stresses that describing the diagram (“the demand curve moves to the right”) is not the same as analysing it (“demand for solar energy increases, so its price rises”).
0455/23/O/N/25 Q1(e) · October/November 2025 · [4 marks]

Source material: The global market for sugar has been affected by health reports which identified the possible harmful effects of consuming too much sugar.

Draw a demand and supply diagram to show the effect of a report stating the health risks of consuming sugar on the market for sugar.

The health report makes consumers less willing to buy sugar at every price, so demand decreases and the demand curve shifts to the left from D1 to D2. The equilibrium price falls from P1 to P2 and the quantity traded falls from Q1 to Q2.

Why this scores
All four marks are for the diagram: axes labelled Price and Quantity, the original curves labelled, a new demand curve shifted to the left, and both equilibria shown with P1, Q1 and P2, Q2. Supply does not shift, because the report changes consumers’ tastes, not producers’ costs.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/20 Q3(b) · October/November 2020 · [4 marks]

Source material: For example, when the price of natural rubber fell, most rubber plantations changed to palm oil production.

Explain two possible reasons for a fall in the price of a product such as natural rubber.

A decrease in demand. If a cheaper substitute becomes available, such as synthetic rubber made from oil, tyre and glove manufacturers switch away from natural rubber. Demand for natural rubber falls and its demand curve shifts to the left, so at the original price there is a surplus and the price falls.

An increase in supply. If there is a good harvest, for example because of favourable weather, or new plantations start producing, more natural rubber is offered for sale at every price. The supply curve shifts to the right, creating a surplus at the old price, so the price falls.

Why this scores
Each reason is identified as a change in demand or supply (1) and explained with a specific cause, such as a substitute or a good yield (1). Taking one reason from each side of the market keeps the two points clearly separate.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/24 Q1(f) · May/June 2024 · [5 marks]

Source material: Tunisian shoe producers have recently experienced a rise in wage costs and a decline in the number of workers they employ.

Analyse, using a demand and supply diagram, how an increase in wage costs would affect the market for shoes.

Wages are a cost of production for shoe producers. An increase in wage costs, like the one experienced by Tunisian shoe producers, raises the cost of making each pair of shoes and reduces profitability.

Producers are therefore less willing and able to supply shoes at each price, so supply decreases and the supply curve shifts to the left from S1 to S2. At the original price P1 there is now a shortage, so the price rises. As it rises there is a contraction in demand, until a new equilibrium is reached at the higher price P2 and the lower quantity Q2.

Consumers therefore pay more for shoes and fewer shoes are bought and sold, which fits the fall in the number of workers that shoe firms employ.

Why this scores
Four marks are for the diagram (labelled axes, original curves, supply shifted left, both equilibria) and one for the written link from higher wage costs to higher costs of production to a higher price. The mark scheme says the higher price must be linked to the higher costs, so a diagram alone, or “the price rises” with no reason, cannot reach full marks.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/22 Q4(c) · October/November 2022 · [6 marks]

Source material: Consumers are also starting to change their spending patterns by moving towards environmentally friendly products such as solar energy.

Analyse, using a demand and supply diagram, the effects on the solar energy industry of the change in spending patterns towards environmentally friendly products.

Consumers are changing their spending patterns towards environmentally friendly products. Solar energy is clean and renewable, so tastes and preferences move towards it, and some consumers switch away from polluting substitutes such as electricity from coal-fired power stations.

As a result, consumers are more willing to buy solar energy, for example solar panels for their homes, at every price. Demand for solar energy increases and the demand curve shifts to the right from D1 to D2. At the original price P1 there is now a shortage, so the price rises. The higher price encourages an extension in supply as firms produce more, until a new equilibrium is reached at P2 and Q2.

The solar energy industry therefore sells a larger quantity at a higher price, so its firms earn more revenue and probably more profit. This may attract new firms into the industry and encourage existing ones to expand and employ more workers, while demand for polluting energy falls.

Why this scores
Up to 4 marks are for the diagram (labelled axes, original curves, demand shifted right, both equilibria) and 2 for the written analysis: why demand for solar energy rises, and that its price and quantity both increase. The examiner report praised answers that said “demand for solar energy increases” rather than just describing the curve moving.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/25 Q2(c) · October/November 2025 · [6 marks]

Source material: In the first half of 2022, Guyana’s GDP grew by 36.4%. … However, in the short run, there was a rise in house prices.

Analyse why house prices may increase.

The price of houses, like any product, is set by demand and supply. House prices rise if demand increases or supply decreases, because either change creates a shortage at the original price, and buyers then compete for the limited number of houses.

Higher incomes increase demand. Guyana’s GDP grew by 36.4% in the first half of 2022, so many households’ incomes rose. With more income, more people are able to afford to buy a house, or a bigger one. Demand for houses increases and the demand curve shifts to the right, so house prices rise.

Lower interest rates increase demand. Most people buy a house with a mortgage. If the central bank cuts interest rates, monthly repayments fall, so more people are willing and able to borrow to buy a house. This also increases demand and pushes prices up. A growing population, for example workers moving into the country for new jobs, has the same effect.

Supply cannot keep up. In the short run the supply of houses is limited, because building takes time and land is scarce. If building costs rise, for example higher wages for construction workers or more expensive cement, builders supply fewer houses at each price and pass the higher costs on to buyers. Stricter planning rules have a similar effect. With demand rising and supply fixed or falling, the shortage grows and house prices rise further.

Why this scores
Every reason is analysed in terms of demand or supply, which the mark scheme requires: identifying reasons without this is capped at 4 marks. Each chain runs from the cause, to buyers’ or builders’ behaviour, to the shift, to the shortage and the higher price, and the Guyana growth figure is used as context.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

2.8 Price elasticity of supply (PES)

These questions test how easily producers can change the quantity they supply when the price changes; examiners reward answers that say clearly whether each influence makes supply elastic or inelastic, and why.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports say the commonest mistake is writing about price elasticity of demand (or about causes of a shift in supply) instead of PES. Another is naming a correct influence, such as storage or the time needed to build a new mine, without stating that it makes supply elastic or inelastic.
0455/23/O/N/22 Q1(a) · October/November 2022 · [1 mark]

Source material: Change in price in –50% … Change in supply of –8%

Calculate the price elasticity of supply of Ecuador’s oil.

PES = % change in quantity supplied ÷ % change in price = −8% ÷ −50% = 0.16

(PES is less than 1, so the supply of Ecuador’s oil is price-inelastic: a 50% fall in price led to only an 8% fall in quantity supplied.)

Why this scores
The single mark is for 0.16. The examiner report says many candidates wrote “16%”, which the mark scheme rejects because PES has no units, and others turned the formula upside down (giving 6.25). Write the formula first to avoid both slips.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/23 Q4(a) · October/November 2023 · [2 marks]

Source material: In the US, the supply of meat from emus, a large bird, is price-elastic.

Define elastic supply.

Supply is price-elastic when a change in price causes a greater percentage change in quantity supplied, so PES is greater than 1. For example, if the price of emu meat rises by 10%, farmers raise the quantity supplied by more than 10%.

Why this scores
Both marks come from comparing the percentage change in quantity supplied with the percentage change in price. Writing only “PES is greater than 1” or “supply changes by more than price” earns just 1 mark.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/24 Q1(d) · February/March 2024 · [4 marks]

Source material: …they take a long time to produce and they are expensive to store.

Explain two reasons why the supply of ships is price-inelastic.

Long production time. Ships take months or even years to build. If the price of ships rises, shipbuilders cannot increase the quantity supplied quickly, because new ships cannot be finished in time to take advantage of the higher price. Quantity supplied therefore changes by a smaller percentage than the price, so supply is price-inelastic.

Expensive to store. Ships are huge and costly to keep, so shipbuilders hold few or no finished ships in stock. When the price rises there are no spare ships that can be taken out of storage and sold straight away, so quantity supplied cannot respond much to the price change.

Why this scores
One mark for each reason identified and one for each explanation, which must link the reason to supply being unable to change quickly. The mark scheme ignores comments on PED, so the stimulus point about postponing the purchase of ships (a demand factor) is deliberately not used.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/M/J/23 Q1(d) · May/June 2023 · [4 marks]

Source material: Coal can be stored to meet changes in market conditions but storage costs can be high. It can also take years to construct a new coal mine.

Explain two influences on the price elasticity of supply of coal.

Time needed to increase capacity. It can take years to build a new coal mine in Australia. If the price of coal rises, firms cannot quickly open new mines, so in the short run the quantity supplied rises by a smaller percentage than the price. This makes the supply of coal price-inelastic, although it becomes more elastic in the long run once new mines open.

Stocks and storage costs. Coal does not go off, so it can be stored, and firms holding stocks can release them when the price rises, which makes supply more elastic. However, storage costs are high, so firms keep only limited stocks. This limits how far quantity supplied can respond to a price rise, so supply tends to be inelastic.

Why this scores
Each influence earns 1 mark and its effect on PES earns 1 mark. The examiner report says many candidates named storage and new mines but never said these made supply inelastic, and others wrote about demand, so each paragraph ends by stating the effect on the PES of coal.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/25 Q2(c) · February/March 2025 · [6 marks]

Source material: Its output includes products with both elastic and inelastic supply.

Analyse the influences on whether supply of a product is elastic or inelastic.

Price elasticity of supply (PES) measures how responsive quantity supplied is to a change in price. Supply is elastic when quantity supplied changes by a greater percentage than price (PES greater than 1) and inelastic when it changes by a smaller percentage (PES less than 1).

Production time. If a product can be made quickly, such as printed T-shirts, firms can raise output soon after a price rise to take advantage of the higher price, so supply is elastic. If production takes a long time, such as crops that need a growing season, output cannot respond for months, so supply is inelastic.

Ability to store the product. Non-perishable goods can be kept in stock. When the price rises, firms take goods out of storage and sell them, so quantity supplied rises quickly and supply is elastic. Perishable goods such as fresh fish cannot be stored, so supply is inelastic.

Spare capacity and mobility of factors. If a firm has unused machinery and unemployed workers are available, it can increase output easily when the price rises, making supply elastic. If it is already working at full capacity, or the factors of production it needs are immobile and cannot be moved from other uses, output can only rise slowly and at high cost, so supply is inelastic.

Time period. In the short run, firms cannot change all their factors of production, so supply is often inelastic. Over a longer period they can build new factories and train workers, so supply becomes more elastic.

Why this scores
It defines PES first, then for each influence explains both the elastic and the inelastic case and why output can or cannot change quickly. The examiner report warns that many answers mixed up which way an influence works, analysed causes of a shift in supply, or wrote about PED instead.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

2.9 Market economic system

These questions test how a market economy uses the price mechanism and private firms to allocate resources, and what its strengths and weaknesses are; examiners reward answers that explain how prices, profit and competition lead to each outcome rather than listing advantages.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports say candidates often confuse a market economy with a mixed or planned economy, or write about the advantages of a mixed economy when asked about a market one. In a market system the decisions are made by consumers and private firms, so keep government out of the “benefits” side.
0455/23/O/N/23 Q3(a) · October/November 2023 · [2 marks]

Source material: Saudi Arabia’s economic system may change in the future.

Identify two types of economic system.

1. Market economic system.

2. Mixed economic system.

Why this scores
One mark for each. A planned (command) economy is also accepted, but the mark scheme gives no marks for “free market system” or “controlled market system”, so use the syllabus names.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/21 Q5(b) · October/November 2021 · [4 marks]

Source material: The US operates a mainly market economic system.

Explain two advantages of a market economic system.

Consumer sovereignty. In a market economic system, firms aim to make a profit, so they produce the goods and services that consumers are willing to pay for. If consumers want more of a product, its price rises and making it becomes more profitable, so firms move resources into producing it. Consumers therefore decide what is produced.

Lower prices and greater efficiency. Private firms compete with each other for customers. To win sales they must keep their costs down, for example by using new technology, and pass this on as lower prices. Firms that are inefficient make losses and leave the market, so resources are used more efficiently.

Why this scores
Each advantage is identified (1) and explained (1) with a reason linked to the price mechanism, competition or the profit motive. The examiner report says some candidates lost marks by writing about the advantages of a mixed economy, and the mark scheme gives nothing for “no taxes”.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/20 Q1(h) · May/June 2020 · [6 marks]

Source material: The Greek government introduced market-friendly measures, including privatisation and labour market reforms. These measures brought back some investors and moved the Greek economy closer to a market economic system.

Discuss whether or not a market economic system improves living standards.

A market economic system is one where resources are allocated by the price mechanism, with consumers and private firms deciding what, how and for whom to produce.

Why it might improve living standards. Firms compete for consumers’ spending and are motivated by profit, so they have an incentive to cut costs, innovate and produce what people want. Consumers get a wide choice of goods at lower prices, so their incomes buy more and their material living standards rise. In Greece, market-friendly measures such as privatisation brought back some investors, and more investment can increase output, create jobs and raise incomes.

Why it might not. A market system will not provide public goods such as street lighting, because firms cannot charge for them, and merit goods such as healthcare and education will be under-consumed by people on low incomes. Income and wealth are likely to be very unequally distributed, so the poor may be left behind. Markets can also be unstable: in a recession many people lose their jobs and incomes, and Greece’s unemployment rate was 24% in 2015.

Conclusion. A market system is likely to raise average material living standards through efficiency and choice, but not the living standards of everyone. It is most likely to improve living standards if competition is strong and the government still provides public goods, merit goods and a safety net for the unemployed, which in practice means a mixed economy.

Why this scores
Up to 4 marks are available for each side, so both are needed for 6. Each point is developed into an effect on living standards (prices, choice, jobs, income), the Greek source is used, and the conclusion makes a judgement with conditions.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/23 Q2(d) · May/June 2023 · [8 marks]

Source material: A growing private sector can move an economy towards a market economic system.

Discuss whether or not a market economy allocates resources in the best possible way.

A market economy is one in which resources are allocated by the price mechanism: private firms decide what to produce in response to consumer demand, with little government intervention.

Why it might allocate resources in the best possible way. The price mechanism responds automatically to consumers’ wishes. If demand for electric bikes rises and demand for petrol cars falls, the price of electric bikes rises and the price of petrol cars falls. Making electric bikes becomes more profitable, so firms move land, labour and capital out of car production and into bikes. Resources are therefore used to make what consumers value most, without a government needing to plan it.

Competition between private firms also encourages efficiency. Firms that keep costs low and make good-quality products win customers, while inefficient firms make losses and close, releasing their resources to better uses. Wages work in the same way: higher wages attract workers into the jobs where labour is most in demand. As a result, output is produced at low cost and sold at low prices.

Why it might not. The market ignores external costs and benefits. A factory that pollutes a river does not pay for the damage, so its product is over-produced, while goods with external benefits, such as vaccinations, are under-produced. In the same way, merit goods such as education are under-consumed because people do not realise their full benefit, demerit goods such as cigarettes are over-consumed, and public goods such as street lighting are not produced at all because firms cannot stop non-payers from benefiting.

A market economy may also not stay competitive. A firm may grow into a monopoly, for example through mergers like the one between the two Thai banks, and then restrict output and raise prices. Resources may be slow to move too: if workers are occupationally or geographically immobile, there can be unemployment in declining industries while growing industries are short of labour.

Conclusion. A market economy is good at allocating resources to private goods that consumers want, such as clothing or smartphones, when markets are competitive. It does not allocate resources in the best possible way where there are external costs or benefits, merit, demerit or public goods, or monopoly power. How close it comes to the best allocation depends on how competitive its markets are and how important these market failures are, which is why most countries choose a mixed economy.

Why this scores
This meets Level 3: a definition, two developed arguments on each side (price signals moving resources and competition; externalities, merit, demerit and public goods, monopoly and immobility) and a conclusion that states the conditions. The examiner report says weaker answers confused a market economy with a mixed or planned economy, or just stated points without explaining how resources move.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/24 Q3(d) · October/November 2024 · [8 marks]

Source material: However, Madagascar has a largely market economic system. Nearly 70% of Madagascar’s population live in poverty.

Discuss whether or not a high level of poverty is likely to exist in a market economic system.

A market economic system allocates resources through the price mechanism, with little government intervention. Absolute poverty is when people do not have enough income to meet their basic needs; relative poverty is when people have much less income than the average in their country.

Why a high level of poverty is likely. In a market system, people’s incomes depend on the factors of production they own. Workers with few skills earn low wages, while owners of capital and land earn profit and rent, so income and wealth are very unequally distributed. With little or no government welfare, such as unemployment benefits or pensions, people who cannot work or who lose their jobs have no income to fall back on, so they fall into poverty.

Merit goods such as education and healthcare are available only to those who can pay for them. Poor families cannot afford them, so their children stay unskilled and unhealthy and earn low wages as adults, which keeps poverty going from one generation to the next. Firms may also grow into monopolies and raise the prices of necessities, reducing what low-income households can buy. Madagascar, with a largely market system, has nearly 70% of its population living in poverty.

Why a high level of poverty is not likely. Competition and the profit motive encourage firms to be efficient, which keeps prices low, so people’s incomes buy more. Efficient firms also expand and create jobs; higher employment means more households earn an income and fewer are in absolute poverty.

Market economies respond quickly to changes in consumer demand and give entrepreneurs an incentive to invest and innovate, which can lead to high economic growth: Madagascar grew by 6% in 2020. Over time, faster growth raises average incomes, so even if relative poverty remains because incomes are unequal, absolute poverty can fall, as it has in many high-income economies with large private sectors.

Conclusion. A market economic system is likely to have a high level of relative poverty because it creates income inequality and provides no safety net. Whether absolute poverty is high depends mainly on how productive and competitive its firms are and how fast the economy grows: a low-income, labour-intensive economy like Madagascar is likely to have high poverty, while a high-income market economy with efficient firms is likely to have low absolute poverty but still some relative poverty.

Why this scores
This meets Level 3: it defines the key terms, develops two arguments on each side, uses the Madagascar data, and the conclusion separates absolute from relative poverty, which the mark scheme credits as an evaluative point.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

2.10 Market failure

These questions test why a free market can produce an inefficient allocation of resources, through external costs and benefits, merit and demerit goods, public goods and monopoly power; examiners reward precise definitions and clear links to over- or under-consumption.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports say candidates often confuse public goods with merit goods, free goods or basic necessities, and give an example of an external cost (such as pollution) without explaining why it is external: it falls on third parties who are not involved in producing or consuming the product.
0455/23/M/J/20 Q5(a) · May/June 2020 · [2 marks]

Source material: Part of the change in price may have been the result of UK energy firms abusing their monopoly power. This would be an example of market failure.

Define market failure.

Market failure is when the market (price) mechanism does not lead to an efficient allocation of resources, for example when firms ignore the external costs of the pollution they cause.

Why this scores
One mark for the market or price mechanism and one for “does not lead to an efficient allocation of resources”. Both key phrases are needed: a vague answer such as “when a market does not work well” is unlikely to score.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/25 Q1(c) · May/June 2025 · [2 marks]

Source material: However, if rainforests continue to be cut down and soil erosion continues to pollute the coral reefs, killing off marine life, tourism may be discouraged.

Explain how tourism causes external costs in St. Kitts and Nevis.

External costs are harmful effects on third parties who are not involved in producing or consuming a product, so the tourists and hotels do not pay for them and the social cost of tourism is greater than the private cost. For example, sewage and waste from hotels and tourist boats in St. Kitts and Nevis can pollute the coral reefs and kill marine life, which harms local fishermen who take no part in the tourism.

Why this scores
One mark for explaining what an external cost is (a cost to third parties, so social cost exceeds private cost) and one for a relevant tourism example. The examiner report says most candidates gave an example such as pollution but did not explain why it was an external cost.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/25 Q3(b) · May/June 2025 · [4 marks]

Source material: Its government provides merit goods and public goods.

Explain, with an example of each, the difference between a merit good and a public good.

Merit good. A merit good is a product that is more beneficial to consumers than they realise, so in a free market it is under-consumed and under-produced. It is rival and excludable, so it can be sold by private firms as well as provided by the government. An example is education.

Public good. A public good is non-excludable, because people who do not pay cannot be stopped from using it, and non-rival, because one person’s use does not reduce the amount left for others. Because of the free rider problem, private firms will not supply it, so it is provided by the government and paid for out of taxes. An example is street lighting.

Why this scores
One mark for each explanation and one for each correct example. The examiner report warns that some candidates compared merit goods with demerit goods, called education or healthcare public goods (they are rival and excludable), or said merit goods are simply “beneficial” rather than more beneficial than people realise. Roads are not accepted as a public good unless qualified.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/22 Q4(b) · October/November 2022 · [4 marks]

Source material: However, public goods are not supplied in a market economic system.

Explain why public goods would not be supplied in a market economic system.

Non-excludability and free riders. Public goods are non-excludable: once a public good such as a flood defence is provided, it is impossible to stop people who have not paid from benefiting. Consumers therefore have an incentive to be free riders and wait for others to pay, so a private firm cannot collect enough revenue to cover its costs.

Non-rivalry and the profit motive. Public goods are also non-rival: one more person using street lighting does not reduce the benefit to anyone else and costs the producer nothing extra, so there is no way to charge each extra user a price. Private firms in a market economy are motivated by profit, and because they would make a loss supplying a product they cannot sell, they do not supply public goods at all.

Why this scores
Each characteristic is identified (1) and linked to why firms cannot earn revenue or profit (1). The mark scheme gives no marks for examples, so the marks come from the explanation. The examiner report notes that some candidates confused public goods with merit goods, free goods or basic necessities.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/23 Q3(c) · October/November 2023 · [6 marks]

Source material: However, there can be market failure due to external costs arising from high energy usage of non‑renewable sources.

Analyse the consequences of market failure.

Market failure occurs when the free market does not lead to an efficient allocation of resources. Its main consequence is that the wrong quantity of some products is produced and consumed, which reduces society’s welfare.

Over-consumption of products with external costs and of demerit goods. Producers and users of digital money do not pay for the pollution caused by the non-renewable energy it uses, so its price is below its full social cost. As a result, more is produced and consumed than is socially efficient, and too many resources are allocated to it. Demerit goods such as cigarettes are over-consumed in the same way because consumers underestimate the harm, leading to ill health and higher healthcare costs for society.

Under-consumption of merit goods and products with external benefits. People underestimate the benefits of education and healthcare and ignore the benefits to others, so they buy too little. The economy then has a less skilled and less healthy labour force, which lowers productivity and economic growth.

Non-provision of public goods. Because of the free rider problem, private firms will not supply public goods such as defence at all, so society goes without services it values.

Abuse of monopoly power. A monopoly can restrict output and charge higher prices, which reduces consumers’ choice and what they can afford, while resources are under-used.

Why this scores
It defines market failure, then traces each type through to its consequence (over- or under-consumption, misallocation of resources, and effects on health, productivity or prices), using the digital money context. Each link in a chain earns credit; the mark scheme also accepts factor immobility leading to unemployment.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

2.11 Mixed economic system

These questions test how and why governments intervene in a mixed economy, through maximum and minimum prices, taxes, subsidies, regulation, privatisation and direct provision; examiners reward answers that trace the effect on price and quantity and then weigh up whether the intervention works.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports say candidates often confuse a minimum price with a maximum price, and on price-control diagrams shift the demand or supply curve instead of drawing a horizontal price line and marking the quantity demanded and quantity supplied at that price.
0455/22/F/M/23 Q3(a) · February/March 2023 · [2 marks]

Source material: The Indian government sets a minimum price for sugar cane and subsidises the export of sugar.

Define a minimum price.

A minimum price is a price floor set by the government above the equilibrium price, below which the product cannot legally be sold, such as the minimum price India sets for sugar cane to protect farmers’ incomes.

Why this scores
One mark for “set by the government” (or a price floor) and one for “above the equilibrium price”. The examiner report says some candidates confused it with a maximum price or described it as the lowest price a firm can charge and still make a profit.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/25 Q1(e) · February/March 2025 · [4 marks]

Source material: In April 2022, the Romanian Government placed a maximum price on electricity.

Draw a demand and supply diagram to show the effect of setting a maximum price on electricity.

The maximum price (Pmax) is set below the equilibrium price (Pe). At this lower price, consumers want to buy Qd of electricity but producers are only willing to supply Qs, so there is a shortage equal to Qd − Qs. The demand and supply curves do not shift.

Why this scores
One mark each for labelled axes, labelled D and S curves, a maximum price drawn below the equilibrium price, and Qd and Qs shown at the maximum price. The examiner report says the strongest diagrams showed both quantities, while some candidates wrongly shifted the demand or supply curve or drew them the wrong way round.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/21 Q1(d) · October/November 2021 · [4 marks]

Source material: A successful sale would have raised money for the Indian government and might have increased the airline’s efficiency.

Explain two reasons why a government may privatise an industry.

To raise revenue. Selling a state-owned firm such as Air India to the private sector brings in a large one-off sum for the government. This money could be spent on education or healthcare, or used to cut taxes or reduce government debt. Air India was also loss-making (its total cost of $2.9bn exceeded its total revenue of $2.2bn in 2018), so privatisation ends the need to use tax revenue to cover its losses.

To increase efficiency. Private sector owners are motivated by profit, so they have an incentive to cut waste, lower costs of production and improve the quality of the service. Facing competition from other airlines, a privatised Air India would have to become more efficient to keep its customers, which could lead to lower fares and a better service for passengers.

Why this scores
One mark for each reason identified and one for each explanation. The examiner report says most candidates picked raising revenue and increasing efficiency from the source; the revenue point is strongest when it says what the money could be used for.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/25 Q5(c) · October/November 2025 · [6 marks]

Analyse how government regulation can reduce market failure.

Market failure occurs when the free market leads to an inefficient allocation of resources. Regulation means rules and laws, backed by penalties such as fines, that control what firms and consumers may do.

Reducing demerit goods and external costs. Demerit goods such as cigarettes are over-consumed because people underestimate the harm. A law banning smoking in public places, or banning sales to under-18s, directly reduces consumption and protects third parties from passive smoking. Similarly, legal limits on the pollution a factory may emit force firms to cut their external costs, so output moves closer to the socially efficient level.

Increasing the consumption of merit goods. Merit goods are under-consumed because people do not realise their full benefits. Making it compulsory to wear seat belts, or for children to attend school until 16, ensures more people consume them, which reduces injuries and raises the skills of the labour force.

Controlling monopoly power. A monopoly can restrict output and charge high prices. A regulator can set a maximum price, for example on what a monopoly water company may charge, or block mergers, so consumers pay lower prices and more is produced.

Setting minimum standards. Sellers often know more than buyers about a product’s quality. Rules such as hygiene standards for restaurants protect consumers who cannot judge quality for themselves.

Why this scores
It defines market failure, then builds several chains: the type of market failure, the regulation used, and how it changes consumption or production. The mark scheme rewards only regulation, so taxes and subsidies are deliberately left out.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/23 Q3(d) · October/November 2023 · [8 marks]

Source material: One way of reducing external costs is to tax the product.

Discuss whether or not a tax on a product can reduce external costs.

External costs are costs imposed on third parties who are not involved in producing or consuming a product, such as pollution from the electricity used to create digital money. A tax on the product is an indirect tax, which raises its cost and price.

Why a tax might reduce external costs. A tax increases firms’ costs of production, so the supply curve shifts to the left and the price rises. Consumers respond to the higher price by buying less, so output falls. Since less is produced and consumed, less pollution is created and the market moves closer to the socially efficient output. In effect, the tax makes the polluter pay for some of the external cost it causes.

The tax also raises revenue for the government, which can be spent on reducing external costs directly, for example by subsidising renewable energy or cleaning up pollution. It also gives firms an incentive to switch to cleaner methods, such as renewable electricity, if that lets them avoid the tax.

Why it might not. If demand for the product is price-inelastic, for example because it is addictive like cigarettes or a necessity with few substitutes like petrol, a rise in price causes only a small fall in quantity demanded. Consumption, and so the external cost, falls very little; the main effect is that consumers pay more.

The tax may also be too small compared with the final price of the product to change behaviour, and it is hard for a government to measure external costs in money terms, so it may not know the right rate. If the tax is set high, it may encourage tax evasion and informal markets, where the product is sold untaxed and unregulated, so external costs may not fall at all. Digital money transactions could also simply move to countries with lower taxes.

Conclusion. A tax is likely to reduce external costs, but by how much depends mainly on the price elasticity of demand for the product and the size of the tax. It works best when demand is elastic, cleaner substitutes are available and the tax can be enforced. For addictive products or necessities, a tax works better combined with regulation, such as pollution limits, and with the revenue used to fund cleaner alternatives.

Why this scores
This meets Level 3: it defines external costs, develops two arguments on each side (the effect on supply, price and quantity; revenue and incentives; inelastic demand; evasion and choosing the rate) and reaches a conclusion that names the conditions. Linking the effect to PED, as the mark scheme does, is what lifts the evaluation.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/24 Q2(d) · May/June 2024 · [8 marks]

Source material: However, the supply of housing has not been able to keep up with demand and the government is now setting a maximum price on housing.

Discuss whether or not a government should introduce a maximum price on housing.

A maximum price is a price ceiling set by the government below the equilibrium price; the product cannot legally be sold for more. A maximum price on housing limits the rent or purchase price that can be charged.

Why a government should. In cities like Shenzhen, where the supply of housing has not kept up with demand, prices and rents rise sharply. A maximum price makes housing more affordable for low-income households and migrant workers, so they can afford shelter, a basic necessity. Because they spend less on housing, they have more income left for food, education and healthcare, which raises their living standards and reduces relative poverty and inequality.

Affordable housing also helps the wider economy. Firms in the city can attract workers more easily, so labour becomes more geographically mobile and labour shortages fall. Lower housing costs also mean workers press less for wage rises, which helps to control cost-push inflation.

Why a government should not. If the maximum price is below the equilibrium price, quantity demanded rises but quantity supplied falls, because building and renting out homes becomes less profitable. This creates a shortage of housing, so many people who want a home at the lower price cannot get one. In the long run, developers may build fewer new homes, making the shortage even worse.

A shortage also encourages informal markets, where landlords charge above the legal price or demand extra payments. Landlords trying to protect their profits may spend less on maintenance, so the quality of housing falls. And if the maximum price is set above the equilibrium price, it has no effect at all.

Conclusion. A maximum price helps households that already have a home, but it is likely to worsen the shortage of housing, which is Shenzhen’s main problem. It is more likely to be beneficial if it is set only slightly below equilibrium, is enforced to stop informal markets, and is combined with measures to increase supply, such as subsidies for house-building or the government building homes itself. Without extra supply, the government should not rely on a maximum price alone.

Why this scores
This meets Level 3: a definition, two developed arguments on each side using the Shenzhen context, analysis of the shortage caused by a price below equilibrium, and a conclusion that makes a judgement and names the conditions (the level of the price, enforcement and action on supply).
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
