Topical Past Papers · Topic 3

Past Paper Questions: Microeconomic Decision-Makers

39 real Cambridge IGCSE Economics Paper 2 questions from 2020 to 2025, sorted by sub-topic, each with its exact paper reference and a full-mark exemplar answer. The questions are quoted exactly; the model answers are my own, written to the published mark schemes and the advice in the examiner reports.

How to use this page
Cover the answer, write your own in timed conditions (about 1½ minutes per mark), then compare. 0455/22/M/J/25 Q2(d) means Paper 2, variant 2, May/June 2025, Question 2 part (d). F/M = February/March, M/J = May/June, O/N = October/November.

Jump to: 3.1 Money and banking · 3.2 Households (spending, saving, borrowing) · 3.3 Workers · 3.4 Trade unions · 3.5 Firms · 3.6 Firms and production · 3.7 Firms’ costs, revenue and objectives · 3.8 Market structure

3.1 Money and banking

These questions test what money is and does, and the different roles of central and commercial banks; examiners reward answers that keep the two types of bank clearly apart and explain why each function matters, rather than just naming it.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports note that many candidates confuse commercial banks with central banks, for example saying a commercial bank sets the interest rate or issues the currency. Central banks serve the government and the banking system; commercial banks serve households and firms.
0455/23/O/N/24 Q5(a) · October/November 2024 · [2 marks]

Define a central bank.

A central bank is a country’s government-owned bank that issues the currency and runs monetary policy, for example by setting the rate of interest. It also acts as the banker to the government and to the commercial banks.

Why this scores
One mark for “government-owned bank” and one for a correct function, such as operating monetary policy or being the banker to the government and commercial banks. Describing what a commercial bank does, such as taking deposits from the public, scores nothing.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/24 Q4(b) · May/June 2024 · [4 marks]

Source material: Its currency, the somoni, has the key characteristics of money.

Explain two characteristics of money.

Generally acceptable. People must be willing to accept money in exchange for goods and services and in settlement of debts. If sellers trust that they can spend the somoni themselves, they will take it as payment, so it can act as a medium of exchange.

Durable. Money has to last a long time without wearing out or losing its physical form. Coins and notes that survive being passed from hand to hand many times can be kept and used later, so they can also be saved as a store of value.

Why this scores
One mark for each characteristic identified and one for explaining why it lets money do its job. Other creditworthy characteristics are portable, divisible, recognisable, homogeneous and limited in supply. Do not give functions (medium of exchange, store of value) as the characteristics themselves: they belong in the explanation.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/25 Q5(b) · February/March 2025 · [4 marks]

Source material: It has a strong commercial banking sector which has increased its lending in recent years.

Explain why commercial banks may increase their lending.

To increase profits. Most commercial banks are private-sector firms whose main aim is profit. Every loan earns the bank interest, and the rate it charges borrowers is higher than the rate it pays savers, so lending more increases its revenue and therefore its profits.

Greater confidence that loans will be repaid. In a high-income country such as Finland, rising incomes and secure jobs mean households and firms are more likely to keep up their repayments. The risk of bad debts falls, so banks are willing to lend more, and to more customers.

Why this scores
One mark for each reason identified and one for each explanation. The examiner report says strong answers linked lending to the extra interest earned or to central bank encouragement; weaker answers named reasons without explaining them. The mark scheme gives only one identification mark in total for interest-rate points, so use two different reasons.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/25 Q3(c) · October/November 2025 · [6 marks]

Source material: Japan has faced significant periods of deflation in recent decades.

Analyse reasons why central banks are important for an economy.

A central bank is the government-owned bank that controls the money supply, runs monetary policy and acts as the banker to the government and to commercial banks.

Keeping prices stable. The central bank changes the rate of interest to influence total demand. In a period of deflation, as Japan has experienced, it can cut the interest rate, which makes borrowing cheaper and saving less rewarding. Households spend more on credit and firms invest more, so total demand rises and prices stop falling. This matters because deflation encourages people to delay purchases, which cuts output and jobs. When inflation is too high it does the opposite, raising interest rates to protect people’s purchasing power.

Maintaining financial stability. The central bank supervises commercial banks, for example by checking they keep enough liquid assets. If a bank runs short of cash it acts as lender of last resort, lending to it so that it does not collapse. This protects savers’ deposits, keeps public confidence in the banking system and makes sure households and firms can still borrow, so spending and investment are not suddenly cut.

Managing the exchange rate. The central bank holds the country’s foreign currency reserves and can buy or sell its own currency to stop sharp swings in the exchange rate. A more stable exchange rate reduces uncertainty for exporters and importers, so they can plan and invest with more confidence.

Why this scores
Each function is linked through to why it matters for the economy, which is what “coherent analysis” needs. The mark scheme credits controlling inflation and deflation through monetary policy, financial stability, lender of last resort and exchange-rate management, and the Japan context shows application.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/22 Q5(d) · October/November 2022 · [8 marks]

Source material: In 2019, commercial bank lending to firms and households in Palau increased.

Discuss whether or not an increase in commercial bank lending will increase economic growth.

Economic growth is an increase in a country’s real GDP, that is, in the output of goods and services. Commercial bank lending is the loans that banks make to households and firms.

Why it might increase economic growth. More loans allow households to buy expensive items such as houses, cars and furniture that they could not pay for out of current income. Consumer spending rises, so total demand rises. Firms respond by producing more and employing more workers, so real GDP increases, especially if the economy has spare capacity.

Firms can also borrow to invest in capital goods such as machinery, buildings and technology, and new firms can borrow to start up. Investment adds to total demand straight away, but it also increases the economy’s productive capacity, because more and better capital raises productivity. This means output can keep rising in the long run.

Why it might not increase economic growth. If the economy is already at or near full employment, firms cannot easily produce more. Higher total demand then pulls up prices rather than output, causing demand-pull inflation instead of growth.

The extra spending may also go on imports. Palau is a small island economy that has to import many goods, so loans spent on imported cars or electronics raise output in other countries, not in Palau. In addition, some households and firms may borrow simply to repay old debts, and those who take on more debt must use future income for repayments. If interest rates later rise, their spending and investment will fall, which can reverse any growth.

Conclusion. Higher commercial bank lending is likely to give at least a short-term boost to growth. It is most likely to raise growth in the long run if the loans finance investment by firms rather than consumer spending, if the economy has unemployed resources, and if the money is spent on domestically produced goods. In a small, import-dependent economy like Palau, much of the effect of extra household borrowing may leak abroad, so the benefit depends mainly on how much of the lending goes to productive investment.

Why this scores
Level 3: definitions first, two developed chains on each side (consumption and investment versus inflation, imports and debt), Palau’s context used, and a conclusion that states the conditions. The examiner report noted that the “might not” side was usually weak and limited to a mention of debt or imports, so develop it as fully as the first side.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

3.2 Households (spending, saving, borrowing)

These questions test what influences how much households spend, save and borrow, and how those choices change with income, interest rates and confidence; examiners reward a clear link from each influence to the household’s decision.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports say weak answers give “because they have a higher income” or “they spend more” as if it were an explanation, or answer the opposite question (why households save more when asked why they save less). Always explain the mechanism: interest rates, confidence, collateral or the cost of what is being bought.
0455/22/M/J/25 Q5(a) · May/June 2025 · [2 marks]

Source material: It was expected that higher output would be caused mainly by an increase in household spending, although this could also result in inflation.

Identify two influences on household spending.

1. Income.

2. The rate of interest.

Why this scores
One mark for each correct influence. Other creditworthy answers include taxation, confidence, wealth, the inflation rate, age and the size of the household. The examiner report notes that some candidates gave consequences of a change in spending instead of influences on it.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/22 Q3(b) · February/March 2022 · [4 marks]

Explain two reasons why a high-income household may borrow more than a low-income household.

Banks are more willing to lend to them. A high-income household is more likely to keep up its repayments, and it often owns assets, such as a house, that can be offered as collateral. Because the bank faces less risk that the loan will not be repaid, it is prepared to lend larger amounts and may charge a lower rate of interest, which encourages more borrowing.

They buy more high-value items. High-income households tend to buy expensive houses, new cars and private education for their children. These cost far more than one month’s income, so they are paid for with mortgages and other loans, which means the household’s total borrowing is higher.

Why this scores
One mark for each reason and one for each explanation. The examiner report says “because they have a higher income” is not an explanation, and saying they “spend more” earns nothing unless you show why that spending is financed by borrowing. Collateral and confidence about repayment were credited in the strongest answers.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/21 Q4(b) · October/November 2021 · [4 marks]

Source material: Tunisia’s GDP increased from 2014 to 2018 but its households saved less.

Explain two reasons why households may save less even though their income has increased.

A fall in the rate of interest. Lower interest rates reduce the reward for saving, so keeping money in a savings account becomes less attractive. Borrowing also becomes cheaper, so households choose to spend more of their income now rather than save it, and saving falls even though income has risen.

Greater confidence about the future. If households expect their jobs to be secure and their incomes to keep rising, they feel less need to save for emergencies or for a period of unemployment. They therefore spend a larger share of their extra income, for example on new products or a higher standard of living, and save less.

Why this scores
One mark for each reason and one for each explanation. The examiner report singled out a fall in the interest rate and a rise in confidence as features of the strongest answers, and warned that some candidates explained why households would save more: read the question carefully.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/22 Q5(c) · May/June 2022 · [6 marks]

Source material: Nicaragua experienced a 4% decrease in GDP in 2019, although household spending rose.

Analyse how the level and pattern of household spending may change when GDP decreases.

GDP is the total value of output produced in a country in a year, so a fall in GDP usually means falling incomes. The level of household spending is the total amount spent; the pattern is what it is spent on.

The level is likely to fall. When output falls, firms cut jobs, hours and pay, so household incomes fall. Households have less purchasing power and buy fewer goods and services. Lower GDP also reduces confidence: people worried about losing their jobs save more for emergencies and are less willing to borrow in case they cannot repay. If they expect prices to fall, they may also delay buying expensive items such as cars. All of this cuts spending further.

But the level may not fall. As in Nicaragua in 2019, spending can rise even when GDP falls. If households believe the fall is only temporary, they may use their past savings or borrow, perhaps helped by lower interest rates during the downturn, to maintain their living standards. Spending then falls by less than income, or not at all, so households spend a higher proportion of their income.

The pattern is likely to change. With lower incomes, households spend a larger share on necessities such as food, rent and electricity, and cut back on luxuries such as holidays, eating out and new clothes. They may also switch to cheaper brands and buy fewer imported goods.

Why this scores
It covers both the level and the pattern, which the question asks for, and develops each chain: lower GDP → lower income or confidence → less spending or more saving. The examiner report said weaker answers stopped at “income falls so they spend less”, while strong ones analysed necessities versus luxuries and the motives for saving and borrowing. The “may not fall” paragraph uses the stimulus and is credited by the mark scheme.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/21 Q5(d) · May/June 2021 · [8 marks]

Source material: Smartphones have made it easier for people to borrow and save their money.

Discuss whether or not an increase in the level of savings is beneficial for an economy.

Saving is the part of disposable income that is not spent on consumption. An increase in the level of savings means households are putting aside more of their income.

Why it may be beneficial. Savings deposited in banks provide funds that banks can lend to firms. Firms borrow to invest in machinery, technology and training, which raises productivity and the economy’s productive capacity. This leads to economic growth and higher living standards in the future. In low-income countries, mobile banking on smartphones lets people in rural areas save safely in banks, so savings that were once kept at home can now finance investment.

Saving also protects households. People who save for retirement, illness or a period of unemployment depend less on borrowing and on government support, so they are less likely to fall into debt or poverty. If the economy has high inflation, more saving and less spending would reduce total demand and help to slow the rise in prices, and less spending on imports could improve the current account.

Why it may not be beneficial. Saving more means spending less now. Lower consumer spending reduces total demand, so firms sell less, cut output and lay off workers. Unemployment rises and economic growth slows, and the government receives less tax revenue from incomes and spending, which reduces what it can spend on public services.

The extra savings may not even be used for investment. If firms see their sales falling, they have little reason to invest in more capital, however much money banks have to lend, so the savings sit idle. A rise in saving can also be a sign that confidence is low, which itself discourages spending and investment.

Conclusion. An increase in savings is likely to be beneficial in the long run if it is channelled through banks into investment, which is especially important in low-income countries where a shortage of savings limits investment, and when the economy is close to full capacity or has high inflation. It is likely to be harmful in the short run if the economy is already in or near a recession, because the fall in spending would deepen the downturn and firms would not want to borrow the extra funds.

Why this scores
Level 3: a definition, two developed arguments each side (investment and security against lower total demand and idle savings), use of the smartphone context and a conclusion that depends on the state of the economy and whether savings reach investment. The examiner report said even good answers often lacked the depth for Level 3, so each point here is taken through to its final effect.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

3.3 Workers

These questions test why people choose particular jobs, why some jobs pay far more than others and how division of labour affects firms and workers; examiners reward answers built on the demand for and supply of labour, applied to the specific workers named.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports note that many candidates cannot define division of labour, confusing it with a country or firm specialising. Division of labour means breaking production into separate tasks done by different workers, and in a question about workers, benefits to the firm are not rewarded.
0455/21/O/N/25 Q4(a) · October/November 2025 · [2 marks]

Source material: The government is trying to attract more workers to the island by using non‑wage factors.

Identify two non‑wage factors that may influence a worker’s job choice.

1. Job security.

2. The number of paid holidays.

Why this scores
One mark for each relevant non-wage factor; others include working hours, fringe benefits, flexibility to work from home and the location of the workplace. Avoid bonuses, commission or overtime pay: these are part of the wage, not non-wage factors.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/24 Q3(b) · February/March 2024 · [4 marks]

Source material: Firms in Monaco pay high wages.

Explain two reasons why low-skilled workers may be highly paid.

A high national minimum wage. If the government sets the minimum wage at a high level, every firm must legally pay at least that rate. Even workers with few skills, such as cleaners or shop assistants, then receive a high wage.

The job is dangerous or unpleasant. Some low-skilled jobs, such as refuse collection or work on fishing boats, are dirty, risky or involve unsociable hours. Few people are willing to do them, so the supply of labour is low and employers have to pay a high wage to attract and keep workers.

Why this scores
One mark for each reason and one for its explanation; the mark scheme gives both marks for a high national minimum wage. Other credited reasons include high or inelastic demand for labour, low supply, strong trade unions and long hours. Explain each in terms of demand for or supply of labour.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/24 Q2(c) · October/November 2024 · [6 marks]

Source material: The average annual wage of a waiter was $23,580 while that of a chief executive of a large commercial bank was $1,617,000.

Analyse why the wage of a chief executive of a large commercial bank is higher than that of a waiter.

Wages are determined by the demand for and supply of labour in each occupation. In 2021 the chief executive earned about 69 times as much as a waiter ($1,617,000 ÷ $23,580 ≈ 68.6), so demand must be relatively high, and supply relatively low, for chief executives.

Supply. To run a large bank, a person needs high qualifications, many years of experience and rare skills in leadership and finance. Few people have these, so the supply of chief executives is small and inelastic. The job also carries great responsibility, stress and long hours, so a high wage is needed to persuade suitable people to do it. Being a waiter needs few qualifications and little training, so many people can do the job, including students and part-time workers. The supply of waiters is large and elastic, which keeps their wage low.

Demand. A chief executive’s decisions can add or lose millions of dollars for the bank, so a good one is extremely valuable. Banks compete to hire the best person and are willing to pay whatever it takes, so demand is high and inelastic. A single waiter adds much less to a restaurant’s revenue and can easily be replaced, or even partly replaced by ordering apps, so demand for waiters is lower and more elastic.

Bargaining power. A chief executive negotiates their own pay from a strong position, because they could move to a rival bank anywhere in the world. Waiters are often young, part-time or migrant workers who are rarely in trade unions, so they have little bargaining power to push up their wages.

Why this scores
It uses demand and supply of labour throughout and compares both workers in every paragraph, which is what the mark scheme rewards: qualifications, training, skills, responsibility, inelastic supply, inelastic demand and bargaining power. The figures are interpreted, not just copied. Note that the mark scheme gives nothing for “banks make more profit than restaurants”, since a cleaner in a bank may earn less than a waiter.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/25 Q3(c) · May/June 2025 · [6 marks]

Source material: The firms in the shampoo industry make use of division of labour.

Analyse how division of labour can benefit firms.

Division of labour is when production is broken down into separate tasks, with each worker doing one particular task, for example mixing, bottling, quality testing or packing shampoo.

Higher productivity and lower costs. Because each worker repeats the same task, they become highly skilled at it and do not waste time moving between jobs or changing tools. Output per worker rises, so the firm produces more shampoo with the same workforce. Its average cost falls, which allows it to cut its price to attract more customers or to keep the price the same and earn a higher profit margin.

Better quality and higher revenue. Skilled, practised workers make fewer mistakes and waste fewer materials, so the quality of the product improves. Higher quality shampoo increases demand and sales, raising the firm’s revenue and profit.

Lower training costs. Each worker only needs to learn one simple task, so training is quick and cheap and new workers become productive sooner.

Easier to use machines. Once production is split into simple, repetitive tasks, many of them, such as filling bottles, can be done by machines. Mechanisation raises output further and reduces the number of workers needed, cutting labour costs.

Why this scores
It defines division of labour, then follows each benefit through to costs, prices, sales or profit, matching the mark scheme’s chains. The examiner report praised these links and noted that few candidates mentioned mechanisation, so including it makes the answer stand out.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/25 Q3(d) · October/November 2025 · [8 marks]

Source material: These advances in technology were combined with a greater use of division of labour.

Discuss whether or not division of labour will benefit workers.

Division of labour is when production is broken down into separate tasks, with each worker specialising in one task, such as processing loans, handling online customer queries or managing IT security in a bank.

Why it might benefit workers. Repeating the same task makes workers highly skilled at it: practice makes perfect. They become more confident and find the work less stressful because they know exactly what to do. Their higher productivity can lead to higher wages or bonuses, and makes the firm more competitive, which improves their job security.

Training for one task is quick, so people can find work without long periods of study and start earning sooner. Workers can also concentrate on the area they are most interested in and best at, such as cyber-security in online banking, and build a career as a specialist.

Why it might not benefit workers. Doing the same task all day can be boring and repetitive. Low job satisfaction reduces motivation and can cause stress, and workers may feel their wider skills and talents are not being used.

Workers who only know one narrow task have low occupational mobility. Simple repetitive tasks are also the easiest to replace with machines and technology: as more banking moves online, a worker whose only skill is counting cash at a branch counter may lose their job and struggle to find another, leading to structural unemployment. Their work can also be disrupted if colleagues doing earlier tasks are absent.

Conclusion. Division of labour is most likely to benefit workers when their tasks are skilled and well paid, as with specialist technology roles in banking, and when employers rotate jobs and retrain staff as technology changes. It is least likely to benefit them when the tasks are simple and repetitive, because those jobs are both boring and the first to be automated, which is exactly what the shift to online banking is doing.

Why this scores
Level 3: a definition, two developed arguments each side, the banking and technology context from the stimulus, and a conclusion that depends on the type of task and how employers manage it. The mark scheme states that benefits to the firm are not rewarded, so every point here is about the workers.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

3.4 Trade unions

These questions test what trade unions do for their members and how their actions affect firms and the wider economy; examiners reward answers that use collective bargaining and follow it through to wages, conditions, productivity and jobs.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports note that some candidates confuse trade unions with international trade or trade organisations, and that in “Analyse how…” questions some waste time on disadvantages that were not asked for. Answer only the side the question asks about unless it says “Discuss”.
0455/22/F/M/23 Q5(c) · February/March 2023 · [6 marks]

Source material: Few raspberry farm workers are members of trade unions. Membership of trade unions in Serbia had fallen to 20% of all workers by 2020.

Analyse why some workers decide not to join a trade union.

A trade union is an organisation of workers that aims to protect and improve its members’ pay, working conditions and job security, mainly through collective bargaining with employers.

The cost of membership. Trade unions charge a subscription fee. Low-paid workers, such as many raspberry farm workers, may not be able to afford it, or may think that the fee is more than the benefit they would get, so they choose to keep the money.

Satisfied with pay and conditions. Workers who already have good wages, safe working conditions and secure jobs see little need for collective bargaining, so they gain little from joining.

Fear of the employer. Some employers discourage union membership. Workers, especially seasonal or temporary farm workers who can easily be replaced, may fear that joining will reduce their chances of being employed again or promoted, so they stay out to protect their jobs.

Weak unions and strikes. If the government has passed laws that limit what unions can do, unions have less power to win higher wages, so there is less benefit from joining. Some workers also do not want to take part in industrial action, because they would lose pay during a strike, or they disagree with the union’s aims.

Why this scores
It defines a trade union and then gives several reasons, each with its consequence for the worker’s decision, using the Serbian farm context. The examiner report praised answers that picked up the word “not” and covered satisfaction with pay and conditions, reluctance to strike, the subscription fee and government discouragement.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/21 Q5(c) · October/November 2021 · [6 marks]

Source material: However, trade unions may also play a key role in economic development. There are over 60 trade unions in Singapore, in both labour-intensive and capital-intensive industries.

Analyse how trade unions could increase economic development.

Economic development is an improvement in people’s living standards and welfare, including income, health, education and working conditions, not just a rise in GDP.

Higher wages. Through collective bargaining, a trade union negotiates on behalf of all its members, which gives workers more bargaining power than they would have alone. This can win higher wages, which increase workers’ purchasing power, so they can afford better food, housing, healthcare and education for their children. Living standards rise.

Better working conditions. Unions push for safer workplaces, reasonable working hours and protection against unfair dismissal. Fewer accidents and less overwork improve workers’ health, which raises their quality of life and life expectancy, and greater job security reduces the risk of falling into poverty.

Higher productivity. Better pay, conditions and job security increase workers’ motivation, and unions in Singapore also support training. More motivated and better-skilled workers produce more output per hour, so firms’ average costs fall. Firms can then lower prices and become more internationally competitive, increasing output, exports and economic growth. Higher incomes and output also raise tax revenue that the government can spend on schools and hospitals, further improving development.

Why this scores
It defines economic development and builds clear chains from union activity to living standards, as the mark scheme sets out. The examiner report said the key to a good answer was productivity, and warned that the question asks only for how unions could increase development, so the answer does not waste time on negative effects. Some candidates confused trade unions with international trade.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/23 Q5(d) · October/November 2023 · [8 marks]

Source material: Trade unions in some of these cities are calling for more affordable housing for workers.

Discuss whether or not trade unions benefit workers.

A trade union is an organisation of workers that aims to protect and improve its members’ pay, working conditions and job security, mainly through collective bargaining with employers.

Why trade unions might benefit workers. Through collective bargaining, a union negotiates for all its members together, so workers have far more bargaining power than if each negotiated alone. The threat of industrial action means employers are more likely to agree to higher wages, raising workers’ incomes and living standards. Unions also negotiate better working hours, safer conditions and protection against unfair dismissal, which gives members greater job security.

Unions can also influence the government on behalf of workers. Trade unions in cities such as London and Hong Kong are calling for more affordable housing; if the government responds, workers’ living costs fall. Unions also campaign for policies such as maternity pay and a higher minimum wage, and provide services to members such as training and legal advice.

Why trade unions might not benefit workers. Higher wages negotiated by a union raise firms’ costs of production. Firms may respond by employing fewer workers or replacing them with machines, so some members lose their jobs. The workers who keep their jobs gain, but at the expense of those made unemployed.

Strikes mean workers lose pay while they are not working, and frequent industrial action can reduce firms’ profits and make the economy less competitive. Firms may then invest less or move production abroad, which reduces the demand for workers in the long run. Some workers may also disagree with the union’s aims or feel the subscription fees are too high for the benefits they get.

Conclusion. Trade unions are likely to benefit workers when the firm is profitable and demand for its product is price-inelastic, because it can pass on higher wages in higher prices without cutting jobs. They are less likely to benefit workers in industries facing strong competition, especially from abroad, where higher wages and strikes lead to job losses. On balance, unions usually benefit the members who stay in work, but the benefit depends on whether wage rises are matched by higher productivity.

Why this scores
Level 3: a definition, two developed arguments each side (collective bargaining and influence on government versus job losses and strikes), use of the housing context and a conclusion that depends on the firm’s profitability, competition and productivity. Each point is followed through to its effect on workers, not on firms.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/24 Q5(d) · October/November 2024 · [8 marks]

Source material: In recent years, trade union membership in the Peruvian fruit-farming industry has increased.

Discuss whether or not an increase in trade union membership will benefit an economy.

A trade union is an organisation of workers that aims to protect and improve its members’ pay, working conditions and job security. An increase in membership gives the union more bargaining power when it negotiates with employers.

Why it might benefit an economy. Stronger unions can win higher wages and better conditions for low-paid workers such as fruit farm workers. Better pay and conditions raise motivation, so productivity and output increase. Higher incomes also mean workers spend more, which increases total demand, so firms produce more and real GDP and employment rise. Living standards improve and the gap between rich and poor narrows.

Unions also provide a channel of communication between workers and employers. Problems can be solved through negotiation rather than conflict, which reduces disputes and staff turnover. Unions that help organise training raise workers’ skills, which increases the quality and quantity of output.

Why it might not benefit an economy. Higher wages raise firms’ costs of production. Firms may pass these on in higher prices, causing cost-push inflation. Peru’s fruit exports would become less price-competitive on world markets, so export sales could fall, worsening the current account. Farms may also cut jobs or replace workers with machines, increasing unemployment.

More powerful unions may also take industrial action. A strike during the harvest could mean fruit is left to rot, so output and GDP fall and export orders are lost. Unions may resist the introduction of new technology to protect jobs, which prevents improvements in efficiency and holds back economic growth.

Conclusion. Higher trade union membership is likely to benefit Peru’s economy if unions cooperate with employers and negotiate wage rises that are matched by higher productivity, because living standards rise without costs rising. It is likely to harm the economy if stronger unions lead to frequent strikes and wage rises well above productivity growth, because fruit is an export industry facing global competition. The overall effect depends on how unions use their extra power.

Why this scores
Level 3: a definition, two developed arguments each side, the Peruvian fruit-farming context, and a conclusion conditional on productivity and union behaviour. The mark scheme caps answers at Level 2 if they misread the question as a rise in trade union fees, so keep the focus on more members and stronger bargaining power.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

3.5 Firms

These questions test whether you can explain how and why firms grow, the different types of merger, and how economies and diseconomies of scale change a firm’s average cost; examiners reward named economies of scale that are explained in the context of the firm, not simply listed.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports say weaker answers confuse stages of production with sectors of production when explaining mergers, and discuss things that raise total cost (hiring more workers, buying more machines) as if they were economies of scale. An economy of scale must lower average cost, the cost per unit.
0455/22/F/M/24 Q3(a) · February/March 2024 · [2 marks]

Source material: Some firms in Monaco have experienced external growth.

Define external growth of firms.

External growth is an increase in the size of a firm (its output or scale of operation) that comes from joining with another firm, through a merger or a takeover, rather than from expanding its own capacity.

Why this scores
One mark for the idea of an increase in the size or output of a firm and one for saying it happens through a merger or takeover. Describing a firm opening a new branch or factory would be internal growth and loses the second mark.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/25 Q3(b) · May/June 2025 · [4 marks]

Source material: Several winter sports firms in Andorra have recently merged.

Explain two types of merger.

Horizontal merger. This is when two firms in the same industry and at the same stage of production join together. For example, two Andorran ski resorts could merge. The new firm has a bigger share of the winter sports market and faces less competition.

Vertical merger. This is when two firms in the same industry but at different stages of production join together. If a ski resort merged with a firm that manufactures skis, it would be a vertical backward merger, because the resort is moving back towards its supplier. If it merged with a travel agent that sells ski holidays, it would be a vertical forward merger, moving towards the final consumer.

Why this scores
One mark for naming each type and one for explaining it, with an example securing the explanation mark. The examiner report says many candidates confused stages of production (raw materials, manufacturing, retailing) with sectors of the economy (primary, secondary, tertiary). A conglomerate merger, between firms in different industries, would also be accepted.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/25 Q3(c) · October/November 2025 · [6 marks]

Source material: It is expected the merger will lower average total cost as the new firm is likely to benefit from greater internal economies of scale.

Analyse the internal economies of scale that a cosmetics firm could experience.

Internal economies of scale are the reductions in average total cost (cost per unit) that a firm gains as a result of its own growth in size.

Purchasing economies. A large cosmetics firm buys ingredients such as oils, fragrances and packaging in very large quantities. Suppliers give it bulk-buying discounts, so the cost of the raw materials in each bottle of perfume or tube of lipstick falls, which lowers average total cost.

Technical economies. A larger firm can afford advanced, automated machinery for mixing, filling and packaging its products. This equipment is expensive, but when it is used to produce millions of units its cost is spread over a much larger output. Output per worker also rises, so the cost of producing each unit falls.

Marketing economies. Advertising a cosmetics brand, for example through a global social media campaign, costs much the same whether the firm sells one million or ten million units. A large firm spreads this cost over far more sales, so its advertising cost per unit is much lower than a small rival’s.

Financial and research and development economies. Banks see a large firm as less risky, so it can borrow more cheaply, at lower interest rates, which reduces its costs. It can also afford its own research laboratory to develop new products, such as perfumes made from the renewable raw materials it plans to use, and the cost of that research is spread across a large output.

Why this scores
Opens with a definition and then fully develops four named internal economies, each ending with the effect on average (unit) cost. The mark scheme caps an undeveloped list of economies at 3 marks, so it is the explanation of how each one lowers cost per unit that earns the top marks.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/M/J/25 Q1(h) · May/June 2025 · [6 marks]

Source material: The Gabonese Government wants to develop its textile industry, encouraging firms to grow in size.

Discuss whether or not a growth in the size of firms in Gabon’s textile industry will reduce their average cost of production.

Average cost is total cost divided by output. It will fall if firms gain economies of scale as they grow, and rise if they suffer diseconomies of scale.

Why average cost may fall. Larger textile firms can buy cotton, dyes and thread in bulk and receive discounts, so the material cost of each metre of cloth falls (purchasing economies). They can also afford advanced weaving machines and spread their cost over a much bigger output (technical economies). In addition, with more workers each one can specialise in one task, such as cutting or sewing, which the source mentions; this raises labour productivity so the labour cost per item falls.

Why average cost may not fall. If a firm grows too large it may suffer diseconomies of scale. Communication becomes harder as there are more layers of management, so decisions are slower and mistakes more frequent. Workers may feel less valued in a very large firm, so motivation and productivity fall. In both cases output rises by a smaller proportion than costs, so average cost rises. Simply employing more workers or buying more machines also raises total cost and will only lower average cost if it makes production more efficient.

Conclusion. Gabon’s textile firms are likely to see average cost fall as they grow, because they are starting from a small size where economies of scale are large. However, this depends on how well the growth is managed: if firms grow so large that communication and worker motivation suffer, diseconomies of scale could push average cost back up.

Why this scores
The mark scheme gives up to 4 marks for each side, and full marks for two explained economies and one explained diseconomy of scale. The examiner report says weaker answers just listed economies of scale or wrote about factors that raise total cost; this answer links every point to cost per unit and uses the source’s reference to specialisation.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/23 Q4(d) · May/June 2023 · [8 marks]

Source material: Some dairy farms and some small firms went out of business in 2020.

Discuss whether or not small firms are more likely to go out of business than large firms.

A small firm is one with few employees, a low output and a small share of its market. A firm goes out of business when its revenue does not cover its costs for long enough that it can no longer pay its bills and has to close.

Why small firms might be more likely to go out of business. Small firms produce a low output, so they cannot gain economies of scale such as bulk-buying discounts or the use of advanced machinery. Their average cost is therefore higher than that of large rivals. Large firms can then charge lower prices, attract customers away from small firms and leave them with too little revenue to cover their costs.

Small firms also find it harder to raise finance. Commercial banks see them as risky, so loans are more difficult to obtain and charge higher interest rates, and small firms usually have little retained profit to fall back on. Many are new and run by inexperienced entrepreneurs who are still testing whether demand exists. If there is a sudden fall in demand, as in Australia in 2020, a small firm has nothing to cover its losses, while a large firm can survive using its reserves.

Why small firms might not be more likely to go out of business. Large firms can suffer diseconomies of scale. Poor communication and low worker motivation can raise their average cost, so a large firm can become inefficient and make losses. Small firms are easier to manage and more flexible: the owner can quickly change the product or cut costs when demand changes.

Many small firms also serve a niche market, such as a specialist cheese made on one dairy farm, where demand is too small for a large firm to bother entering. In that market the small firm may be the only supplier and have loyal customers. Governments in many countries also give small firms grants, cheap loans or tax relief, which helps them survive.

Conclusion. Small firms are generally more likely to go out of business than large firms, mainly because they have higher average costs and less access to finance to survive a downturn. However, a small firm with a niche market, loyal customers and government support may be very secure, while a large firm that has grown too big and suffers diseconomies of scale can still fail. The risk depends most on whether the small firm competes head-on with large firms on price or serves a specialist market.

Why this scores
This fits Level 3: definitions first, two developed arguments each side (economies of scale and finance against diseconomies, flexibility, niche markets and government support) and a conclusion naming the key condition. The examiner report gave a Level 1 example that stated points such as “small firms are not as developed” without explaining them, and noted that weaker answers did not distinguish average from total cost.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

3.6 Firms and production

These questions test what determines a firm’s demand for labour and capital, why firms choose labour-intensive or capital-intensive production, and what raises productivity; examiners reward answers that link each influence to the firm’s costs, output or profit.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports note confusion between productivity (output per worker or per hour) and production (total output). Remember too that the demand for a factor of production is a derived demand: firms want workers and machines because consumers want the products they make.
0455/22/F/M/23 Q4(a) · February/March 2023 · [2 marks]

Source material: Over this period, labour productivity increased.

Identify two causes of an increase in labour productivity.

1. Better education and training, giving workers higher skills.

2. Advances in technology, giving workers better capital equipment to work with.

Why this scores
One mark for each correct cause. Other accepted answers include better healthcare, higher pay or bonuses, more experience and specialisation. The mark scheme gives nothing for just “more motivated”, and the examiner report warns against confusing productivity with total production.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/24 Q5(b) · May/June 2024 · [4 marks]

Source material: In 2020, demand for both economic goods and factors of production increased in Poland.

Explain two influences on demand for factors of production.

Demand for the product. Factors of production are a derived demand. When Polish consumers demanded more football shirts, clothing firms needed to increase output, so they demanded more workers, more sewing machines and more factory space.

The price of substitute factors. Labour and capital can often replace each other. If the price of machinery falls, or wages rise, firms find it cheaper to use machines instead of workers, so their demand for capital rises and their demand for labour falls.

Why this scores
Each influence is identified (1) and explained by showing the direction of its effect on demand for the factor (1). The mark scheme wants the price of a substitute or complementary factor, not just “price”. Other valid influences are the price of the factor itself, its productivity and the method of production.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/24 Q3(b) · October/November 2024 · [4 marks]

Source material: Most of the country’s industries are labour-intensive.

Explain two reasons why a firm may adopt labour-intensive production.

Labour is cheap. In a country such as Madagascar, where many people live in poverty, there is a large supply of workers willing to work for low wages. Labour may therefore be cheaper than buying expensive machinery, so using more workers keeps the firm’s costs of production low.

The product needs a personal service. Some products, such as hairdressing, tour guiding or handmade crafts, depend on individual attention and skill that machines cannot provide. Consumers value this quality, so the firm uses mainly labour because that is what creates the product customers want to buy.

Why this scores
One mark for each reason and one for each explanation. The first point uses the context of the stimulus (poverty and a large labour supply). Other accepted reasons include a government subsidy for employing workers and a low-skilled labour force that cannot operate high-technology machinery.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/23 Q4(c) · October/November 2023 · [6 marks]

Source material: Recently, US farmers have switched to keeping other livestock and growing crops that changed their demand for labour.

Analyse what influences a farmer’s demand for labour.

A farmer’s demand for labour is the number of workers the farmer is willing and able to employ at a given wage rate. It is a derived demand, because workers are needed only to produce the food that consumers buy.

Demand for the food produced. If demand for the crops or meat the farmer produces rises, the price and the profit from extra output rise. The farmer then expands output and needs more workers, so demand for labour increases. This explains why switching to different livestock or crops changes how many workers farmers need.

The wage rate. The lower the wage, the cheaper each worker is to employ, so the farmer can hire more workers and still make a profit. A rise in the national minimum wage would make labour more expensive and reduce the quantity of labour demanded.

The productivity and cost of machinery. If the price of farm machinery such as harvesters or drones falls, or it becomes more productive, the farmer can replace workers with machines and production becomes more capital-intensive. Demand for labour falls. If workers become more skilled and productive, each one adds more output, so they become more worth employing and demand for labour rises.

Subsidies and the type of farming. A government subsidy for selected foods lowers the farmer’s costs, encouraging higher output and more workers. Some types of farming, such as fruit picking, are labour-intensive, and demand for labour is especially high at harvest time.

Why this scores
It defines demand for labour and then analyses four separate influences, each with a chain to the number of workers the farmer will hire. Linking to the stimulus (switching crops, subsidies) and to capital as a substitute for labour is exactly what the mark scheme rewards.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/24 Q4(d) · May/June 2024 · [8 marks]

Source material: Despite this possibility, some Tajik firms bought new capital equipment.

Discuss whether or not consumers would benefit from firms buying new capital equipment.

Capital equipment is the man-made machinery, tools and technology that firms use to produce goods and services. Buying new capital equipment is a form of investment.

Why consumers might benefit. New machinery usually embodies the latest technology, so it raises productivity: more output is produced with the same workers and materials. This lowers the firm’s average cost of production. If the firm passes these lower costs on, consumers pay lower prices, which raises their purchasing power and allows them to buy more goods and services.

Modern equipment also tends to be more accurate, so it reduces mistakes and improves the quality and reliability of products. It may allow the firm to produce entirely new products, and because output increases, goods are more widely available and shortages are less likely. Consumers therefore gain more choice and better products.

Why consumers might not benefit. New capital equipment is expensive. In the short run the firm may raise its prices to cover the cost of the purchase or the interest on a loan, so consumers pay more. While workers learn to use the new machines there may be disruption, so output and quality could fall for a time.

In addition, the firm may not pass lower costs on at all. A firm with little competition, such as a monopoly, may keep prices the same and take the cost savings as higher profit. Machine-made products can also become more standardised, so consumers who valued handmade or personalised goods lose out. If workers are replaced by machines, some consumers may lose their jobs and their income.

Conclusion. Consumers are likely to benefit in the long run, mainly through lower prices and better quality, once the new equipment is working fully. The benefit is smaller, or may not appear, in the short run, or when the firm faces little competition and so has no need to cut its prices. Whether consumers gain therefore depends mainly on how competitive the market is and on the time period being considered.

Why this scores
Level 3 needs a reasoned discussion of both sides: the answer defines capital, develops two arguments each way (cost and price; quality and choice; short-run price rises; lack of competition and standardisation) and ends with a judgement that depends on competition and time. Each chain goes all the way to the effect on consumers, which is the focus of the question.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

3.7 Firms’ costs, revenue and objectives

These questions test the definitions and formulas for costs and revenue, how fixed and average costs behave as output changes, and why firms do not always aim to maximise profit; examiners reward precise formulas and a clear distinction between total and average figures.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports say weaker answers drew total fixed cost rising with output or confused average fixed cost with average variable cost. When asked why a loss-making firm carries on, “to pay its workers” or “to meet customer demand” earns nothing, because these do not stop the losses.
0455/23/O/N/24 Q1(a) · October/November 2024 · [1 mark]

Source material: Number of cars produced 32 000 Total cost of cars produced $340.8m

Calculate the average cost of producing a car in Belarus in 2021.

Average cost = total cost ÷ output = $340 800 000 ÷ 32 000 = $10 650 per car.

(On average, each car cost $10 650 to produce in 2021.)

Why this scores
The single mark is for $10 650 (or $0.01065m). Write the formula first and convert $340.8m into $340 800 000 before dividing, otherwise it is easy to give 0.01065 with no units and lose the mark.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/25 Q3(a) · October/November 2025 · [2 marks]

Define average total cost.

Average total cost is total cost divided by the quantity of output, that is, the cost of producing each unit. For example, if a firm’s total cost is $5 000 and it produces 1 000 bottles of perfume, its average total cost is $5 per bottle.

Why this scores
The mark scheme gives both marks for “total cost divided by output”, and only one for the vaguer “cost per unit”, so state the formula in words. The short example is not required but shows you understand it.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/21 Q5(b) · October/November 2021 · [4 marks]

Source material: Many markets are becoming more competitive, causing some firms to make losses.

Explain two reasons why a loss-making firm may continue to produce.

It expects the loss to be temporary. The firm may be making a loss because of a recession or because it is a new firm still building up its sales. If it expects demand to rise or its costs to fall in the future, it will keep producing because it expects to return to profit in the long run.

It can cover the loss. The firm may have large retained profits from earlier, more successful years, which it can use to pay its costs while it is making a loss. Alternatively, the government may subsidise it, for example because it produces a merit good or employs many workers, so the subsidy covers the gap between its revenue and its costs.

Why this scores
One mark for each reason identified and one for each explanation. The examiner report praised answers based on subsidies and expected future profit, and gave no credit for “to pay its workers” or “to meet customer demand”, which would not solve the losses.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/22 Q3(c) · February/March 2022 · [6 marks]

Analyse, using a diagram, the effect of an increase in output on average fixed cost (AFC) and total fixed cost (TFC).

Fixed costs are costs that do not change with the level of output in the short run, such as rent, insurance and loan repayments. They have to be paid even if the firm produces nothing.

Total fixed cost. Because fixed costs do not depend on output, an increase in output has no effect on total fixed cost. If a firm pays $10 000 a month in rent, it pays $10 000 whether it makes 100 units or 1 000 units. TFC is therefore shown as a horizontal line in the diagram.

Average fixed cost. AFC is total fixed cost divided by output. As output rises, the same total fixed cost is spread over more units, so AFC falls continuously. With rent of $10 000, AFC is $100 per unit at an output of 100 but only $10 per unit at an output of 1 000. In the diagram, an increase in output from Q1 to Q2 causes AFC to fall from AFC1 to AFC2.

This is why firms with high fixed costs try to increase output: spreading fixed costs more thinly lowers their average total cost and allows them to charge lower prices or earn more profit on each unit. In the long run, however, all costs can change, for example if the firm moves to bigger premises, which would shift TFC upwards.

Why this scores
The mark scheme gives up to 4 marks for the diagram (axes labelled costs and output, a horizontal TFC, a downward-sloping AFC, both curves labelled) and 2 for the written analysis that TFC does not change and AFC falls because TFC is divided by a larger output. The examiner report says some candidates drew TFC rising, or confused AFC with average variable cost.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/23 Q2(d) · February/March 2023 · [8 marks]

Source material: Some of their firms have grown and now compete with foreign multinational companies (MNCs) and public sector firms.

Discuss whether or not private sector firms are likely to charge lower prices than public sector firms.

Private sector firms are owned by individuals or shareholders and usually aim to maximise profit. Public sector firms are owned by the government and often aim to provide an affordable service and to increase social welfare.

Why private sector firms might charge lower prices. Most private sector firms face competition from rivals. To attract customers and avoid losing market share, they must keep their prices low. They also have a strong incentive to be efficient: if they waste resources their costs rise, they make losses and they may go out of business. Lower costs allow them to charge lower prices than a public sector firm that has no fear of closing down.

Large private sector firms, such as MNCs, may also produce on a very large scale and gain economies of scale, such as bulk buying and advanced technology. They may have more funds to invest in new machinery than a government-owned firm, which lowers their average cost and allows them to cut prices.

Why private sector firms might not charge lower prices. The main objective of a private sector firm is usually profit, so if it can charge a high price without losing many customers, it will. This is most likely if it is a monopoly with no close rivals, or if demand for its product is price-inelastic, for example water or electricity.

Public sector firms, by contrast, may deliberately charge low prices to make essential services affordable for poorer households, for example public transport. They may be subsidised by the government, so they can charge a price below their cost of production. A public sector firm may also be very large, such as a national electricity company, and gain its own economies of scale.

Conclusion. Private sector firms are likely to charge lower prices than public sector firms when they face strong competition, because competition forces them to keep costs and prices down. However, where a private firm is a monopoly selling an essential product, it is likely to charge more than a subsidised public sector firm whose objective is affordability rather than profit. The answer therefore depends mainly on the level of competition in the market and on whether the public sector firm is subsidised.

Why this scores
This reaches Level 3 by explaining how each firm’s objective and situation affects its price, rather than only stating the objectives. The examiner report said many candidates recognised the different motives but did not go on to explain the effect on prices, and that stronger answers explored competition, funds available and the size of firms, as this answer does.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

3.8 Market structure

These questions test the features of competitive markets and monopoly and how each affects consumers through price, quality, choice and efficiency; examiners reward explained chains (why a monopoly might have lower costs, or why competition pushes prices down), not bare assertions.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports say weaker answers state that one firm “may charge a lower price” without explaining why (economies of scale), are one-sided against monopolies, or write about perfect competition, which is not on the syllabus. Stay with the idea of a competitive market with many firms and few barriers to entry.
0455/22/O/N/21 Q1(c) · October/November 2021 · [2 marks]

Source material: share of India’s international and national air travel market in 1994 100%

Explain one characteristic that suggests that Air India was a monopoly in 1994.

In 1994 Air India had 100% of India’s air travel market, so it was the only firm supplying air travel in the country, a pure monopoly. With no competing airline for passengers to switch to, Air India faced no effective competition.

Why this scores
One mark for using the evidence (a 100% market share) and one for explaining what it means: the only firm, with no competition. The examiner report says most candidates found the figure but only some went on to explain it, which the command word “Explain” requires. Being able to set price (a price maker) would also be credited.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/24 Q2(b) · October/November 2024 · [4 marks]

Source material: Some of the country’s firms operate in competitive markets but some are monopolies.

Explain two characteristics of monopoly.

High barriers to entry. A monopoly is protected by barriers that make it difficult for new firms to enter the industry, such as the very high cost of setting up, patents or legal rights granted by the government. As a result, it can keep its position as the single or dominant supplier and continue to earn high profits in the long run.

It is a price maker. Because consumers have no close substitutes to switch to, the monopoly controls the supply of the product and can set its own price. It can restrict output to push the price up, and consumers have little choice but to pay it, so demand for its product is relatively price-inelastic.

Why this scores
One mark for each characteristic identified and one for each explanation. Other creditworthy characteristics include being the only firm or having a dominant market share, a unique product with no close substitutes, and large size giving economies of scale.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/24 Q5(c) · October/November 2024 · [6 marks]

Source material: There are many firms in the Peruvian tourism industry.

Analyse the benefits consumers may gain from a competitive market.

A competitive market is one with many firms selling similar products and low barriers to entry, so consumers have many close substitutes to choose from.

Lower prices. Because consumers can easily switch to a rival, each firm must keep its prices low to attract customers and win market share. If one Peruvian tour operator raised its prices, its customers would go to another. Lower prices increase consumers’ purchasing power, so they can buy more with the same income.

Higher quality and innovation. To stand out from rivals, firms try to improve their products, for example better hotels, guides or booking websites. Competition also pressures firms to be efficient and to cut waste, because inefficient firms with high costs are driven out of the market. Consumers therefore receive better products at lower cost.

More choice. With many firms, consumers can choose between a wide variety of products, from budget backpacker tours to luxury holidays, so they are more likely to find one that matches their tastes and income.

Responsiveness to consumers. Firms in a competitive market must respond quickly to changes in what consumers want, or lose sales to rivals that do. Resources are therefore allocated according to consumer demand, which is known as consumer sovereignty.

Why this scores
Defines a competitive market, then analyses four separate benefits, each with a chain from many firms → firm behaviour → gain for consumers, applied to the tourism context. The mark scheme credits exactly these: low prices, quality and innovation, choice and responsiveness to demand.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/25 Q3(d) · May/June 2025 · [8 marks]

Source material: There are several firms in the industry, but the number is declining. There is a possibility that soon there may be just one firm in the industry.

Discuss whether or not consumers would benefit from only one firm producing shampoo.

If only one firm produced shampoo, it would be a monopoly: the single supplier of a product, protected by barriers to entry.

Why consumers might benefit. A single firm would supply the whole market, so it would produce on a very large scale. It could buy ingredients and bottles in bulk at a discount and use the most advanced, automated production lines. These economies of scale would lower its average cost. If the firm passed some of these savings on, consumers could pay a lower price than when several smaller firms each had higher costs.

A monopoly may also earn high profits and feel secure that rivals cannot copy its ideas. It can therefore afford to spend heavily on research and development, producing better shampoos, for example for different hair types, and maintaining a high quality. Consumers would also save time, as they would not need to compare many brands.

Why consumers might not benefit. With no competition, consumers cannot switch to another supplier, so demand for the firm’s shampoo becomes more price-inelastic. A profit-maximising monopoly can restrict supply and raise its price without losing many sales, so consumers pay more and lose purchasing power.

Consumers would also have less choice: the firm may offer only a few types of shampoo. With no rivals to fear, it has less incentive to keep quality high or to be efficient, and if its quality falls, consumers have no alternative. A very large firm may also suffer diseconomies of scale, such as poor communication, which raise its average cost and its prices.

Conclusion. Consumers are more likely to lose than gain from only one shampoo producer, because without competition the firm has the power and the incentive to raise prices and reduce choice. They could benefit if the firm gains large economies of scale and passes them on as lower prices, but that is most likely only if the government regulates its prices or if the threat of imported shampoo keeps it efficient.

Why this scores
This is a Level 3 answer: it defines monopoly and then explains why a single firm might have lower costs or higher prices, using economies of scale, R&D and price elasticity. The examiner report criticised answers that claimed one firm would charge a lower price without explaining why, and those that said pollution would fall, since pollution depends on total output and methods, not the number of firms.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/20 Q3(d) · October/November 2020 · [8 marks]

Source material: Privatisation can reduce monopoly power in a market.

Discuss whether or not a government should allow monopolies.

A monopoly is a single firm that supplies the whole of a market, or has a dominant share of it, and is protected by barriers to entry.

Why a government should allow monopolies. A monopoly produces on a very large scale, so it can gain economies of scale, such as bulk-buying discounts and specialised machinery. Its average cost may therefore be lower than if many small firms shared the market, and it can charge lower prices. In industries such as water supply or railways, having several firms each building their own pipes or tracks would waste resources, so one firm is more efficient.

Monopolies often earn high profits, which they can reinvest in research and development. This can lead to new, better-quality products and more efficient methods of production. A large monopoly may also be able to compete with big foreign firms in international markets, increasing the country’s exports. A state-owned monopoly may even have social welfare objectives and keep its prices low.

Why a government should not allow monopolies. A private monopoly with no rivals can abuse its market power. Since consumers have no alternative, it can restrict output and raise its price to maximise profit, so consumers pay more and buy less. This is a form of market failure because resources are not allocated in the way consumers would choose.

Without the pressure of competition, a monopoly may also become complacent. It has little incentive to cut costs, improve quality or offer choice, so costs may rise and products may be poorer. The high profits go to the owners at the expense of consumers, which can widen income inequality, especially if the product is a necessity such as electricity.

Conclusion. A government should allow monopolies in industries where large-scale production gives major economies of scale or where competition would waste resources, such as water supply, but only if it regulates their prices and quality. Where a monopoly is likely to raise prices and become inefficient, the government should prevent it or open the market to competition, for example through privatisation and allowing new firms to enter. The decision depends on whether the cost savings from size are large enough to outweigh the risk of abuse of market power.

Why this scores
Level 3 requires both sides to be developed; the examiner report noted that weaker answers were stronger on not allowing monopolies, while strong answers also evaluated the benefits (better quality, funds for research and development, competing with foreign firms). This answer gives both sides equal depth and ends with a conditional judgement about regulation and economies of scale.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
