Topical Past Papers · Topic 4

Past Paper Questions: Government and the Macroeconomy

38 real Cambridge IGCSE Economics Paper 2 questions from 2020 to 2025, sorted by sub-topic, each with its exact paper reference and a full-mark exemplar answer. The questions are quoted exactly; the model answers are my own, written to the published mark schemes and the advice in the examiner reports.

How to use this page
Cover the answer, write your own in timed conditions (about 1½ minutes per mark), then compare. 0455/22/M/J/25 Q2(d) means Paper 2, variant 2, May/June 2025, Question 2 part (d). F/M = February/March, M/J = May/June, O/N = October/November.

Jump to: 4.1 The role of government · 4.2 The macroeconomic aims of government · 4.3 Fiscal policy · 4.4 Monetary policy · 4.5 Supply-side policy · 4.6 Economic growth · 4.7 Employment and unemployment · 4.8 Inflation and deflation

4.1 The role of government

These questions test what governments do locally, nationally and internationally, and why they step into markets; examiners reward answers that link each role to a specific problem the market alone would not solve.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports note that many candidates correctly state that the public sector aims for social welfare and the private sector for profit, then stop. Stronger answers go on to explain how that different motive changes what is produced and the prices charged.
0455/23/M/J/20 Q2(a) · May/June 2020 · [2 marks]

Source material: The local government not only offers housing at subsidised rates, to get more labour into the area, but also tries to get more investment into the town.

State two functions of local government.

1. Collecting and disposing of household waste.

2. Providing and maintaining local roads and street lighting.

Why this scores
One mark for each correct function. “State” needs only the function, so no explanation is required. Make sure each is a local service: national functions such as defence or running the central bank would not be credited. Other creditworthy answers include local parks and recreation, low-income housing (as in Nagicho), zoning land for building and supporting local businesses.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/24 Q4(b) · October/November 2024 · [4 marks]

Source material: Davos is a ski resort in Switzerland famous for hosting a world economic conference for leaders from both private and public sectors.

Explain the difference between the private sector and the public sector.

Ownership. The private sector is the part of the economy owned and run by individuals and firms, such as sole traders, partnerships and companies, for example a hotel in Davos. The public sector is the part of the economy owned and run by the government, at local or national level, such as state schools, public hospitals and state-owned enterprises.

Main aim. Private sector firms are mainly motivated by profit, so they decide what to produce and what price to charge according to what earns them the most income. The public sector aims to increase the welfare of society as a whole, so it may provide goods and services free or at a low price, such as healthcare or street lighting, and takes account of external costs and benefits that a private firm would ignore.

Why this scores
The mark scheme gives 2 marks for a developed point about each sector: who owns and runs it (1), and the basis on which decisions are made, profit versus social welfare (1). A difference must cover both sectors in the same point, so the answer compares them directly rather than describing each one separately.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/24 Q4(c) · October/November 2024 · [6 marks]

Source material: For example, in 2018 India imposed a tariff of approximately 90% on imports of coffee to protect its coffee farmers.

Analyse possible reasons why a government may want to intervene in a market.

A government may intervene in a market when the market mechanism leads to market failure, an inefficient allocation of resources, so that the price system alone produces the wrong goods, in the wrong quantities, for the wrong people.

To provide public goods. Goods such as street lighting and defence are non-excludable and non-rival, so people can enjoy them without paying. This free rider problem means private firms cannot make a profit from them, so the market will not supply them at all. The government therefore provides them itself, financed by taxation.

To correct external costs and benefits. Consumers and producers ignore external effects when they decide how much to buy or sell. Merit goods such as vaccinations have external benefits, so they are under-consumed; the government may subsidise them or provide them free so that consumption rises towards the socially efficient level. Demerit goods such as cigarettes have external costs, so they are over-consumed; an indirect tax raises their price and reduces the quantity bought.

To limit monopoly power. A firm with monopoly power can restrict output and charge high prices, reducing consumer choice and quality. Competition policy and regulation can stop harmful mergers or cap prices, so that consumers pay lower prices.

To protect producers and workers. A government may impose tariffs, as India did with a 90% tariff on coffee imports, to protect domestic farmers from cheaper foreign competition. This keeps domestic firms in business and protects jobs, and it may give an infant industry time to grow until it can compete. Similarly, a national minimum wage raises the pay of low-paid workers above the market wage.

Why this scores
The answer opens by linking intervention to market failure, which earns a mark, then develops separate reasons into full chains: the problem the market creates → why it happens → how intervention fixes it. The mark scheme caps identification at 3 marks, so the other marks come from this development. Using the coffee tariff from the stem shows application.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/23 Q2(d) · October/November 2023 · [8 marks]

Source material: Many blamed strict government regulations for this. However, government intervention also helped many firms to survive.

Discuss whether or not government intervention is beneficial for firms.

Government intervention is when the government acts to influence what happens in markets, for example through taxes, subsidies, regulations, price controls and its own spending.

Why it might be beneficial for firms. Subsidies lower firms’ costs of production. This lets them cut prices or keep them steady, sell more, and stay in business during difficult times; for example, government support during the pandemic helped many Alpine tourism firms to survive a winter with very low revenue. Lower rates of corporation tax have a similar effect: firms keep more profit after tax, which they can use to invest in new capital and expand.

Government spending can also raise demand and productivity. Spending on education and training increases the skills of the labour force, so firms’ workers become more productive and average costs fall. Spending on infrastructure, such as roads and rail links to ski resorts, cuts transport costs and makes it easier for customers to reach the firms. In addition, competition regulation stops large firms abusing monopoly power, which helps smaller firms to enter and survive in the market.

Why it might not be beneficial for firms. Regulations often raise costs. Firms may have to meet health and safety rules, pollution limits or, as in the pandemic, restrictions that forced resorts to close or limit customer numbers. Revenue falls while some costs remain, so profits fall and some firms may close. A national minimum wage above the market wage raises labour costs for firms such as hotels that employ many low-paid workers, reducing their profit margins or leading them to employ fewer staff.

Taxes and price controls can also harm firms. An increase in indirect tax raises the price consumers pay, so quantity demanded falls and the firm’s revenue falls. A maximum price set below the equilibrium price cuts firms’ revenue per unit and may make production unprofitable. Even subsidies can harm firms in the long run if they remove the incentive to become efficient.

Conclusion. Government intervention is likely to benefit firms when it lowers their costs or raises demand, as subsidies, training and infrastructure spending do, and especially in a crisis such as the pandemic when firms might otherwise collapse. It is less likely to benefit them when it mainly adds costs, as strict regulations and minimum wages do. Overall, the effect depends on the type of intervention, which firms are affected (a protected domestic firm gains, while its rivals and suppliers may lose) and whether the intervention is temporary support or a permanent cost.

Why this scores
This fits the Level 3 description: a definition, developed arguments on both sides covering several mark-scheme points (subsidies, tax cuts, training, regulation of monopolies; costs of regulation, minimum wage, taxes, maximum prices) and a conclusion that judges which types of intervention help. Each point ends with its effect on firms’ costs, revenue or profit, which keeps the answer focused on the question.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

4.2 The macroeconomic aims of government

These questions test the government’s macroeconomic aims and why achieving one can make another harder; examiners reward precise statements of each aim and a full chain showing how a change affects each one.

What examiners keep seeing
Examiners report that weaker answers name aims too vaguely, writing “unemployment” or “inflation” instead of low unemployment or stable prices, or give things that are not aims at all, such as higher total demand.
0455/21/M/J/25 Q4(a) · May/June 2025 · [2 marks]

Source material: In 2023, India became the country with the largest population. This may affect the government’s macroeconomic aims.

Identify two macroeconomic aims of government that may conflict.

Low unemployment and stable prices (low inflation).

Why this scores
One mark for each aim. The two aims must be stated precisely as aims: the examiner report notes that just writing “unemployment” or “inflation” was too vague, and that some candidates wasted time giving two pairs. Other creditworthy pairs include economic growth and balance of payments stability, and economic growth and environmental sustainability.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/22 Q1(e) · May/June 2022 · [4 marks]

Source material: The aim is for the eSports industry to contribute 1% to Malta’s GDP, provide 3000 more jobs and improve the current account of its balance of payments.

Analyse how the development of eSports in Malta can help the government achieve its macroeconomic aims.

Economic growth. Malta’s service sector already contributes about 90% of its GDP, and eSports is another service industry. If the government’s plan succeeds and eSports contributes 1% of GDP, Malta’s output will rise, so real GDP grows. Investment by sponsors and in infrastructure for events will also add to total demand and boost output further.

Low unemployment and balance of payments stability. The plan aims to create 3000 more jobs, for example for professional players, trainers, event staff and workers in hotels and restaurants, which reduces unemployment. The eSports events are also designed to attract foreign visitors. When they spend money in Malta, this counts as an export of services, which brings money into the country and improves the current account of the balance of payments.

Why this scores
The mark scheme gives marks for each aim identified and for analysing how eSports helps achieve it. The examiner report praised answers that first named the aims and then used the 1% of GDP and 3000 jobs from the source, exactly as this answer does. Linking tourism spending to exports of services is the step weaker answers missed.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/24 Q3(c) · October/November 2024 · [6 marks]

Source material: In 2020, Madagascar had an economic growth rate of 6% and a deficit on the current account of its balance of payments of $0.6bn.

Analyse how the macroeconomic aims of economic growth and balance of payments stability may conflict.

Economic growth is an increase in a country’s real GDP. Balance of payments stability means avoiding a large, persistent deficit or surplus on the current account. Achieving one aim can make the other harder to achieve.

Higher incomes raise imports. When the economy grows, more workers are employed and wages rise. Households have more disposable income, so they spend more, and part of this extra spending goes on imported goods such as cars and electronics. Higher import spending worsens the current account, which may explain why Madagascar had a current account deficit of $0.6bn while growing at 6% in 2020.

Firms need more imported inputs. To increase output, firms buy more raw materials, components and capital goods. If these are not produced at home, they must be imported, which again raises import spending and worsens the current account deficit.

Exports fall. Strong domestic demand means firms can sell more at home, so they may switch some output from export markets to the domestic market. In addition, fast growth may cause demand-pull inflation. Higher domestic prices make the country’s exports less price competitive and imports relatively cheaper, so export revenue falls and imports rise further.

The conflict is not certain, however. If growth is export-led, for example because of higher sales of a country’s products abroad, both output and export revenue rise together, and the current account may improve.

Why this scores
Opens by defining both aims, then gives three separate chains, each ending in the effect on imports, exports and the current account, which is what the mark scheme credits. The data from the stem is applied, and the export-led growth point shows the conflict is not inevitable, a point the mark scheme also accepts.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/21 Q2(d) · February/March 2021 · [8 marks]

Source material: The government is concerned that trying to reduce unemployment may increase India’s inflation rate.

Discuss whether or not a government can reduce unemployment without increasing inflation.

Unemployment is when people in the labour force who are willing and able to work cannot find a job. Inflation is a sustained rise in the general price level over time.

Why it may be able to. If the government uses supply-side policy, such as spending more on education and training, workers become more skilled and productive. This makes it easier for unemployed people to find jobs, and higher productivity lowers firms’ costs per unit, so they can expand output without raising prices. Total (aggregate) supply rises alongside demand, so unemployment falls without inflation.

The state of the economy also matters. If there is a recession with many unemployed workers and spare capacity, firms can increase output by taking on unemployed workers who are willing to accept the current wage. A rise in total demand caused by more government spending then mainly raises output and employment rather than prices. Subsidies to firms can also help: they lower costs, so firms can take on more workers and keep prices down.

Why it may not be able to. Expansionary fiscal or monetary policy, such as cutting income tax or lowering interest rates, raises disposable income and spending, so total demand rises. If the economy is already close to full employment, firms cannot easily increase output, so the extra demand pulls up prices: demand-pull inflation.

As unemployment falls, firms also compete for a smaller pool of workers, and trade unions are in a stronger position to bargain for higher wages. Higher wages raise firms’ costs of production, and firms pass these on as higher prices, causing cost-push inflation. In addition, supply-side policies take a long time to work, so in the short run the government may have to rely on demand-side policies that are more likely to be inflationary.

Conclusion. A government can reduce unemployment without increasing inflation if the economy has a lot of spare capacity, as in a recession, or if it uses supply-side measures that raise total supply as well as demand. It is much harder when the economy is close to full employment and the government uses expansionary demand-side policy, since rising demand and wages are then likely to push up prices. For India, with growth already high, success depends on whether the government focuses on training and productivity rather than simply boosting spending.

Why this scores
This is a Level 3 answer: both terms defined, two developed arguments on each side, and a conclusion that depends on the level of spare capacity and the type of policy. The examiner report singled out answers that contrasted demand-side policy (risk of demand-pull and cost-push inflation) with supply-side policy, and warned that weaker answers skipped stages in the chain.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/M/J/22 Q2(d) · May/June 2022 · [8 marks]

Source material: In 2019, the government of Papua New Guinea increased income tax to reduce its inflation rate. It used other policy measures to increase its economic growth rate.

Discuss whether or not governments should aim for a high rate of economic growth.

Economic growth is an increase in a country’s real GDP, that is, a rise in the output of goods and services over time.

Why a government should aim for high growth. Higher output means higher incomes on average. Households can afford more and better goods and services, such as better housing, food and healthcare, so material living standards rise. As firms produce more, they also need more workers, so unemployment falls, and fewer people live in poverty.

Growth also increases tax revenue without the government having to raise tax rates, because incomes, spending and profits all rise. The government can then spend more on education, healthcare and infrastructure, which raises living standards further and can increase the economy’s productive capacity, helping future growth.

Why a government should not aim for a high rate. Very rapid growth can harm the environment. Producing more output uses up non-renewable resources such as oil and minerals, and it often increases pollution and carbon emissions. This lowers the quality of life and can reduce the economy’s ability to grow in the future, so the growth is not sustainable.

High growth can also conflict with other macroeconomic aims. If total demand rises faster than the economy’s capacity, firms raise prices and inflation increases, which is why Papua New Guinea had to raise income tax to control inflation while trying to grow. Higher incomes also lead to more spending on imports, which can create a current account deficit. In addition, the benefits of growth are often unevenly shared, so income inequality may widen, and some workers may face longer hours and more stress.

Conclusion. Governments should aim for economic growth, especially low-income countries where higher output is needed to reduce poverty and raise living standards. However, a high rate is only desirable if it is sustainable: if it is driven by rising productivity and investment rather than by excess demand, and if the government protects the environment and spreads the benefits through its tax and spending policies. A steady, moderate rate is usually better for a high-income country that is already close to full capacity.

Why this scores
Fits Level 3: definition, two developed arguments on each side and a conclusion that distinguishes low- and high-income countries and names the conditions (sustainability, productivity, distribution). The examiner report said answers were often stronger on the benefits of growth, and weak ones listed points without developing them; here every drawback is explained, not just named.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

4.3 Fiscal policy

These questions test the government budget, taxation and government spending; examiners reward answers that name the specific tax or spending change and trace its effect through total demand, costs or incentives to the outcome asked about.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports repeatedly mention candidates confusing a government budget deficit (spending greater than tax revenue) with a deficit on the current account of the balance of payments, and so answering a different question.
0455/22/M/J/25 Q1(a) · May/June 2025 · [1 mark]

Source material: Government spending $310m Tax revenue $292m

Calculate St. Kitts and Nevis’s government budget deficit in 2022.

Budget deficit = government spending − tax revenue = $310m − $292m = $18m

(The government spent $18m more than it received in tax revenue in 2022.)

Why this scores
The mark is for $18m (a minus sign is accepted). The examiner report notes that some candidates subtracted wrongly ($28m), dropped the units by writing $18 instead of $18m, or gave the current account deficit of $56m instead, confusing the two kinds of deficit.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/23 Q5(a) · May/June 2023 · [2 marks]

Define government budget.

A government budget is the government’s plan, usually for the coming year, of its expected revenue (mainly from taxation) and its planned expenditure.

Why this scores
The mark scheme awards marks for “a plan or forecast”, “government expenditure” and “government revenue”, up to 2. The examiner report says the biggest weakness was mentioning only spending or only revenue: you need both, and calling it a plan secures the marks.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/22 Q3(b) · October/November 2022 · [4 marks]

Source material: In both 2018 and 2019, the government did not change its income tax rate. Russia operates a proportional tax rate system.

Explain the difference between a proportional income tax system and a progressive income tax system.

Proportional income tax. Everyone pays the same percentage of their income in tax, whatever their income. For example, with a 13% rate a person earning $10,000 pays $1,300 and a person earning $100,000 pays $13,000. The rich pay more in total, but the share of income taken stays the same, so the system does not change the distribution of income.

Progressive income tax. The rate of tax rises as income rises, so higher earners pay a larger percentage of their income in tax. For example, income up to $20,000 might be taxed at 10% and income above that at 40%. This places a heavier burden on the rich and reduces the gap between rich and poor after tax, so it redistributes income.

Why this scores
The mark scheme allows up to 3 marks for each system: same percentage for all income groups and no effect on distribution (proportional); the rate rises with income, a larger percentage taken from the rich, and redistribution (progressive). The examiner report says the key is the percentage of income, since the rich pay more in total under both systems, and warns against confusing progressive with regressive taxes.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/24 Q3(c) · February/March 2024 · [6 marks]

Source material: Workers in Monaco benefit from fiscal policy which contributes to high employment.

Analyse how fiscal policy can increase employment.

Fiscal policy is the use of government spending and taxation to influence the economy. To increase employment a government uses expansionary fiscal policy: higher government spending and/or lower taxes.

Raising total demand. If the government increases its spending, for example on building schools and hospitals, or cuts income tax so that households have more disposable income, total (aggregate) demand rises. Firms see their sales increase, so they expand output, and because labour is a derived demand they need more workers. This reduces cyclical unemployment.

Cutting taxes on firms. A cut in corporation tax means firms keep more of their profit after tax. They have both more funds and a stronger incentive to invest in expansion, and new or larger factories and offices need more workers. Lower taxes may also attract multinational companies to set up in the country, creating further jobs.

Improving the quality and mobility of labour. Government spending on education and training raises workers’ skills and productivity. Unemployed workers become more attractive to employers and can move into industries where there are vacancies, which reduces structural unemployment. Spending on affordable housing in areas with job vacancies also makes it easier for workers to move to where the jobs are.

Incentives to work. A cut in income tax increases the reward from working, while a cut in unemployment benefit reduces the income people receive when not working. Both increase the incentive for unemployed people to search for and accept jobs, so employment rises.

Why this scores
Defines fiscal policy, then gives four separate chains, each running from a specific fiscal measure to more jobs, matching the mark-scheme points (higher total demand, corporation tax, training, housing and work incentives). Naming the type of unemployment reduced, cyclical or structural, shows precise analysis.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/M/J/25 Q4(d) · May/June 2025 · [8 marks]

Source material: Some economists suggested that to achieve its central inflation rate target, the government should cut its spending.

Discuss whether or not a decrease in government spending will reduce inflation.

Inflation is a sustained rise in the general price level. A decrease in government spending is a form of contractionary fiscal policy.

Why it might reduce inflation. Government spending is a component of total (aggregate) demand. If the government cuts its spending, for example on welfare benefits, households have less income to spend, so consumer expenditure falls. With lower total demand, firms face less pressure to raise prices, so demand-pull inflation falls. This works best when the economy is close to full employment, which is when demand-pull inflation is most likely.

Lower government spending may also reduce the need for taxes. If the government can then cut indirect taxes, such as VAT, firms’ prices fall directly, lowering the inflation rate. For India, where inflation averaged 6% between 2019 and 2022, a fall in demand could help bring inflation back towards the central bank’s target.

Why it might not reduce inflation. The effect depends on which spending is cut. If the government cuts subsidies, firms’ costs of production rise. They pass the higher costs on to consumers through higher prices, which causes cost-push inflation. Cutting spending on infrastructure, such as roads and ports, can also raise firms’ transport costs and push prices up.

In the long run, cuts in spending on education and training reduce the skills and productivity of the labour force. Average costs rise and the economy’s productive capacity grows more slowly, so total supply cannot keep up with demand, which adds to inflation. Finally, if inflation is caused by something the government spending cut does not affect, such as rising world oil prices or wage increases, the cut will have little effect on the price level.

Conclusion. A decrease in government spending is likely to reduce inflation if the inflation is demand-pull and the cuts fall on spending that boosts demand, such as transfer payments. It is less likely to work if inflation is cost-push, or if the cuts fall on subsidies and infrastructure that keep firms’ costs down. A government should therefore first identify the cause of inflation and then choose carefully which spending to cut.

Why this scores
Matches Level 3: definition, two developed arguments each way and a conclusion that depends on the cause of inflation and the type of spending cut. The examiner report praised answers that took individual components of government spending and linked each one to demand-pull or cost-push inflation, and warned against drifting onto growth or unemployment, or writing about an increase in spending.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/25 Q4(d) · October/November 2025 · [8 marks]

Source material: Lanzarote’s low taxes also attract workers to the island.

Discuss whether or not low taxes are beneficial for an economy.

Taxes are compulsory payments to the government. They can be direct taxes on income and profits, such as income tax and corporation tax, or indirect taxes on spending, such as VAT.

Why low taxes might be beneficial. Low income tax leaves households with more disposable income, so consumer spending rises and total (aggregate) demand increases. Firms respond by increasing output and employing more workers, which raises economic growth and cuts unemployment. Low income tax also increases the reward from working, encouraging people to work longer hours and attracting workers from abroad, as Lanzarote’s low taxes attract workers to the island. This can prevent a “brain drain” of skilled workers.

Low corporation tax increases firms’ profits after tax, giving them funds and an incentive to invest in new capital. More investment raises productive capacity and productivity in the long run. Low taxes on firms can also attract multinational companies to set up in the country, bringing jobs, skills and export earnings. Low indirect taxes reduce the prices of goods and services, which lowers the cost of living and inflation.

Why low taxes might not be beneficial. Low tax rates mean the government collects less tax revenue. It then has less to spend on merit goods and public goods such as education, healthcare, roads and defence, or it must borrow more and run a budget deficit. Lower spending on education and infrastructure may reduce productivity and growth in the long run, so the benefits of lower taxes may be cancelled out.

Low taxes can also increase inequality: with low income tax rates, especially for the rich, the tax system does less to redistribute income, so the gap between rich and poor may widen. Low indirect taxes on demerit goods such as cigarettes and alcohol make them cheaper, so consumption rises and external costs, such as healthcare costs and pollution, increase. Finally, if the economy is already at full employment, the extra spending from tax cuts may cause demand-pull inflation rather than higher output.

Conclusion. Low taxes are likely to benefit an economy by encouraging work, investment and foreign firms, particularly in a small economy such as Lanzarote that is competing to attract workers and businesses. They are less beneficial if they leave the government unable to fund essential public services, or if they are applied to demerit goods. On balance, low taxes help most when the economy has spare capacity and the government can still raise enough revenue, for example because a growing tax base makes up for the lower rates.

Why this scores
Level 3: a definition, two developed arguments on each side using several mark-scheme points (disposable income, corporation tax and investment, MNCs, brain drain; lost revenue, inequality, demerit goods, inflation) and a conclusion that names specific conditions. The stem’s point about Lanzarote attracting workers is applied rather than simply repeated.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

4.4 Monetary policy

These questions test how central banks use the interest rate, the money supply and the exchange rate; examiners reward answers that follow each change through borrowing, saving and spending to total demand and inflation.

What examiners keep seeing
Examiners report that a high proportion of candidates think the central bank carries out fiscal policy, and others get the direction of interest-rate effects wrong. Monetary policy works through the interest rate, the money supply and the exchange rate, not taxes and government spending.
0455/23/M/J/22 Q5(a) · May/June 2022 · [2 marks]

Source material: Since he came to power, he has focused on using supply-side policy measures, rather than monetary policy.

Define monetary policy.

Monetary policy is the use of changes in the money supply, the rate of interest and the foreign exchange rate, usually by the central bank, to influence total demand in the economy, for example to control inflation.

Why this scores
The mark scheme gives marks for naming the instruments (money supply, interest rates or the exchange rate) and for saying what they affect (total demand or inflation). The examiner report found a clear divide between candidates who knew these instruments and those who did not, so always name at least two of them.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/25 Q5(b) · October/November 2025 · [4 marks]

Source material: It uses fiscal, monetary and supply-side policies to increase its economic growth rate.

Explain two differences between monetary policy and supply-side policy.

Who uses them and what they target. Monetary policy is usually carried out by the central bank, which changes the interest rate and the money supply to influence total (aggregate) demand. Supply-side policy is carried out by the government and aims to increase total (aggregate) supply, the economy’s productive capacity, for example through education and training or privatisation.

Direction and speed. Monetary policy can be used either to expand or to slow down economic activity: a cut in the interest rate boosts spending, while a rise reduces it to control inflation. It can also take effect fairly quickly. Supply-side policy is only used to increase the economy’s capacity, and it usually takes a long time to work, since it may be years before better-trained workers raise productivity.

Why this scores
Each difference is made by comparing both policies in the same point, which earns 1 mark for each side. The mark scheme credits who implements each policy, demand versus supply, expanding or reducing activity versus only expanding, and short-term versus long-term impact. Contrasting examples of instruments (interest rate versus training) can also earn credit.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/21 Q4(c) · October/November 2021 · [6 marks]

Source material: The central bank considered increasing the interest rate to reduce the inflation rate. An increase in interest rates might influence total demand in an economy and lead to an appreciation of its currency.

Analyse how an increase in the interest rate could reduce total demand in an economy.

Total (aggregate) demand is the total spending on a country’s goods and services: consumer spending, investment, government spending and net exports. A rise in the interest rate can reduce several of these.

Borrowing becomes more expensive. When the central bank raises the interest rate, commercial banks charge more on loans, mortgages and credit cards. Households borrow less to buy expensive items such as cars and furniture, and people with mortgages have higher repayments, leaving them less disposable income. Consumer spending therefore falls. Firms also face a higher cost of borrowing for investment, so fewer projects are profitable and investment in capital goods falls.

Saving becomes more rewarding. A higher interest rate raises the return on savings, so households have an incentive to save more of their income and spend less of it. This further reduces consumer spending.

The currency appreciates. Higher interest rates attract money from abroad into the country’s banks. To deposit money in Uruguay, foreigners must buy pesos, so demand for the currency rises and it appreciates. Uruguay’s exports, such as soybeans, become more expensive in foreign currencies, while imports become cheaper. Exports fall and imports rise, so net exports fall.

As consumer spending, investment and net exports all fall, total demand in the economy falls, which is why the central bank hoped the rise would reduce Uruguay’s 8.4% inflation rate.

Why this scores
Defines total demand, then builds three complete chains, through borrowing, saving and the exchange rate, each ending with a fall in a named component of total demand, exactly as the mark scheme sets out. The examiner report said the strongest answers went beyond borrowing to cover saving, investment and the appreciation of the currency.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/24 Q5(d) · October/November 2024 · [8 marks]

Source material: In 2021, the central bank of Turkey cut the interest rate on four occasions. This was despite the country’s inflation rate rising from 15% at the start of the year to 36% at the end of the year.

Discuss whether or not a cut in the interest rate will reduce inflation.

Inflation is a sustained rise in the general price level. The interest rate is the cost of borrowing money and the reward for saving, and it is set by the central bank as part of monetary policy.

Why a cut might reduce inflation. A lower interest rate cuts firms’ cost of borrowing. Firms with loans pay less interest, which lowers their costs of production, so they may be able to keep prices down, reducing cost-push inflation.

Cheaper borrowing also encourages firms to invest in new machinery and technology. This raises productivity and increases total (aggregate) supply. If total supply grows faster than total demand in the long run, the pressure on prices eases and inflation may fall.

Why a cut might not reduce inflation. A lower interest rate makes borrowing cheaper and saving less rewarding, so households borrow more and save less, and consumer spending rises. Total demand increases, and if output cannot rise as fast, firms raise prices, causing demand-pull inflation. This is the main reason central banks normally raise interest rates to fight inflation.

A cut can also cause the currency to depreciate, as money flows out to countries with higher interest rates. A weaker currency makes imports more expensive, so imported raw materials, fuel and food cost more, raising firms’ costs and consumer prices. Turkey’s experience in 2021 fits this: after four interest rate cuts, the lira fell to a record low and inflation rose from 15% to 36%.

Conclusion. In most cases a cut in the interest rate will not reduce inflation, because its main and fastest effect is to raise borrowing, spending and total demand, and it may weaken the currency, as the Turkish data show. It could help only if inflation is mainly cost-push and caused by high borrowing costs, and if the extra investment raises total supply faster than demand, which takes several years. The answer therefore depends on the cause of inflation and on the time period considered.

Why this scores
Level 3: definitions, two developed arguments on each side from the mark scheme (lower costs and higher investment and total supply; higher borrowing and spending causing demand-pull inflation) plus the exchange-rate effect, a clear judgement and the conditions it depends on. Using the Turkish data in both the argument and the conclusion shows strong application.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/25 Q3(d) · February/March 2025 · [8 marks]

Source material: Estonia’s firms were affected in 2022 by the government increasing the money supply.

Discuss whether or not an increase in the money supply will benefit an economy.

The money supply is the total amount of money in an economy, including notes and coins and bank deposits. An increase in the money supply is a form of expansionary monetary policy.

Why it might benefit an economy. With more money in the economy, banks have more funds to lend, so the rate of interest tends to fall and loans become easier to obtain. Households borrow and spend more, and firms borrow to invest in new capital. Higher consumer spending and investment increase total (aggregate) demand.

As total demand rises, firms increase output to meet it, so real GDP grows and firms take on more workers, reducing unemployment. This is especially beneficial in a recession or when there is a risk of deflation, since the extra spending uses resources that would otherwise be idle. Investment financed by easier borrowing can also raise productive capacity, supporting growth in the long run.

Why it might not benefit an economy. If the money supply grows faster than the output of goods and services, more money is chasing the same goods, so total demand exceeds total supply and firms raise prices, causing demand-pull inflation. Inflation reduces the purchasing power of money, harming people on fixed incomes and savers, and makes the country’s exports less price competitive.

Some of the extra spending will also go on imports. As imports rise, the current account of the balance of payments worsens, and less of the extra demand benefits domestic firms. In addition, easier lending may lead some households and firms to borrow more than they can repay, so debt problems build up and may cause financial difficulties later.

Conclusion. An increase in the money supply is likely to benefit an economy when there is spare capacity, such as in a recession, because the extra spending raises output and employment rather than prices. It is unlikely to benefit an economy that is already close to full employment, where the main effect will be higher inflation and imports. The benefit therefore depends on how large the increase is compared with the growth of output, and on how much of the extra spending goes on domestic rather than imported goods.

Why this scores
Level 3: a definition, developed arguments both ways and a conclusion conditional on spare capacity and the size of the increase. The examiner report praised answers that linked an increase in the money supply to higher total demand, and criticised unexplained claims such as “the exchange rate will fall” that turned the answer into a different question.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

4.5 Supply-side policy

These questions test whether you can name specific supply-side policy measures and trace how each one improves the quantity or quality of resources, and so raises total supply and productive capacity; examiners reward answers that follow each measure through a full chain to the outcome the question asks about.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports say weaker answers state that deregulation is “a decrease in regulation” without saying what it does, and in Discuss questions some candidates confused a government’s budget deficit with a current account deficit, which meant they answered a different question. Name the measure, then keep linking it to the aim in the question.
0455/23/O/N/20 Q4(a) · October/November 2020 · [2 marks]

Source material: The use of supply-side policy measures, including deregulation, is moving China closer to a market economic system.

Define deregulation.

Deregulation is the removal of government rules and regulations that restrict how firms can enter or operate in a market. It is a supply-side policy measure used to increase competition and efficiency.

Why this scores
Two marks for “the removal of rules and regulations”; the second sentence would also earn both marks by the alternative route (a supply-side policy (1) to increase competition (1)). The examiner report singles out “Deregulation is the decrease in regulation” as a common weak answer, because it repeats the word without saying what is removed or why.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/23 Q3(c) · October/November 2023 · [6 marks]

Source material: The reduction in the country’s output resulted in a rise in its unemployment rate. The government used supply-side policy measures to reduce unemployment.

Analyse how supply-side policy measures could reduce unemployment.

Supply-side policy measures aim to increase the quantity and quality of resources and so raise total supply and productive capacity. Several of them could reduce unemployment in Suriname.

Education and training. Government spending on training gives unemployed workers new skills, which improves the quality of labour and their occupational mobility. Workers who lost their jobs when firms stopped producing could then move into industries that have vacancies, which reduces structural unemployment.

Lower income tax and lower unemployment benefit. A cut in income tax increases the reward from working compared with being unemployed. If unemployment benefit is also reduced, unemployed people have a stronger incentive to search actively and accept a job sooner, which reduces frictional unemployment.

Lower corporation tax and subsidies. A cut in corporation tax leaves firms with more profit after tax, giving them the funds and the incentive to invest and expand. Subsidies lower firms’ costs of production, which may allow firms that could not cover their costs to keep producing. As firms increase output, they need more workers, so employment rises.

Deregulation and labour market reforms. Removing rules that make it costly or slow to hire workers lowers firms’ labour costs and risks, so firms are more willing to take on extra workers.

Why this scores
The mark scheme rewards each link in a chain: the measure (1), its effect on skills, incentives or costs (1), and the link to a fall in unemployment, ideally a named type (1). Each measure here is followed to the end of its chain, and no idea (such as “raises productivity”) is used twice, because the mark scheme rewards the same point only once.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/25 Q5(d) · February/March 2025 · [8 marks]

Source material: In 2022, Finland’s Government had a budget deficit with government spending exceeding tax revenue.

Discuss whether supply-side policy measures will reduce a government’s budget deficit.

A budget deficit occurs when government spending is greater than tax revenue. Supply-side policy measures aim to increase total supply and productive capacity, for example education and training, privatisation, subsidies and cuts in direct taxes.

Why supply-side policy measures might reduce the budget deficit. Spending on education and training raises the skills and productivity of workers. More productive workers are more likely to be employed and earn higher incomes, so the government collects more income tax. Firms’ costs per unit fall, so they can expand and earn higher profits, which raises corporation tax revenue, and the extra spending from higher incomes raises revenue from indirect taxes. At the same time, with fewer people unemployed, the government spends less on unemployment benefits. Tax revenue rises and spending falls, so the deficit narrows.

Privatisation can also reduce the deficit. Selling state-owned firms brings in revenue for the government, and if the firms were making losses, the government no longer has to spend money supporting them. If the privatised firms become more efficient and profitable, they pay corporation tax in future years.

Why supply-side policy measures might not reduce the budget deficit. Many supply-side measures cost money. Education, training, subsidies and infrastructure all raise government spending straight away, while any extra tax revenue may take many years to arrive, so in the short run the deficit is likely to grow. The policies may also fail: trained workers may emigrate to higher-paying countries, so Finland pays for their training but loses the tax revenue it hoped to gain.

Cutting income tax or corporation tax lowers tax rates, so unless incomes and profits rise by enough to make up for this, tax revenue falls and the deficit widens. Selling profitable state-owned firms also means the government loses the profits they used to pay it every year. Finally, if total demand is weak, the extra productive capacity may not be used, so output, incomes and tax revenue do not rise.

Conclusion. Supply-side policy measures are unlikely to reduce a budget deficit in the short run, because most of them raise government spending or cut tax rates immediately. They are more likely to reduce it in the long run if they succeed in raising productivity, employment and incomes enough to lift tax revenue and cut benefit spending. The outcome depends mainly on which measure is used (selling loss-making firms helps quickly, while training costs money first), how long the government is willing to wait, and whether total demand is strong enough for the extra capacity to be used.

Why this scores
Level 3: a definition, two developed chains on each side that keep returning to government spending and tax revenue, and a conclusion that judges the short run against the long run and names what the result depends on. The examiner report warns that answers which confuse a budget deficit with a balance of payments deficit are placed in Level 1, even if they know supply-side policy well.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/22 Q5(d) · May/June 2022 · [8 marks]

Source material: Since he came to power, he has focused on using supply-side policy measures, rather than monetary policy.

Discuss whether or not supply-side policy measures always promote economic growth.

Economic growth is an increase in a country’s real GDP, its output of goods and services. Supply-side policy measures aim to increase total supply by improving the quantity and quality of resources, which shifts the production possibility curve outwards.

Why supply-side policy measures might promote economic growth. Education and training raise the skills and productivity of the labour force. Each worker can produce more, and better-quality, output per hour, so firms’ average costs fall. Firms can then expand and compete more effectively in export markets, which raises real GDP. Better healthcare has a similar effect, because a healthier labour force loses fewer working days.

Lower direct taxes improve incentives. A cut in income tax increases the reward from working, so more people may join the labour force or work longer hours, increasing the quantity of labour. A cut in corporation tax leaves firms with more profit to invest in capital goods, which increases productive capacity. Deregulation and privatisation can increase competition and the profit motive, pushing firms to cut costs and innovate.

Why they might not always promote economic growth. Supply-side measures take a long time to work. A training programme may take years to produce more skilled workers, so it may do little for growth in the short run. They also have an opportunity cost: spending on training may mean less spending on other services or higher taxes, and tax cuts reduce government revenue, which may lead to a budget deficit and more government borrowing.

Even if productive capacity increases, actual output only rises if there is enough total demand. In a recession, firms will not use extra capacity if consumers are not buying, so the economy simply produces further inside its new PPC. Some measures may also backfire: privatised firms may become private monopolies that raise prices and cut quality rather than becoming more efficient.

Conclusion. Supply-side policy measures do not always promote economic growth, but they are one of the best ways of raising long-run growth because they increase productive capacity. They are most likely to work when they are well targeted (for example, training in skills that firms actually need), when there is enough total demand for the extra output, and when the government can afford them without large cuts elsewhere. In the short run, and especially in a recession, demand-side policies are likely to raise growth more quickly.

Why this scores
Level 3: both sides are developed with economic reasoning and the conclusion explains when the policies work and when they do not. The examiner report’s strong answer was praised because it kept its focus on economic growth throughout, rather than drifting into a general description of supply-side policies.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

4.6 Economic growth

These questions test what economic growth is (an increase in real GDP), what causes it on both the demand and the supply side, and its benefits and costs; examiners reward answers that keep linking every point back to output or productive capacity.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports note that some candidates measure economic growth by GDP per head, or by unemployment and inflation figures, rather than by the change in a country’s total real output. In Discuss questions, weaker answers name one or two benefits or costs (jobs, pollution, inflation) without explaining how growth actually causes them.
0455/22/M/J/23 Q1(c) · May/June 2023 · [2 marks]

Source material: The main reason why Guyana’s real GDP increased so rapidly was the increase in oil production.

Explain how economic growth is measured.

Economic growth is measured by the percentage change in a country’s real GDP, the total value of the goods and services it produces adjusted for inflation, from one year to the next.

Why this scores
One mark for “real” and one for GDP (the country’s total output). The examiner report says some candidates wrote GDP per head, which the mark scheme does not accept, because growth is about the increase in total output.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/25 Q2(b) · October/November 2025 · [4 marks]

Source material: Most of this growth was due to higher output in the primary sector.

Explain two causes of rising GDP.

An increase in investment. If firms spend more on capital goods, such as machinery or, in Guyana’s case, oil-drilling equipment, total (aggregate) demand rises because investment is part of it. The extra capital also increases the economy’s productive capacity, so firms can produce more output and GDP rises.

A cut in the rate of interest. Lower interest rates make borrowing cheaper, so households borrow more to buy goods such as cars and houses and firms borrow to expand. Consumer spending and investment increase, so total demand rises and firms increase output to meet it, raising GDP.

Why this scores
One mark for each cause identified and one for each explanation that shows how it leads to higher output. The two causes work through different routes, so they are not marked as the same point.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/20 Q3(c) · May/June 2020 · [6 marks]

Source material: Other reasons why firms want to produce in Ireland include access to freer trade with other European countries, higher labour productivity and government grants.

Analyse, using a production possibility curve (PPC) diagram, the impact of higher labour productivity on an economy.

Labour productivity is output per worker per hour. If labour productivity rises, each worker can produce more goods and services in the same time with the same other resources.

This increases the productive capacity of the economy: the maximum output it can produce when all its resources are fully and efficiently used. On a production possibility curve diagram, this is shown by an outward shift of the curve from PPC to PPC1 (see diagram). The economy can now produce more capital goods and more consumer goods at the same time, for example moving from point A to point B.

As a result, the economy can achieve economic growth: real GDP can rise and people can enjoy more goods and services, which can raise living standards. Higher productivity also lowers firms’ costs per unit, making Ireland’s products more internationally competitive and attracting more MNCs. However, the economy will only produce on PPC1 if there is enough demand; otherwise it will be at a point inside the new curve.

Why this scores
Up to 4 marks for the diagram: labelled axes (1), the original curve (1), the new curve (1) and the outward shift shown by an arrow or labels (1). Up to 2 marks for the analysis that output per worker rises (1) and so productive capacity increases (1). Showing a movement along the curve instead of a shift would lose the diagram marks.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/25 Q5(c) · October/November 2025 · [6 marks]

Source material: More workers in Canada would increase the tax revenue available to spend on pensions, and more workers would also increase consumer spending.

Analyse how an increase in consumer spending could increase economic growth.

Economic growth is an increase in real GDP. Consumer spending is the largest component of total (aggregate) demand, so a rise in it has a direct effect on output.

Firms produce more. When households spend more, firms sell more goods and services, so their revenue and profits rise. To meet the extra demand they increase output, which raises real GDP. Rising sales also boost business confidence and encourage new firms to start up.

More jobs and a further rise in spending. To produce more, firms need more workers, so unemployment falls and incomes rise. The newly employed spend part of their wages, which increases consumer spending again and leads to a further rise in output (the multiplier effect).

More investment. Higher profits give firms the funds and the incentive to buy new capital goods and technology. This increases the economy’s productive capacity, so output can keep growing in the long run, not just in the short run.

Higher tax revenue. More spending raises the government’s revenue from indirect taxes such as sales tax. In Canada this could be spent on infrastructure, education or healthcare, improving the quality of resources and raising productive capacity further.

Why this scores
Each paragraph is a separate chain that the mark scheme credits step by step: more spending (1), higher demand and confidence (1), more output, jobs, investment or tax revenue (1), higher GDP or productive capacity (1). The mark scheme rewards “raising GDP” and “increasing productive capacity” only once, so each chain adds a different step rather than repeating the ending. The multiplier is rewarded but not required.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

4.7 Employment and unemployment

These questions test the meaning of full employment and the types, causes and consequences of unemployment; examiners reward answers that name the type of unemployment and explain who is affected and why.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports say that some candidates simply define unemployment when asked to define full employment, and in Discuss questions write about how a government could reduce unemployment instead of whether it should. Another common weakness is making one point, such as lost skills, several times in different words instead of giving different reasons.
0455/22/O/N/22 Q3(a) · October/November 2022 · [2 marks]

Source material: Russia experienced full employment in the past.

Define full employment.

Full employment is when unemployment is at the lowest level possible, so the number of people unemployed is roughly equal to the number of job vacancies. There may still be a small amount of frictional unemployment, because some workers are between jobs.

Why this scores
Two marks for “the lowest possible unemployment” or vacancies matching the number unemployed; the point about frictional unemployment confirms understanding. The examiner report says stronger answers recognised that full employment still includes a very low percentage of unemployed workers, while a number of candidates just defined unemployment.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/23 Q3(b) · October/November 2023 · [4 marks]

Source material: The reduction in the country’s output resulted in a rise in its unemployment rate.

Explain two types of unemployment.

Frictional unemployment. This is when workers are unemployed for a short time while they are between jobs, for example after leaving one job and searching for a better one. It occurs because it takes time for workers and employers to find each other.

Cyclical (demand-deficient) unemployment. This is caused by a lack of total demand in the economy, usually during a recession. When demand falls, firms cut output or stop producing, as some did in Suriname in 2020, so they need fewer workers and make some redundant.

Why this scores
One mark for each type identified and one for each explanation of its cause. Structural unemployment (skills that no longer match the vacancies after a change in demand or technology) would also earn both marks.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/25 Q4(b) · October/November 2025 · [4 marks]

Source material: One is to maintain price stability and the other is to achieve full employment.

Explain two benefits to an economy of full employment.

Higher output. At full employment the economy is making full use of its labour, so it produces close to the maximum output on its production possibility curve. More goods and services are produced, so real GDP and living standards are higher.

Higher tax revenue and lower spending on benefits. With almost everyone who wants a job in work, the government collects more income tax and indirect tax and spends less on unemployment benefits. It can use the money on education or healthcare, or keep tax rates low.

Why this scores
One mark for each benefit identified and one for each explanation. Lower poverty, because more households earn an income and can afford necessities, would also score.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/M/J/23 Q5(c) · May/June 2023 · [6 marks]

Source material: More than half of those unemployed in Greece had been unemployed for more than a year.

Analyse why someone who has been unemployed for more than a year may not get another job.

Someone who has been out of work for more than a year is long-term unemployed, and several linked reasons can make it harder for them to find another job.

Loss of skills and experience. The longer a person is out of work, the more their skills and qualifications become out of date, especially where technology changes quickly. They also lack recent work experience. Employers see them as less productive and know they would have to pay to retrain them, so they prefer to hire someone who has been working recently.

Their industry may be in decline. If the person lost their job because demand for their industry’s product fell, their skills may no longer be wanted (structural unemployment). If they are occupationally immobile, they cannot move into growing industries without retraining; if they are geographically immobile, for example because housing elsewhere is too expensive or they have family ties, they cannot move to areas where jobs exist.

Few vacancies in a recession. When household spending falls, as it did in Greece in 2020, firms cut output and stop hiring. Many unemployed people compete for very few vacancies, and the long-term unemployed are usually the least attractive candidates.

Loss of confidence and motivation. Repeated rejections can lead to depression and ill health. The person may become discouraged, put less effort into searching or stop looking altogether. Older workers may also face age discrimination, or decide to retire and leave the labour force.

Why this scores
Each paragraph gives a different reason and develops it, which is what the mark scheme rewards (a reason (1) and its development (1)). The examiner report says less successful candidates focused too heavily on lost skills and repeated the same point in several ways, while stronger answers also considered the effects of a recession, mental health and age.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/23 Q3(d) · May/June 2023 · [8 marks]

Source material: In 2021, the Canadian government encouraged higher investment and aimed to prevent a rise in unemployment.

Discuss whether or not a government should try to prevent a rise in unemployment.

Unemployment is when people who are willing and able to work cannot find a job. A rise in unemployment means a larger share of the labour force is out of work.

Why a government should try to prevent a rise in unemployment. Unemployed workers are an unused resource, so the economy produces inside its production possibility curve and output is lower than it could be, and that lost output can never be recovered. The unemployed also lose income, so they spend less; firms sell less, their profits fall and they cut investment, which can push unemployment up further.

A rise in unemployment also worsens the government’s budget. Tax revenue falls because fewer people pay income tax and spending falls, while government spending on unemployment benefits rises, leaving less money for education and healthcare. For the unemployed themselves, lower incomes can push households into poverty, and long spells out of work can damage health and skills.

Why a government should not always try to prevent it. The rise may be small or temporary. If it is mostly frictional unemployment, as workers move between jobs, it will correct itself, and some unemployment gives the economy flexibility because expanding firms can find workers. If unemployment is already low, trying to stop it rising may push total demand above productive capacity, causing demand-pull inflation, and higher incomes may raise imports and worsen the current account.

Policies to prevent unemployment also have an opportunity cost. Extra government spending on subsidies or training to protect jobs means less spending on other services, or higher taxes or borrowing. Some policies may not work, or may take a long time, and the government may decide that another aim, such as controlling inflation, is more important at that time.

Conclusion. A government should try to prevent a large rise in unemployment, especially cyclical unemployment caused by a recession, because the costs in lost output, lower tax revenue and poverty are high. It does not need to act on a small, temporary rise in frictional unemployment, or when unemployment is already very low and inflation is the bigger risk. The decision depends on the type and size of the rise and on whether the policy chosen can work without causing inflation.

Why this scores
Level 3: a definition, developed arguments on both sides, and a conclusion that depends on the type and size of the rise and the risk of inflation. The examiner report notes that some candidates wrote about how a government could reduce unemployment, which does not answer whether it should; strong answers explained the harm to the unemployed, firms and the economy and why a rise might not be a problem.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

4.8 Inflation and deflation

These questions test how inflation is measured, its causes (demand-pull and cost-push) and the consequences of inflation and deflation; examiners reward answers that name the type of inflation and say exactly who gains or loses and why.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports say weaker answers write that “prices rise” without naming demand-pull or cost-push inflation, or muddle them into “demand-push” and “cost-pull”. When a question asks about one group, such as firms, some candidates drift into the general effects of inflation on the whole economy.
0455/23/O/N/23 Q5(a) · October/November 2023 · [2 marks]

Source material: In 2020, Paraguay’s Consumer Prices Index rose by 3% and its foreign exchange rate fluctuated.

Define Consumer Prices Index.

A Consumer Prices Index (CPI) is a measure of changes in the cost of living, and so of inflation. It tracks the prices of a weighted basket of goods and services bought by a typical household, compared with a base year.

Why this scores
One mark for a measure of inflation or the cost of living and one for the weighted basket of goods and services. Both key words, “weighted” and “basket”, are worth including.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/21 Q3(b) · October/November 2021 · [4 marks]

Source material: An increase in wages can cause inflation and can increase the number of consumer goods bought and produced.

Explain two ways an increase in wages can cause inflation.

Demand-pull inflation. Higher wages increase workers’ disposable income, so they spend more on goods and services. Total (aggregate) demand rises, and if firms cannot increase output fast enough to meet it, they raise their prices, causing demand-pull inflation.

Cost-push inflation. Wages are a cost of production for firms. When wages rise, firms’ costs per unit rise, so to protect their profit margins they pass the higher costs on to consumers as higher prices, causing cost-push inflation.

Why this scores
One mark for each link (higher demand; higher costs of production) and one for naming the type of inflation it causes. The examiner report says weaker answers just wrote about rising prices without naming the type, and a few confused the terms as “demand-push” and “cost-pull”.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/25 Q5(c) · May/June 2025 · [6 marks]

Source material: It was expected that higher output would be caused mainly by an increase in household spending, although this could also result in inflation.

Analyse how an increase in household spending could cause inflation.

Inflation is a sustained rise in the general price level. Household spending (consumption) is a component of total (aggregate) demand, so an increase in it can cause inflation in two ways.

Demand-pull inflation. When households spend more, total demand rises. If the economy is close to its productive capacity, firms cannot increase output quickly enough, so total demand exceeds total supply. Shortages appear and firms can raise their prices without losing sales, so the general price level rises.

Cost-push inflation. To meet the higher demand, firms increase output and hire more workers. As workers become scarcer, firms compete for them by offering higher wages. Firms also buy more raw materials and capital goods, which pushes up their prices. Firms’ costs of production rise and they pass them on to consumers as higher prices.

A wage-price spiral. Higher prices reduce workers’ real incomes, so they ask for further pay rises. Higher wages raise both household spending and firms’ costs again, so prices rise further and inflation keeps going.

The effect on inflation is greater when the economy is already close to full employment, and smaller if firms have spare capacity, because they can then increase output instead of raising prices.

Why this scores
The mark scheme credits each step: consumption is part of total demand (1), total demand rises (1) and exceeds total supply (1), leading to demand-pull inflation (1), plus the route through higher wages and input prices to cost-push inflation and a wage-price spiral. The examiner report praised answers that analysed both demand-pull and cost-push inflation.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/25 Q4(d) · February/March 2025 · [8 marks]

Source material: Its high inflation rate of 22% had several effects on the country’s firms.

Discuss whether or not inflation will harm a country’s firms.

Inflation is a sustained rise in the general price level, measured by the annual percentage change in the Consumer Prices Index.

Why inflation might harm firms. Inflation usually raises firms’ costs. The prices of raw materials and capital goods rise, and workers demand higher wages to protect their real incomes, sometimes taking industrial action that disrupts production. If a firm cannot raise its own prices by as much, for example because it faces strong competition, its profit margin is squeezed and profits fall. With a rate as high as Nigeria’s 22%, firms also face menu costs from constantly changing their prices.

If Nigeria’s prices rise faster than other countries’, its firms become less internationally competitive: exporters lose sales abroad and domestic firms lose customers to cheaper imports. Uncertainty about future costs and prices also makes it hard for firms to plan, so they may postpone investment. In addition, if the central bank raises the rate of interest to reduce inflation, firms’ borrowing costs rise and consumers cut their spending.

Why inflation might not harm firms. If inflation is demand-pull, it is caused by rising spending, so firms are selling more. They may be able to raise their prices by more than their costs, so revenue and profits increase. Wages are often fixed for a year, so in the short run the real cost of labour falls.

Inflation also reduces the real value of debt. A firm that borrowed to buy machinery repays a fixed sum that is worth less in real terms, which helps firms with large loans. If inflation is low and stable, or lower than in rival countries, firms can predict it, build it into their plans and may even become more internationally competitive.

Conclusion. High and unpredictable inflation like Nigeria’s 22% is likely to harm most firms, especially those facing strong competition or selling abroad, because their costs rise faster than they can raise prices and planning becomes very difficult. Low, stable inflation of around 2% is unlikely to harm firms and may help them. Whether inflation harms a firm depends on its cause (cost-push or demand-pull), how its rate compares with other countries’, and whether the firm can raise its prices without losing customers.

Why this scores
Level 3: every argument is about firms specifically (costs, prices, profits, competitiveness, planning, debt), both sides are developed, and the conclusion depends on the cause and rate of inflation. The examiner report criticised answers that wrote about the general effects of inflation on an economy, and its Level 1 example simply listed effects without explaining them.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/25 Q3(d) · October/November 2025 · [8 marks]

Source material: Japan has faced significant periods of deflation in recent decades. Despite efforts to stimulate the economy through fiscal policy measures and monetary policy measures, economic growth remains low.

Discuss whether or not deflation is a problem for an economy.

Deflation is a sustained fall in the general price level, so the inflation rate is negative.

Why deflation may be a problem. If consumers expect prices to keep falling, they delay buying items such as cars, furniture and electronics, waiting for them to become cheaper. Total (aggregate) demand falls, so firms sell less, cut their prices further and reduce output. They need fewer workers, so unemployment rises and incomes fall, which reduces spending even more. This deflationary spiral can lead to a long period of low growth, as Japan has experienced.

Deflation also increases the real value of debt. Households and firms must repay a fixed amount that is now worth more in real terms, so they cut spending to pay off their debts. Firms’ revenues and profits fall with lower prices, so they invest less and confidence in the economy falls. Monetary policy also becomes less effective, because interest rates cannot easily be cut much below zero to encourage borrowing.

Why deflation may not be a problem. If prices are falling because costs of production are falling, for example because of new technology or higher productivity, firms can cut prices and still make a profit. Consumers’ real incomes rise, so they can afford more goods and services and living standards improve.

Lower prices can also make a country’s exports more internationally competitive, if its prices are falling faster than other countries’. Export sales rise, which can increase output and employment. A mild, short period of deflation is also unlikely to change consumers’ spending habits very much.

Conclusion. Deflation is a serious problem when it is caused by falling total demand and lasts a long time, because expectations of falling prices lead to a spiral of lower spending, output and employment, as Japan’s low growth suggests. It is much less of a problem, and can even be beneficial, when it is caused by falling costs and is mild and short-lived. The key factors are its cause, how long it lasts and whether people expect prices to keep falling.

Why this scores
Level 3: a definition, two developed arguments on each side (the deflationary spiral and debt against cost-driven deflation and competitiveness), and a conclusion that turns on the cause and length of the deflation, which the mark scheme names as the key evaluative point.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
