Topical Past Papers · Topic 5

Past Paper Questions: Economic Development

20 real Cambridge IGCSE Economics Paper 2 questions from 2020 to 2025, sorted by sub-topic, each with its exact paper reference and a full-mark exemplar answer. The questions are quoted exactly; the model answers are my own, written to the published mark schemes and the advice in the examiner reports.

How to use this page
Cover the answer, write your own in timed conditions (about 1½ minutes per mark), then compare. 0455/22/M/J/25 Q2(d) means Paper 2, variant 2, May/June 2025, Question 2 part (d). F/M = February/March, M/J = May/June, O/N = October/November.

Jump to: 5.1 Living standards · 5.2 Poverty · 5.3 Population · 5.4 Differences in economic development between countries

5.1 Living standards

These questions test what living standards are, how they are measured (real GDP per head and the HDI) and what raises or lowers them; examiners reward answers that follow every change through to people’s actual quality of life, not just to output or income.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports repeatedly note candidates giving “GDP” as an indicator of living standards: it must be real GDP per head, because total GDP ignores population size. Weaker answers also stop at “more money” instead of saying what people can now afford and how their health, education or working lives improve.
0455/23/M/J/24 Q1(c) · May/June 2024 · [2 marks]

Explain how real GDP per head can be an indicator of living standards.

Real GDP per head is a country’s output adjusted for inflation and divided by its population, so it shows the average real income of each person. A higher real GDP per head means that, on average, people can afford more goods and services such as food, housing and healthcare, so their material living standards are higher.

Why this scores
One mark for showing that real GDP per head is average income adjusted for inflation and population, and one for linking it to how much people can consume. Saying just “GDP” would not score, because it does not take population into account.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/25 Q4(b) · May/June 2025 · [4 marks]

Source material: People who work abroad often send money home to relatives.

Explain two ways that money received from relatives working abroad could raise their families’ living standards.

More and better food. The money sent home raises the family’s income, so they can buy more food and better-quality food. Better nutrition improves their health, so family members are ill less often, and the family may be lifted out of absolute poverty.

Paying for education. The family can use the money to pay for school fees, books or a training course for the children. This raises the children’s skills and qualifications, so they are more likely to get better-paid jobs when they grow up, which raises the family’s living standards in the future.

Why this scores
One mark for each way the money is used and one for explaining how it raises living standards. The examiner report warns that some candidates wrote about why the money sent home might be large (higher wages abroad, the exchange rate), which earns nothing: the question is about what the family does with it.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/24 Q4(c) · October/November 2024 · [6 marks]

Source material: South Africa experienced an increase in output, driven largely by the growth in its tertiary sector.

Analyse how growth in a country’s tertiary sector can increase its living standards.

Living standards are the quality of life of a country’s people, covering both material things such as income and the goods and services they can buy, and non-material things such as health, education and working conditions. The tertiary sector produces services such as banking, retail, tourism and healthcare.

More jobs and higher incomes. As the tertiary sector grows, firms such as banks, hotels and call centres take on more workers. This reduces unemployment, which in South Africa is very high at 35%. Newly employed workers earn a wage instead of relying on family or benefits, so they can afford more necessities such as food and housing, which reduces poverty. Many service jobs are also skilled and productive, so they pay higher wages, allowing workers to buy some luxuries as well.

Better working conditions. Service-sector jobs are usually less physically demanding and less dangerous than jobs in mining or farming. Workers moving into the tertiary sector are therefore less likely to suffer accidents or long-term illness, which improves their health and life expectancy and so raises their quality of life.

Better health and education services. Healthcare and education are themselves part of the tertiary sector. If more clinics, hospitals, schools and training colleges open, people have easier access to treatment and learning. This raises life expectancy and skills, both of which are part of living standards and push up the country’s HDI value.

Higher tax revenue. Higher incomes and spending in the service sector raise the government’s revenue from income tax and VAT. The government can then spend more on public services such as clean water, roads and state schools, which improves living standards even for people who do not work in the tertiary sector.

Why this scores
It defines living standards in both material and non-material terms, then builds full chains such as growth in services → more jobs → higher income → more necessities → less poverty. Using South Africa’s 35% unemployment rate applies the stimulus, and covering health, working conditions and tax revenue shows the range the mark scheme rewards.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/M/J/24 Q2(d) · May/June 2024 · [8 marks]

Source material: The country’s economy experienced the world’s seventh highest economic growth rate, with an increase in GDP of 7.4%.

Discuss whether or not people living in countries with a high GDP enjoy higher living standards than people in countries with a low GDP.

GDP is the total value of output produced in a country in a year. Living standards are people’s quality of life, including both their material income and non-material factors such as health, education and the environment.

Why people in high-GDP countries might enjoy higher living standards. A high GDP usually means firms are producing a large amount of goods and services and paying out a large amount of income as wages and profits. People with higher incomes can afford better housing, a more varied diet and more consumer goods, so their material living standards are higher.

A high GDP also generates high tax revenue for the government. It can spend this on hospitals, schools, clean water and pensions. Better healthcare raises life expectancy, and better education gives people the skills for more interesting, better-paid jobs with better working conditions, so non-material living standards rise too.

Why they might not. A high GDP does not mean a high GDP per head. A country with a very large population, such as Bangladesh with over 170 million people, can have a high total GDP while the average income of each person is low. A small country such as Luxembourg has a much lower GDP but a far higher income per head and higher living standards.

Even where GDP per head is high, income may be very unevenly distributed. If a small group of rich people receives most of the income, many others may still live in poverty. In addition, high-GDP countries often have a higher cost of living, so the same income buys less, and high output can come with long working hours, stress, pollution and traffic congestion, which reduce quality of life. GDP in low-income countries may also under-record people’s real incomes, because a lot of activity takes place in the informal economy.

Conclusion. People in countries with a high GDP do not necessarily enjoy higher living standards. They are likely to be better off only if the high GDP is also a high real GDP per head once the cost of living is taken into account, if income is shared fairly evenly, and if the government uses its tax revenue on health and education rather than the gains being offset by pollution and long working hours. For Bangladesh, 7.4% growth in GDP will raise living standards most if it is faster than population growth and reaches low-income households.

Why this scores
This meets the Level 3 description: definitions first, two developed arguments on each side, and a conclusion that gives a clear judgement with the specific conditions (GDP per head, cost of living, distribution, use of tax revenue). The key evaluative point the mark scheme looks for is that high GDP is not the same as high GDP per head.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/25 Q4(d) · May/June 2025 · [8 marks]

Source material: As cities experience economic growth, the buildings in the city become taller.

Discuss whether or not economic growth will improve living standards in a city.

Economic growth is an increase in a country’s or region’s real GDP, the output of goods and services. Living standards are the quality of life of people living in the city, including their income, health, housing and environment.

Why it might improve living standards in a city. When a city grows, existing firms expand and new offices, shops and construction projects appear, such as the taller buildings in the stimulus. This increases the demand for labour, so unemployment in the city falls and wages tend to rise. City residents then have more income to spend on better food, housing and leisure.

Growth also raises the tax revenue of the city and national government, because more people pay income tax and more is spent on goods subject to VAT. This revenue can fund better public transport, street lighting, hospitals and schools in the city, improving residents’ health and education. With more firms competing, residents also have a wider choice of shops, restaurants and entertainment.

Why it might not. Growth brings negative externalities. More production and more car ownership increase air pollution, noise and traffic congestion. City residents may suffer more breathing problems and spend hours a day commuting, which reduces their quality of life even though their incomes are higher.

A growing city also attracts migrants from rural areas and other countries looking for work. The demand for housing rises faster than new homes can be built, so rents and house prices rise and some people end up living in overcrowded or poor-quality housing. The benefits of growth may also go mainly to skilled workers and business owners, while low-skilled workers face higher prices without higher wages, so inequality and relative poverty in the city increase.

Conclusion. Economic growth is likely to raise material living standards for most people in the city, through jobs and higher incomes. Whether it improves overall living standards depends on how fast and how well managed the growth is: if the city authorities use the extra tax revenue for housing and public transport and regulate pollution, the gains will outweigh the costs, but rapid, unplanned growth that pulls in migrants faster than housing and infrastructure can expand may leave many residents worse off.

Why this scores
The mark scheme caps answers at 4 marks if they do not refer to a city, so every paragraph is about the city: jobs, tax revenue for city services, pollution, congestion, overcrowding and housing costs. Two developed points on each side and a conditional judgement place it in Level 3; the examiner report praised answers that kept attention on the focus of the question throughout.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

5.2 Poverty

These questions test the difference between absolute and relative poverty, what causes poverty and how policies can reduce it; examiners reward answers that trace a clear chain from each cause or policy to the incomes and living conditions of the poor themselves.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports say relative poverty is the weaker definition: “they can afford their needs but not their wants” earns no credit. Relative poverty means being poor compared with others in the same country, for example having an income below 60% of the average. In Discuss questions, strong answers also note that a policy may cut absolute poverty while relative poverty remains.
0455/23/O/N/23 Q2(a) · October/November 2023 · [2 marks]

Source material: Almost 60% of Mozambique’s population live in absolute poverty.

Identify two reasons why a person may experience absolute poverty.

1. Unemployment.

2. A natural disaster, such as a storm, destroying their home and source of income.

Why this scores
One mark for each reason. “Identify” needs only the reason, so do not explain. Other creditworthy reasons include sickness or disability, old age, war or famine, being a refugee, a lack of government benefits and a low level of education.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/M/J/22 Q4(b) · May/June 2022 · [4 marks]

Explain the difference between absolute poverty and relative poverty.

Absolute poverty is when a person does not have enough income to afford the basic necessities needed to survive, such as food, clean water, clothing and shelter. It is measured against a fixed international poverty line, for example living on less than about $2 a day.

Relative poverty is when a person is poor compared with other people in the same country. Their income is so far below the average that they cannot take part in the normal activities of their society, even if they can survive. It is often measured as having an income of less than 60% of the country’s median income, so a person in a high-income country can be in relative poverty without being in absolute poverty.

Why this scores
Two marks for each type: one for the definition and one for an example or measure (a poverty line of about $2 a day; below 60% of average income). The mark scheme gives no credit for saying people in relative poverty “can meet their needs but not their wants”, a common weak answer in the examiner report.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/25 Q4(b) · October/November 2025 · [4 marks]

Source material: Lanzarote, a Spanish island, was once an area with very high rates of poverty. However, the rise of tourism has greatly reduced unemployment.

Explain how unemployment can lead to poverty.

Loss of income. When a worker becomes unemployed, they lose their wage, which is the main source of income for most households. Without it, they may be unable to afford basic necessities such as food and rent, which pushes the family into absolute poverty. Their children may also have a poorer diet and miss out on education, so poverty can affect the next generation too.

Long-term unemployment and relative poverty. Where unemployment benefits are available, they are usually much lower than wages, so an unemployed person’s income falls well below the average and they fall into relative poverty. The longer they stay out of work, the more their skills become out of date, making it harder to get a job again, so they can become trapped in poverty. This is why the fall in unemployment brought by tourism has reduced poverty in Lanzarote.

Why this scores
Each paragraph identifies a link (lost income; low benefits and loss of skills) and develops it to a specific type of poverty, which is how the mark scheme awards its four marks. Naming absolute and relative poverty shows precise use of terms.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/25 Q4(c) · October/November 2025 · [6 marks]

Source material: The Central African country Chad has a triangle-shaped population pyramid and a high level of poverty.

Analyse the causes of poverty.

Poverty is when people do not have enough income to reach an acceptable standard of living. Absolute poverty is being unable to afford basic necessities; relative poverty is being poor compared with others in the same country.

Low levels of education and skills. People who leave school early or receive poor-quality education lack the skills employers want. They can usually get only low-skilled jobs, which are low-paid and often insecure, or no job at all. Their income is then too low to afford necessities, and because they cannot pay for their own children’s schooling, the children also end up in low-paid work, creating a cycle of poverty.

Illness and poor healthcare. If someone is sick or disabled and cannot get good healthcare, they may be unable to work or may have to work fewer hours. Their income falls, while they may also have to pay for treatment. In countries with few state benefits, such as many low-income countries, this can quickly push a family into absolute poverty.

Large families. A triangle-shaped population pyramid, like Chad’s, shows a high birth rate and a large proportion of children. In large families, the income of one or two adults has to be shared among many dependants, so income per person in the household is very low. Children may also have to leave school early to work, which lowers their future earnings and keeps the family in poverty.

Why this scores
The mark scheme rewards a maximum of three causes, so three fully developed chains (cause → effect on income → poverty) score more than a long list. Linking the third cause to Chad’s population pyramid uses the stimulus intelligently.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/24 Q5(d) · October/November 2024 · [8 marks]

Source material: It is estimated that 9% of Hawaii’s population were living in poverty in 2022.

Discuss whether or not fiscal policy is effective in reducing poverty.

Fiscal policy is the use of government spending and taxation to influence the economy. Poverty is a lack of income to reach an acceptable standard of living, either absolute (unable to afford necessities) or relative (poor compared with others in the country).

Why fiscal policy might be effective. The government can raise the incomes of the poor directly by paying more generous state benefits, such as unemployment benefit and state pensions. With Hawaii’s ageing population, higher pensions would help retired people who cannot work to afford food, heating and medicine, reducing absolute poverty quickly.

Fiscal policy can also tackle the causes of poverty. Spending more on state education and training raises the skills of children from poor families, so they can get better-paid jobs in future. If this is financed by progressive taxation, the rich pay a higher proportion of their income and the money is redistributed to the poor, which narrows the income gap and reduces relative poverty. Cutting indirect taxes such as VAT on food also lowers the price of necessities, so low-income households can buy more.

Why it might not be effective. Extra spending has to be paid for through higher taxes or borrowing. Higher income taxes may discourage people from working or investing, and skilled workers may leave. In Hawaii, high labour mobility between US states means young workers can easily move away, so the tax base shrinks and there is less revenue to spend on the poor.

Expansionary fiscal policy can also be inflationary. Hawaii already has rising prices because of growth in tourism, and if extra government spending increases total demand further, the prices of necessities such as housing and food rise faster. The poor then lose purchasing power, which can increase poverty. In addition, generous benefits may reduce the incentive to find work, and education spending takes many years to affect incomes.

Conclusion. Fiscal policy can be effective in reducing poverty, especially absolute poverty, if spending is targeted at those who need it most, for example pensions for the elderly and free education and healthcare for low-income families, and is financed by progressive taxes. It is less effective when inflation is already high, as in Hawaii, when taxes push mobile workers to leave, or when benefits are designed in a way that discourages work.

Why this scores
Level 3: definitions of both key terms, two developed arguments on each side using Hawaii’s ageing population, inflation and labour mobility, and a conclusion that judges which fiscal measures work and under what conditions. Distinguishing absolute from relative poverty adds the evaluation the mark scheme rewards.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

5.3 Population

These questions test what determines a country’s population size, growth and structure (birth rate, death rate and net migration) and the economic effects of a young or ageing population; examiners reward answers that name exactly which rate changes and then follow the effect through to the labour force, government spending and living standards.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports note candidates confused about how a fall in the death rate affects population size (it increases it), and candidates confusing retirement with unemployment when writing about an ageing population. Another common weakness is stating “a high birth rate” without explaining what causes it.
0455/21/M/J/25 Q2(a) · May/June 2025 · [2 marks]

Source material: The shape of Colombia’s population pyramid is changing.

Define population pyramid.

A population pyramid is a diagram that shows the structure of a country’s population by age group and by gender (males on one side, females on the other).

Why this scores
One mark for age and one for gender. The examiner report notes that some candidates wrongly said a population pyramid shows the birth rate and death rate, or income and wealth.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/M/J/25 Q4(b) · May/June 2025 · [4 marks]

Source material: In 2023, India became the country with the largest population.

Explain two reasons why a country may have a high population growth rate.

A high birth rate. In some countries people marry at a young age, have little education about or access to birth control, and see children as extra workers on the family farm and as support in old age. Families therefore have many children, so there are many births per thousand of the population and the population grows quickly.

A falling death rate. Improvements in healthcare, vaccinations, clean water and nutrition mean fewer people die from disease and life expectancy rises. If the death rate falls while the birth rate stays high, the gap between them (the natural increase) widens, so the population grows rapidly.

Why this scores
Each reason is identified (1) and its cause explained (1), as the mark scheme requires. The examiner report says weaker answers simply stated “high birth rate and low death rate” without explaining what caused them. Net immigration due to higher incomes would also be credited.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/24 Q3(c) · May/June 2024 · [6 marks]

Analyse two economic consequences of having a large proportion of the population aged under 16.

A country with a large proportion of its population aged under 16 has a young population. Most of these people are dependants: they are too young to be in the labour force and rely on others for income.

A high dependency ratio and lower living standards. Because so many people are children, there are relatively few people of working age to support them. Workers’ incomes have to be shared among many dependants, so income per head in households is lower. The government also collects less income tax relative to the number of people it must provide for, so it has less money per person for public services. As a result, living standards tend to be lower than in a country with a smaller share of children.

High spending on education, but a bigger labour force in future. A large number of children means the government must spend heavily on schools, teachers and child healthcare. This has an opportunity cost: the money cannot be used for other things such as roads, hospitals or pensions. However, this spending is an investment. In ten years or so these children will reach working age, so the size of the labour force will increase. With more workers, the economy’s productive capacity rises, labour costs may be kept down, and firms, including foreign multinationals, may be encouraged to invest. This gives the country high potential for future economic growth.

Why this scores
It analyses exactly two consequences, as asked, and develops each into a full chain: many dependants → income shared → lower living standards; education spending → opportunity cost → future labour force → growth potential. The mark scheme credits both the short-run cost and the long-run benefit.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/24 Q2(c) · October/November 2024 · [6 marks]

Source material: Immigration is the main cause of the country’s growth in population.

Analyse the causes of migration between countries.

Migration between countries is the movement of people from one country to live and work in another. People are influenced by push factors (problems in their own country) and pull factors (attractions of the other country).

Higher wages and more jobs. The most important pull factor is the chance to earn a higher income. A worker from a low-income country may earn several times more doing the same job in a highly developed country such as Luxembourg. If unemployment is high at home, the chance of finding any job at all is a strong push factor. By moving, migrants can buy more goods and services and may also send money home, raising their family’s standard of living.

Better public services. People may move to gain access to better healthcare and education. Better healthcare raises their life expectancy and quality of life, and a better education gives their children a greater chance of getting a well-paid job in the future, so migration can improve living standards across generations.

Escaping danger. Push factors such as war, persecution, high crime, famine or natural disasters force people to leave their country to stay safe. Refugees may move even without a job waiting, because staying would threaten their lives or livelihoods. Some people also migrate to join family members who have already moved abroad.

Why this scores
The mark scheme limits answers to 3 marks if causes are only identified, so each cause is developed: what attracts or pushes people, and how moving improves their income, health, education or safety. Organising by push and pull factors shows clear understanding.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/24 Q2(d) · February/March 2024 · [8 marks]

Source material: Since 2016, South Korea’s population has declined, due to a falling birth rate, and a high proportion of its population is now aged over 64.

Discuss whether or not having a high proportion of a country’s population over the age of 64 is a benefit to its economy.

A high proportion of a population over the age of 64 is described as an ageing population. It results from a falling birth rate and rising life expectancy.

Why it might be a benefit. An ageing population is usually a sign of success: people are living longer because of better healthcare, nutrition and living standards. Many people over 64 are still healthy and can keep working, especially if the retirement age is raised. Older workers are experienced, skilled and often reliable, so they can be highly efficient and can train younger workers.

Older people also contribute in other ways. Many look after their grandchildren, allowing parents to stay in full-time work. They often have savings and pensions to spend, which increases demand for healthcare, leisure and tourism and creates jobs in those industries. With fewer young dependants, the government can also spend less on schools, as South Korea has done by having fewer schools.

Why it might not be a benefit. A large proportion of retired people raises the dependency ratio: fewer people of working age have to support more people who are not working. The government must spend more on state pensions and healthcare for the elderly, which has an opportunity cost, and it must pay for this with higher taxes on a shrinking group of workers or by borrowing. Higher taxes may reduce workers’ disposable income and incentive to work.

In addition, the labour force shrinks, which can cause labour shortages and push up wage costs for firms, reducing output and economic growth. Older workers may also be less occupationally mobile and less familiar with new technology, so productivity and innovation may grow more slowly.

Conclusion. For South Korea, a high proportion of people over 64 is more likely to be a cost than a benefit, because its falling birth rate means the labour force is shrinking at the same time as pension and healthcare spending rises. It can become a benefit if older people stay healthy and keep working for longer, have private savings rather than relying on the state, and if the government offsets the labour shortage through immigration and investment in technology.

Why this scores
Level 3: a definition, two developed arguments on each side (benefits: experience, childcare, spending; costs: dependency ratio and pensions, smaller and less adaptable labour force) and a conclusion that applies the judgement to South Korea and names the conditions. It avoids the pitfall the examiner reports mention of treating retired people as unemployed.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

5.4 Differences in economic development between countries

These questions test why some countries are more economically developed than others, through differences in income, productivity, education, healthcare, population growth, the size of each sector, and saving and investment; examiners reward answers that treat development as a rise in living standards, not simply as economic growth.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports describe definitions of economic development as often vague or circular (“development of an economy”), and the mark schemes do not accept an increase in GDP on its own. Development must be linked to living standards, for example GDP per head, education, healthcare or HDI.
0455/23/M/J/21 Q5(a) · May/June 2021 · [2 marks]

Define economic development.

Economic development is an improvement in the living standards and economic welfare of a country’s population, shown by higher income per head together with better education and healthcare, for example a rise in the country’s HDI.

Why this scores
Two marks for the idea of a rise in living standards or quality of life; naming HDI or income per head would earn a mark on its own. The mark scheme states that an increase in GDP is not sufficient, and the examiner report criticised vague, circular definitions.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/23 Q2(b) · October/November 2023 · [4 marks]

Source material: The country’s people are keen to increase Mozambique’s economic development.

Explain two causes of an increase in a country’s economic development.

Improvements in education. If the government spends more on schools and training, workers become more skilled and productive. More productive workers can earn higher wages and find jobs more easily, so incomes rise and unemployment falls. People can then afford more goods and services, raising living standards.

Investment in infrastructure. If the government or foreign firms invest in rebuilding the roads, bridges and electricity lines destroyed by the 2019 storm, firms can produce and transport goods more easily. This increases the country’s productive capacity and creates jobs, raising incomes and helping to reduce Mozambique’s high level of absolute poverty.

Why this scores
One mark for each cause and one for explaining how it raises development. Both explanations end with living standards, not just output, and the second applies the Mozambique context. Other credited causes include better healthcare, FDI, technology and more saving to fund investment.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/25 Q3(c) · May/June 2025 · [6 marks]

Source material: The public sector in Andorra is focusing on developing the healthcare, biotechnology and sports industries.

Analyse how differences in healthcare can lead to differences in economic development between countries.

Economic development is an improvement in a country’s living standards, including income per head, health and education. Healthcare differs greatly between countries in the number of doctors and hospitals, access to medicines and the quality of treatment.

Productivity and incomes. In a country with good healthcare, such as Andorra, workers are healthier and take less time off sick. A healthy workforce is more productive, producing more output per hour. Firms’ average costs fall and their output rises, so they can pay higher wages. Higher incomes allow people to buy more goods and services, so living standards are higher than in a country where frequent illness keeps productivity and wages low.

Life expectancy and HDI. Better healthcare lowers the death rate, especially among babies and young children, and raises life expectancy. Life expectancy is one of the three components of the Human Development Index, so a country with better healthcare will have a higher HDI value and a higher level of development. Lower child mortality also means families need fewer children, reducing the birth rate and the dependency ratio, so income per head rises.

Investment and growth. A healthy, productive workforce makes a country’s firms more competitive, so exports increase, and it attracts investment, including from multinational companies. This creates more jobs, reduces unemployment and poverty, and raises tax revenue that the government can spend on education. In a country with poor healthcare, the opposite cycle keeps incomes and development low.

Why this scores
The examiner report praised answers that focused on how healthcare affects productivity, incomes and living standards; this answer does that and adds life expectancy and HDI. It avoids two mistakes the report highlights: treating the question as public versus private healthcare, and giving two sides when the command word is “Analyse”.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/24 Q4(c) · October/November 2024 · [6 marks]

Analyse the causes of differences in economic development between countries.

Economic development is an improvement in the living standards of a country’s people. It is not the same as economic growth: a country’s output can rise without most people becoming better off. Countries differ in development for several reasons.

Saving and investment. In low-income countries, most income is spent on necessities, so people can save very little. With little saving, banks have few funds to lend for investment, so firms cannot buy modern machinery and technology. Workers’ productivity stays low, which keeps incomes low, and the cycle continues. High-income countries can save and invest more, raising productivity and income per head.

The size of each sector. Less developed countries usually have a large primary sector, with many people in subsistence farming. Farming produces low value added, and incomes are low and depend on the weather. More developed countries have large secondary and tertiary sectors, where output per worker and wages are higher, so average incomes and living standards are higher.

Population growth. A country with a rapidly growing population has many young dependants. Income must be shared among more people, so income per head grows slowly, and the government has to stretch spending on schools and healthcare across a rising number of children. A country with slower population growth can raise income per head more easily.

Education. Countries with better education have more skilled workers, who are more productive and can do higher-paid jobs. This raises incomes and attracts foreign firms, creating more jobs, whereas poorly educated workforces are limited to low-skilled, low-paid work.

Why this scores
It defines economic development and states that it is not the same as economic growth, a distinction the mark scheme insists on. It then analyses four separate differences, each ending with income per head or living standards, which provides the logical chains the top marks need.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/25 Q2(d) · October/November 2025 · [8 marks]

Source material: The government used some of the tax revenue generated from this growth to fund long‑run development projects such as infrastructure and education.

Discuss whether or not increased government spending on education will lead to economic development.

Economic development is an improvement in the living standards of a country’s people, measured by indicators such as income per head, life expectancy and years of schooling (for example the HDI).

Why it might lead to economic development. More government spending on education, such as building schools, training teachers and funding universities, raises people’s skills and qualifications. A more skilled workforce is more productive, so workers can get better-paid jobs and unemployment falls. Higher incomes mean people can afford more goods and services, raising their living standards. In Guyana, a better-educated workforce could take skilled jobs in the growing oil industry rather than these jobs going to foreign workers.

Education also improves development directly and indirectly. Years of schooling are part of the HDI, so the HDI rises. Educated people tend to make healthier choices and consume fewer demerit goods such as cigarettes, so life expectancy rises. An educated workforce also attracts investment from foreign multinationals, which increases the country’s productive capacity and creates more jobs.

Why it might not. Education takes a long time to have an effect. It may be ten or fifteen years before today’s pupils enter the labour force, so the spending does little to raise living standards in the short run. Extra spending also has an opportunity cost: the money could have been used on healthcare or infrastructure, and if it is paid for by higher taxes or borrowing, households may have less disposable income now.

The money may also be spent inefficiently, for example on new school buildings without enough trained teachers, or the courses may not match the skills firms need. Even if education succeeds, skilled workers may emigrate to higher-paid jobs abroad, so the country loses the benefit of the investment. Without enough job opportunities, educated young people may simply become unemployed graduates.

Conclusion. Increased spending on education is likely to lead to economic development in the long run, but not quickly. It is most likely to succeed if it is spent on the quality of teaching and on skills that match the jobs being created, and if those jobs exist so that educated workers stay in the country. For Guyana, oil revenue means the spending can be funded without raising taxes, so the long-run benefits are likely to outweigh the costs, provided the revenue is not wasted.

Why this scores
This fits Level 3: definition, two developed points on each side (skills, productivity and incomes; HDI, health and FDI against time lags, opportunity cost, inefficiency and brain drain) and a conclusion that judges the time period and the conditions. Using Guyana’s oil revenue and growing oil sector applies the stimulus.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
