Topical Past Papers · Topic 6

Past Paper Questions: International Trade and Globalisation

23 real Cambridge IGCSE Economics Paper 2 questions from 2020 to 2025, sorted by sub-topic, each with its exact paper reference and a full-mark exemplar answer. The questions are quoted exactly; the model answers are my own, written to the published mark schemes and the advice in the examiner reports.

How to use this page
Cover the answer, write your own in timed conditions (about 1½ minutes per mark), then compare. 0455/22/M/J/25 Q2(d) means Paper 2, variant 2, May/June 2025, Question 2 part (d). F/M = February/March, M/J = May/June, O/N = October/November.

Jump to: 6.1 International specialisation · 6.2 Globalisation, free trade and protection · 6.3 Foreign exchange rates · 6.4 Current account of balance of payments

6.1 International specialisation

These questions test why whole countries concentrate on producing particular goods and services and what they gain and risk by doing so; examiners reward answers that stay at the level of the country and link specialisation to efficiency, trade, growth and dependence.

What examiners keep seeing
The 2021 and 2022 examiner reports both note candidates writing about workers specialising (division of labour, boredom on a production line) when the question was about a country specialising. Keep every point about national output, exports, imports and reliance on a few industries.
0455/22/M/J/21 Q1(b) · May/June 2021 · [2 marks]

Source material: Producing just a few products can be risky for a country.

Identify two disadvantages of a country specialising.

1. World demand for the country’s main export may fall, for example if new competitor countries start producing it.

2. Output of the main product may be cut by bad weather, disease or a natural disaster, as can happen with an agricultural crop such as cherries.

Why this scores
One mark for each of two disadvantages. The mark scheme says that simply writing “it relies on a small number of industries” is not enough: say what could go wrong. Other creditworthy answers are non-renewable resources running out, structural unemployment and over-dependence on imports. The examiner report notes that some candidates wrote about workers specialising instead of countries.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/23 Q1(c) · October/November 2023 · [2 marks]

Source material: Honduras used to concentrate on growing bananas and coffee, gaining skills and a good reputation in those industries.

Explain one advantage of an economy specialising.

By concentrating on bananas and coffee, Honduran farmers and workers gained skills in those industries. More skilled workers are more productive, so Honduras could produce more output at a lower cost per unit and of a higher quality, making its exports more competitive on world markets.

Why this scores
One mark for an advantage taken from the source material (gaining skills, or gaining a good reputation) and one for explaining it (higher productivity and lower costs, or a good reputation raising demand). The mark scheme says the identification mark must come directly from the source, so quote its idea rather than a textbook advantage.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/23 Q4(b) · October/November 2023 · [4 marks]

Source material: However, despite still being in the top 5 of natural rubber exporters in the world, Malaysia has moved towards producing manufactured goods that use natural rubber instead of just producing and exporting this primary product.

Explain two reasons why a country may change its specialisation.

A change in costs of production. Natural rubber is labour-intensive, so as a country develops and its wages rise, producing it becomes more expensive. Countries with cheaper labour can then produce it at a lower cost, so the country loses its cost advantage and moves its resources into products where it is now relatively more efficient, such as manufactured rubber goods.

A change in demand. If world demand shifts towards higher-value products, for example rubber gloves and tyres rather than raw rubber, firms can earn more revenue and profit by making those products. Resources move to the goods that buyers now want, so the country’s pattern of specialisation changes.

Why this scores
Each reason is identified (1) and then explained (1) by showing why producing the old product became less attractive. Other reasons the mark scheme credits include the discovery of new resources, new technology and other countries’ producers gaining a competitive advantage.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/25 Q5(c) · May/June 2025 · [6 marks]

Source material: This agency also aims to coordinate trade between African countries to help technological development.

Analyse the benefits a country may gain from exporting to and importing from other countries.

International trade is the buying and selling of goods and services across national borders: exports are sold abroad and imports are bought from abroad.

Benefits of exporting. Exporting lets a country specialise in what it is best at producing. Its workers build up skills and its firms become more efficient, so output rises. Selling abroad also gives firms a much larger market than the home country alone, so they can grow and gain economies of scale, which lowers their average cost and lets them charge more competitive prices.

Exports are also a component of total (aggregate) demand. When foreign buyers spend more on a country’s products, domestic firms raise output and take on more workers, which causes economic growth and reduces unemployment. The export revenue also brings foreign currency into the country, which helps the current account of the balance of payments.

Benefits of importing. Imports give consumers a wider choice of goods and services, often at lower prices than domestic firms could offer, which raises living standards. Firms can import raw materials and capital goods that the country cannot produce, or cannot produce cheaply, which lowers their costs of production.

Imports also bring competition, which puts pressure on domestic firms to cut costs and improve quality. Trading with other countries spreads ideas and technology, as the African Space Agency hopes, which raises productivity and allows the economy to produce more in the future.

Why this scores
It defines international trade, then analyses exports and imports separately, as the examiner report advised, linking each to a macroeconomic outcome (growth, employment, living standards, costs). Each paragraph is a chain worth several marks. The report warns against treating this as a “Discuss”: no disadvantages are needed.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/22 Q1(g) · May/June 2022 · [6 marks]

Source material: However, to ensure growth is sustainable, the government sees the importance of economic diversification and does not want the economy to overspecialise.

Discuss whether or not specialisation at a national level is harmful to an economy.

Specialisation at a national level means a country concentrating its resources on producing a narrow range of goods and services, then trading for the rest.

Why it might be harmful. The country becomes over-dependent on a few industries. Malta’s service sector provides about 90% of its GDP and relies heavily on tourism, so if demand for holidays falls, for example because of a recession abroad or a pandemic, output and incomes fall across the whole economy and unemployment rises sharply.

When a specialised industry declines, its workers have skills that are hard to transfer to other industries, so they suffer long-term structural unemployment. If the country specialises in a natural resource, the resource may also run out, leaving the industry unsustainable.

Why it might not be harmful. By focusing on what it does best, a country uses its resources more efficiently. Workers become highly skilled and productive, firms gain economies of scale, and output rises at a lower cost per unit. This makes its exports competitive and brings in export revenue that the country can use to buy a wide range of imports, raising living standards and tax revenue.

Conclusion. Specialisation is not harmful in itself: it raises efficiency and incomes. It becomes harmful when a small economy such as Malta relies on one or two industries whose demand is unstable, which is why diversification into areas such as eSports reduces the risk while keeping most of the gains.

Why this scores
The mark scheme gives up to 4 marks for each side, capped at 6, with a maximum of 3 if there is no development. Both sides are developed and applied to Malta, and the conclusion makes a judgement with a condition. The examiner report notes that strong answers used over-dependence and resource exhaustion, while weak ones drifted into specialisation of workers.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/20 Q4(d) · October/November 2020 · [8 marks]

Source material: The intention is to raise economic growth through more international trade.

Discuss whether or not increased international trade can promote economic growth.

Economic growth is an increase in a country’s real GDP, its output of goods and services. International trade is the exchange of goods and services between countries, through exports and imports.

Why it might promote economic growth. Lower trade barriers give domestic firms access to a much larger market. Small and medium-sized firms in ASEAN countries can sell to millions more customers, so exports rise. Exports are an injection into total (aggregate) demand, so firms increase output and employ more workers, and real GDP rises.

Selling to a larger market also lets firms specialise and gain economies of scale, which lowers their average costs. Imports of cheaper raw materials and capital goods reduce costs further, while foreign competition pushes domestic firms to become more efficient. Lower costs and higher productivity increase the economy’s productive capacity, so growth can be sustained in the long run.

Why it might not promote economic growth. More trade also means more imports. If consumers switch from domestic products to cheaper foreign ones, spending leaks out of the economy, total demand for domestic output falls and real GDP may fall rather than rise.

Domestic firms that cannot compete with more efficient foreign producers may lose revenue and profit and close, causing unemployment, especially in industries that were previously protected. An economy that depends more on trade is also more exposed to sudden falls in world demand, such as a recession in its main trading partners, which can reduce its output.

Conclusion. Increased international trade is likely to promote growth when the country’s firms are competitive enough for exports to rise faster than imports and can use the larger market to gain economies of scale. It is less likely to do so in the short run for a country whose industries are inefficient or still developing, where rising imports and closures may outweigh export gains until resources move into the industries where it has an advantage.

Why this scores
This fits the Level 3 descriptor: definitions first, two developed arguments on each side using total demand, economies of scale and competition, and a conclusion that judges when trade helps (competitive firms, exports growing faster than imports) and when it may not. The mark scheme’s Level 2 example was marked down for being limited on one side.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

6.2 Globalisation, free trade and protection

These questions test how removing or adding trade barriers affects consumers, firms, workers and the government; examiners reward answers that follow each measure through the price of imports to what happens to domestic output, jobs and trade.

What examiners keep seeing
The 2025 examiner report notes that many candidates could say a tariff makes imports more expensive but did not develop the point any further, and that many focused on the effect on the government. Keep each chain going: higher import price, then what happens to consumers, firms, trade or inflation.
0455/21/O/N/24 Q1(b) · October/November 2024 · [2 marks]

Source material: The increased connection of people through migration, the trade of goods and services and the sharing of ideas were major features of the World Expo.

Identify two features of globalisation.

1. Increased migration of people between countries.

2. Increased international trade in goods and services.

Why this scores
One mark for each feature. The source material gives them directly, so pick two and state them briefly; the sharing of ideas and technological development are also accepted.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/25 Q5(a) · October/November 2025 · [2 marks]

Source material: Some countries engage in dumping by selling their products at less than cost price in Cambodia.

Identify two motives for a firm dumping some of its products in a foreign market.

1. To drive domestic firms in the foreign market out of business and gain market share or monopoly power there.

2. To get rid of surplus stock without having to lower its price in its home market.

Why this scores
One mark for each motive. Dumping is selling abroad below cost, so the motives are about gaining market power or clearing stock. Increasing export sales or revenue is also credited. Give exactly two.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/M/J/25 Q5(b) · May/June 2025 · [4 marks]

Source material: The Australian Government imposes relatively low import tariffs and the country has a floating exchange rate.

Explain two disadvantages of import tariffs.

Risk of retaliation. If a country puts tariffs on another country’s goods, that country may respond with tariffs of its own. The first country’s exports then become more expensive abroad, so demand for them falls, export firms cut output and workers in those industries may lose their jobs.

Cost-push inflation. A tariff raises the price of imported raw materials and components. Domestic firms that use them face higher costs of production, and they pass these on as higher prices, so the general price level rises and consumers’ real incomes fall.

Why this scores
One mark for each disadvantage identified and one for each explanation. Both explanations go one step further than “imports become dearer”, which the examiner report says many candidates failed to do; it also notes that answers about the effect on the government missed the point.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/25 Q5(c) · October/November 2025 · [6 marks]

Source material: Cambodia subsidises some infant industries.

Analyse how a government subsidy could help to protect an infant industry against foreign competition.

An infant industry is a new industry that has not yet grown large enough to compete with established foreign firms. A subsidy is a payment from the government to producers.

An infant industry’s average costs are high at first because its firms are small and cannot yet benefit from economies of scale. Established foreign firms can therefore sell at lower prices and could drive the new firms out of the market before they have had time to grow.

A subsidy reduces the firms’ costs of production, so they can lower their prices and become price-competitive with imports. Domestic consumers then buy more from the infant industry and fewer imports, so its sales and revenue rise.

Firms can also use the subsidy to buy more capital goods, adopt new technology and train or hire skilled workers. This raises their productivity and the quality of their products, so they also become more quality-competitive.

As output grows, the firms gain economies of scale and their average cost falls further. Eventually they can compete with foreign firms without help, both at home and in export markets, so the subsidy can be removed. Unlike a tariff, a subsidy does this without raising prices for consumers or risking retaliation from other countries.

Why this scores
It defines both terms, explains why the industry needs protection, then builds a full chain: subsidy → lower costs → lower prices → more competitive → higher sales → investment and economies of scale → able to compete unaided. Every link in that chain is a mark in the mark scheme.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/24 Q4(d) · October/November 2024 · [8 marks]

Source material: For example, in 2018 India imposed a tariff of approximately 90% on imports of coffee to protect its coffee farmers.

Discuss whether or not high tariffs can reduce unemployment.

A tariff is a tax on imports. Unemployment is when people who are willing and able to work cannot find a job.

Why high tariffs might reduce unemployment. A high tariff, such as India’s 90% tariff on coffee, raises the price of imports. Consumers switch to domestically produced substitutes, so demand for Indian coffee rises. Domestic producers increase output and, since the demand for labour is a derived demand, they employ more workers.

High tariffs also raise tax revenue for the government. It could spend this on education and training, which makes workers more skilled and occupationally mobile, or on subsidies to domestic firms. Both could reduce unemployment, especially structural unemployment.

Why high tariffs might not reduce unemployment. Other countries may retaliate with tariffs on the first country’s exports. Its exports become more expensive abroad, foreign demand for them falls and export industries cut jobs, which may cancel out the jobs saved in protected industries.

Tariffs on imported raw materials and capital goods raise domestic firms’ costs of production, which reduces their profits and output and may lead them to cut jobs. In addition, protected firms face less competition, so they may become inefficient and raise prices rather than expand output. If imports are still much cheaper even after the tariff, or the economy is already near full employment, few new jobs will be created.

Conclusion. High tariffs are most likely to reduce unemployment in the short run, in industries where good domestic substitutes exist and foreign countries do not retaliate. Over the long run they are less likely to work, because retaliation, higher costs for firms that use imported inputs and inefficiency in protected industries can destroy as many jobs as they save. The outcome depends mainly on how other countries respond and how much the economy relies on imported inputs.

Why this scores
This meets Level 3: definitions, two developed chains on each side (the switch to domestic goods and use of tariff revenue; retaliation and higher input costs), use of the India example, and a conclusion that judges the short run against the long run and names the conditions.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/25 Q4(d) · October/November 2025 · [8 marks]

Source material: Some economists suggest that the Chadian Government should impose import quotas to reduce its trade in goods deficit.

Discuss whether or not imposing import quotas will reduce a trade in goods deficit.

An import quota is a limit on the quantity or value of a product that can be imported over a period. A trade in goods deficit occurs when spending on imports of goods is greater than revenue from exports of goods.

Why quotas might reduce the deficit. A quota directly restricts the quantity of goods coming into the country. With fewer imports allowed in, the amount spent on imports falls, so the gap between import spending and export revenue narrows.

Because imports are scarcer, their price rises. Consumers and firms then switch to domestically produced substitutes. Domestic firms sell more and may expand, which further reduces the need for imports and could, in time, allow them to export.

Why quotas might not reduce the deficit. If the quota is set above the quantity currently imported, it has no effect. If it only covers a few goods, total import spending changes little. A low-income country such as Chad may also have few domestic substitutes for products like machinery and fuel, so firms and households must still buy imports, and when supply is restricted they may pay higher prices for each unit.

Trading partners may retaliate by restricting Chad’s exports, which reduces export revenue and can leave the deficit just as large. Quotas may also encourage smuggling. If imported raw materials and capital goods are restricted, Chad’s own producers face higher costs, which makes its exports less competitive.

Conclusion. Import quotas can reduce a trade in goods deficit in the short run if they cover a wide range of products for which domestic substitutes exist and trading partners do not retaliate. For Chad, which lacks substitutes for many imported goods and would harm its own producers by restricting imported inputs, quotas are unlikely to reduce the deficit by much. Raising the quality and quantity of its exports would be a more lasting solution.

Why this scores
Level 3: both key terms are defined (the mark scheme gives Level 1 credit for understanding quotas), each side has two developed arguments, and the conclusion is applied to Chad and names the conditions: breadth of the quota, domestic substitutes and retaliation.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

6.3 Foreign exchange rates

These questions test how a currency’s price is set by demand and supply in the foreign exchange market and how a change in it affects export and import prices; examiners reward answers that get the direction of every effect right.

What examiners keep seeing
Examiner reports keep noting candidates who confuse a rise in the exchange rate with a fall, and the 2025 mark scheme warns of analysis that is “wrong way around”. Write the price effect out each time: a fall in the currency makes exports cheaper abroad and imports more expensive at home.
0455/21/M/J/22 Q2(a) · May/June 2022 · [2 marks]

Define foreign exchange rate.

A foreign exchange rate is the price of one currency in terms of another currency, for example A$1 = US$0.65.

Why this scores
One mark for “price” (or value) of a currency and one for “in terms of another currency”. The examiner report notes that weaker answers mentioned another currency but left out the word price or value, and very weak ones confused it with the balance between exports and imports.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/23 Q4(b) · May/June 2023 · [4 marks]

Source material: The year saw an increase in the value of the country’s floating foreign exchange rate.

Explain two causes of an increase in the value of a country’s floating foreign exchange rate.

A floating exchange rate is set by the demand for and supply of the currency, so its value rises when demand for it increases or supply of it falls.

Higher demand for the country’s exports. If incomes rise abroad, or the country’s products become cheaper or better quality than rivals’, foreigners buy more of its exports. To pay for them they must buy the country’s currency, so demand for the currency rises and its value increases.

A rise in the rate of interest. Higher interest rates make it more rewarding to save money in the country’s banks. Foreign savers move funds (hot money) into the country, which means buying its currency, so demand for the currency rises and the exchange rate appreciates.

Why this scores
Each cause is identified (1) and explained through the demand for the currency (1). The mark scheme also credits causes on the supply side, such as a fall in imports, but credits “higher demand” only once, so the two causes here are clearly different. The examiner report notes that some candidates explained a fall instead of a rise.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/25 Q4(c) · February/March 2025 · [6 marks]

Source material: Its high inflation rate of 22% had several effects on the country’s firms. In 2022 and the start of 2023, Nigerians increased their demand for foreign currency.

Analyse the causes of an increase in the demand for foreign currency.

People and firms demand foreign currency when they need to pay for goods, services or assets priced in another country’s currency. Several changes can increase that demand.

Buying more imports. With inflation at 22%, Nigerian goods were rising in price faster than many foreign goods, so imports became relatively cheaper and more attractive. Nigerian households and firms switched to imports, and to pay foreign suppliers they had to buy more US dollars and other currencies. Rising incomes, more foreign travel and more students studying abroad have the same effect.

Higher interest rates abroad. If interest rates in other countries rise above those at home, savers earn a higher return by moving their money abroad. To deposit funds in foreign banks they must first buy foreign currency, so these hot money flows increase demand for it.

Speculation and loss of confidence. If people expect their own currency to fall in value, perhaps because of high inflation, they buy foreign currency to protect the value of their savings and to make a profit when they later sell it. Expectations can therefore raise demand for foreign currency quickly.

Investment abroad and profit flows. Firms that want to set up branches or buy assets in other countries need foreign currency to do so. Foreign MNCs operating in the country also buy their home currency when they send profits back, which adds to demand for foreign currency.

Why this scores
It explains why foreign currency is demanded, then analyses four separate causes, each linked to a reason for buying the currency, and uses Nigeria’s 22% inflation from the stimulus. The examiner report praised the strong analysis of interest rates and foreign direct investment on this question.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/25 Q1(h) · October/November 2025 · [6 marks]

Source material: Malawi’s foreign exchange rate fell in 2022. The country’s currency, the kwacha, bought fewer US dollars.

Discuss whether or not a government should try to stop its country’s foreign exchange rate falling in value.

A fall in the foreign exchange rate (depreciation) means the currency buys fewer units of other currencies, so exports become cheaper abroad and imports more expensive at home.

Why the government should try to stop the fall. Imports become more expensive. Malawi imports fuel, and many firms rely on imported raw materials and capital goods, so their costs of production rise. They pass these on as higher prices, causing cost-push inflation, which hits the poor hardest and can slow economic growth.

A falling currency can also damage confidence. Foreign investors may expect further falls and hold back investment, and any debts owed in US dollars become more expensive to repay in kwacha.

Why the government should allow the fall. Malawi’s exports, such as tea and sugar, become cheaper for foreign buyers, so demand for them rises, while imports fall. This could help reduce its current account deficit of 12.9% of GDP and raise output and employment in export industries.

Stopping the fall is also costly. The central bank would have to use its foreign currency reserves to buy kwacha or raise interest rates, which would reduce investment and spending, and money used this way has an opportunity cost in lower spending on education or healthcare.

Conclusion. Malawi should allow a gradual fall, because it needs more competitive exports to reduce its large current account deficit. It should intervene only if the fall becomes rapid enough to cause high inflation through fuel and other import prices, since a poor country with low reserves cannot afford to defend its currency for long.

Why this scores
Up to 4 marks are available for each side, with a maximum of 3 if reasons are not developed. Each reason here is a developed chain applied to Malawi (imported fuel, tea and sugar exports, the 12.9% deficit). The mark scheme warns that many candidates get the effects the wrong way round, so the opening sentence fixes the direction before the argument starts.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/M/J/25 Q5(d) · May/June 2025 · [8 marks]

Source material: The Australian Government imposes relatively low import tariffs and the country has a floating exchange rate.

Discuss whether or not having a floating exchange rate benefits an economy.

A floating exchange rate is one whose value is determined by the market forces of demand for and supply of the currency, without the government or central bank fixing it.

Why it might benefit an economy. A floating rate can correct a current account imbalance automatically. If a country has a deficit, it is supplying more of its currency to buy imports than foreigners demand to buy its exports, so the exchange rate falls. Its exports become cheaper and its imports more expensive, so exports rise, imports fall and the deficit shrinks without the government having to act.

The government and central bank do not have to keep large reserves of foreign currency to defend a fixed value, and they do not need to change interest rates to protect the exchange rate. This leaves interest rates free to be used for other macroeconomic aims, such as controlling inflation or reducing unemployment.

Why it might not benefit an economy. A floating rate can change frequently and unpredictably. Firms do not know how much they will earn from exports or pay for imports, which makes planning difficult and can discourage trade and investment, including investment by foreign MNCs.

Speculation can make the swings larger. If traders expect the currency to fall, they sell it, which makes it fall further than the economy’s trade position would justify. A sharp fall raises import prices and can cause cost-push inflation, while a sharp rise makes exports uncompetitive and can cause unemployment in export industries.

Conclusion. A floating exchange rate is likely to benefit a large, diversified economy with well-developed financial markets, such as Australia, where firms can protect themselves against currency changes and the automatic adjustment of the current account is valuable. It is less beneficial for a small economy that relies on one export or on imported essentials, where large swings in the currency cause damaging uncertainty and inflation.

Why this scores
Level 3: a definition, then developed arguments that are about the floating system itself (automatic correction, no reserves needed, freedom for interest rates; uncertainty, speculation) rather than just a strong or weak currency, which the examiner report says limited many answers. The report singled out automatic correction of the current account as the mark of the best responses.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

6.4 Current account of balance of payments

These questions test the four components of the current account and what changes the balance between money coming in and going out; examiners reward answers that link every cause to export revenue or import expenditure.

What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports note candidates confusing a current account deficit with a government budget deficit, and many forgetting that the current account includes primary and secondary income as well as trade in goods and services. Name the component you are talking about.
0455/22/M/J/24 Q1(a) · May/June 2024 · [1 mark]

Source material: Trade in goods and services balance – $3bn Primary and secondary income balance $0.6bn

Calculate Tunisia’s balance on the current account of its balance of payments.

Current account balance = trade in goods and services balance + primary and secondary income balance = −$3bn + $0.6bn = −$2.4bn

(Tunisia has a current account deficit of $2.4bn: its surplus on income only partly offsets its trade deficit.)

Why this scores
The mark is for −$2.4bn; the minus sign or the word deficit is needed to show which way the balance goes. Add the two balances: subtracting the $0.6bn instead of adding it gives −$3.6bn and scores zero.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/24 Q4(a) · May/June 2024 · [2 marks]

Source material: In 2021, the US had a trade in goods deficit on the current account of its balance of payments.

Define a trade in goods deficit.

A trade in goods deficit is when a country’s expenditure on imports of goods is greater than its revenue from exports of goods over a period of time.

Why this scores
Two marks for saying that import expenditure on goods exceeds export revenue from goods. “Imports exceed exports” on its own scores only 1, so use the words expenditure and revenue (or value).
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/22 Q4(b) · May/June 2022 · [4 marks]

Source material: Despite having more imports of goods than exports of goods, it still records a surplus on the current account of its balance of payments.

Explain how a country with more imports of goods than exports of goods can still record a surplus on the current account of its balance of payments.

The current account has four parts: trade in goods, trade in services, primary income and secondary income. Importing more goods than it exports gives a country a deficit on trade in goods, but the overall current account can still be in surplus if the other three parts together have a larger surplus.

For example, the country may export more services than it imports, such as tourism or financial services. It may also receive a large primary income from abroad, such as interest, profits and dividends on investments its citizens own in other countries, and a large secondary income, such as foreign aid and money sent home by its nationals working abroad. If these credits outweigh the trade in goods deficit, the current account is in surplus.

Why this scores
Marks go for naming the components, recognising that the goods deficit can be outweighed, and giving examples of surpluses on the other components. The examiner report says very few candidates saw this; answers about high export prices and low import prices got only partial credit.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/F/M/22 Q2(c) · February/March 2022 · [6 marks]

Source material: South Africa’s foreign exchange rate fell from 1 SA rand = US$0.08 in 2018 to 1 SA rand = US$0.06 in 2019.

Analyse how a fall in a country’s foreign exchange rate could reduce a deficit on the current account of its balance of payments.

A current account deficit occurs when the money flowing out of a country for imports and other debit items is greater than the money flowing in from exports and other credit items.

Exports become cheaper. When the rand falls from US$0.08 to US$0.06, a South African product priced at R100 falls in price for American buyers from $8 to $6. South African exports become more price-competitive, so foreign demand for them rises and South Africa’s export revenue increases.

Imports become more expensive. A product costing $10 now costs South Africans about R167 instead of R125. Households and firms buy fewer imports and switch to domestically produced substitutes, so spending on imports falls and less money flows out of the country.

With export revenue rising and import expenditure falling, net exports increase and the deficit on the current account narrows. This is most likely if demand for exports and imports is price-elastic, so that the changes in quantity are larger than the changes in price.

In addition, money sent home by South Africans working abroad is worth more in rand, which raises the value of secondary income and further helps reduce the deficit.

Why this scores
It defines a current account deficit (1), then follows both chains from price to demand to revenue or expenditure, which the examiner report praised in strong answers. Using the stimulus exchange rates to work out new prices shows clear understanding, and the price-elasticity condition and remittances are extra points the mark scheme credits.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/23 Q5(d) · May/June 2023 · [8 marks]

Source material: In 2019, China’s economic growth rate was 6.1% and Chinese households increased their spending.

Discuss whether or not a country with a high economic growth rate will have a deficit on the current account of its balance of payments.

Economic growth is an increase in real GDP. A deficit on the current account occurs when a country’s spending on imports and other debit items exceeds its earnings from exports and other credit items.

Why it might have a deficit. Higher growth raises incomes, so households are both able and more willing to spend more, including on imported goods and on services such as foreign holidays. As spending on imports rises faster than export earnings, more money flows out than in. Firms expanding production also import more raw materials and capital goods.

A fast-growing home market can also draw goods away from exports, as firms find it easier to sell at home. If total demand grows faster than the economy’s ability to produce, prices rise and the country’s products become less price-competitive abroad, so exports fall and imports rise.

Why it might not have a deficit. The growth may be export-led. If the economy is growing because foreign demand for its products is rising, as with China, export revenue increases along with output, so the current account may move into surplus.

Growth also encourages firms to invest in new capital and technology, which raises productivity, lowers costs and improves quality. Higher tax revenue from growth can fund education and healthcare, raising labour productivity. Both make domestic products more competitive, so exports rise and consumers switch away from imports.

Conclusion. A country with a high growth rate is likely to have a current account deficit when growth is driven by household spending and it relies on imported consumer goods and inputs. It is unlikely to have a deficit when growth is export-led and accompanied by rising productivity, as in China, where strong export earnings outweigh the extra imports that higher incomes bring.

Why this scores
Level 3: definitions, two developed arguments on each side with clear links to imports and exports, and a conclusion that depends on what is driving the growth. The examiner report criticised answers that said exports would rise without explaining why, and those that confused a current account deficit with a budget deficit.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/25 Q5(d) · October/November 2025 · [8 marks]

Source material: The country has one of the world’s largest deficits on the current account of its balance of payments (as a percentage of GDP). Some economists suggest cutting taxes would reduce this deficit.

Discuss whether or not cuts in taxes will reduce a deficit on the current account of a country’s balance of payments.

A current account deficit is when a country’s spending on imports and other debit items is greater than its earnings from exports and other credit items. Tax cuts could be cuts in income tax, corporation tax, indirect taxes or tariffs.

Why tax cuts might reduce the deficit. A cut in corporation tax leaves firms with more profit after tax, so they are more able and willing to invest in new machinery and technology. This raises productivity and quality and lowers costs, so their products become more competitive. Exports rise and consumers buy domestic products instead of imports, which reduces the deficit. Lower corporation tax may also attract export-oriented MNCs to the country.

A cut in income tax increases the reward from working, which may motivate workers to work harder, raising productivity and lowering costs. Cutting tariffs on imported raw materials and components lowers firms’ costs too, so they can cut the prices of their exports.

Why tax cuts might not reduce the deficit. A cut in income tax raises disposable income, and some of this extra income is spent on imports, so import spending may rise and the deficit may widen. Higher spending also increases total demand, which may cause demand-pull inflation and make the country’s products less price-competitive abroad.

Tax cuts also reduce government revenue, so the government may have to spend less on education, healthcare and transport, which harms productivity in the long run. In addition, if foreign MNCs are attracted, they send their profits home, which is a debit on primary income and adds to the current account deficit.

Conclusion. Tax cuts are more likely to reduce the deficit if they are targeted at firms, such as lower corporation tax linked to investment or cheaper imported inputs for exporters, because these raise competitiveness. Cuts in income tax are likely to make the deficit worse in the short run, since much of the extra spending will go on imports. The overall effect depends on which taxes are cut and on whether higher investment raises productivity quickly enough to outweigh the extra spending on imports.

Why this scores
Level 3: definitions, developed arguments on both sides that distinguish between types of tax, and a conclusion that judges which tax cuts would work and why. The mark scheme rewards recognising that different taxes have different effects, and the MNC profit point links to primary income.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

Topical Past Papers · Topic 2

Past Paper Questions: Price Elasticity of Demand

Real Cambridge IGCSE Economics Paper 2 questions on price elasticity of demand (PED) from 2020 to 2025, each with its exact paper reference and a full-mark exemplar answer. The questions are quoted exactly; the model answers are my own, written to the published mark schemes and the advice in the examiner reports.

How to use this page
Cover the answer, write your own in timed conditions (about 1½ minutes per mark), then compare. The reference tells you the paper: 0455/22/M/J/25 Q2(d) means Paper 2, variant 2, May/June 2025, Question 2 part (d). F/M = February/March, M/J = May/June, O/N = October/November.
What examiners keep seeing
The examiner reports for these papers say weaker answers confuse elastic demand with an increase in demand, or say that having more substitutes makes demand inelastic. Price elasticity is about how strongly quantity demanded responds to a price change, not about whether demand goes up or down. Strong answers open with a definition and then keep linking every point back to the size of that response.

Identify / Calculate / Explain one [1–2]

0455/22/F/M/23 Q5(a) · February/March 2023 · [2 marks]

Source material: Serbia is the world’s second largest producer of raspberries, a product with elastic demand.

Identify two determinants of price elasticity of demand.

1. The number and closeness of substitutes.

2. The proportion of income spent on the product.

Why this scores
One mark for each correct determinant. “Identify” only needs the name, so there is no need to explain. Give exactly two: other creditworthy answers include whether the product is a necessity or a luxury, whether it is habit-forming, whether the purchase can be postponed, the time period and brand loyalty.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/M/J/24 Q1(a) · May/June 2024 · [1 mark]

Source material: It has been estimated that a 10% change in the price of coffee will cause a 3% change in the quantity of coffee demanded.

Calculate the price elasticity of demand for coffee.

PED = % change in quantity demanded ÷ % change in price = 3% ÷ 10% = (−)0.3

(PED is less than 1, so demand for coffee is price-inelastic.)

Why this scores
The single mark is for 0.3 (the minus sign is accepted but not required). Write the formula first: it takes five seconds and protects you from putting the figures the wrong way up, which gives 3.33 and scores zero.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/O/N/23 Q1(c) · October/November 2023 · [2 marks]

Source material: Tourism is a very competitive market. Demand for holidays is price‑elastic which has implications for the pricing strategy of firms in the industry.

Explain one way price elasticity of demand may influence firms’ decision making.

Demand for holidays in Montenegro is price-elastic, so a tourism firm knows that if it cuts its prices, quantity demanded will rise by a greater percentage than the fall in price. Its total revenue will therefore rise, so the firm is likely to decide to lower prices (for example, by offering discounts) rather than raise them, since a price rise would cause revenue to fall.

Why this scores
One mark for identifying that demand is price-elastic, one for explaining the effect on revenue of a price change. The mark scheme gives no credit for the effect on demand or on profit, so the answer talks about revenue, not “more customers” or “more profit”.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

Explain [4]

0455/22/M/J/20 Q2(b) · May/June 2020 · [4 marks]

Explain two influences on whether demand for a product is price-elastic or price-inelastic.

Availability of close substitutes. If a product has many close substitutes, consumers can easily switch when its price rises. A small rise in price therefore causes a large percentage fall in quantity demanded, so demand is price-elastic. A product with few or no close substitutes, such as electricity, has price-inelastic demand.

Proportion of income spent on the product. If a product takes up a large share of a consumer’s income, such as a car, a price rise has a noticeable effect on what they can afford, so they cut their purchases significantly and demand is price-elastic. If the product takes only a small share of income, such as salt or matches, a price rise is hardly noticed, so quantity demanded changes very little and demand is price-inelastic.

Why this scores
Each influence is identified (1) and then explained by showing which way it pushes PED and why (1). Showing both the elastic and the inelastic case makes the explanation secure.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/M/J/22 Q3(b) · May/June 2022 · [4 marks]

Source material: France … is the world’s top exporter of luxury perfume and the 7th largest exporter of soap.

Explain why demand for soap is more price-inelastic than demand for a luxury brand of perfume.

Necessity versus luxury. Soap is a necessity that people need for basic hygiene every day, so they keep buying it even if its price rises. A luxury brand of perfume is a want rather than a need, so if its price rises consumers can simply go without or buy it less often. Quantity demanded of soap therefore responds much less to a price change than quantity demanded of the perfume.

Proportion of income. A bar of soap is cheap and takes up only a very small proportion of a household’s income, so even a 20% price rise is barely noticed. A bottle of luxury perfume is expensive and takes up a much larger proportion of income, so a price rise has a big effect on consumers’ budgets and leads to a larger percentage fall in quantity demanded.

Why this scores
The question is a comparison, so each point covers both products. The mark scheme gives 2 marks for each fully comparative reason (or 1 + 1 for a point about each product). Other valid reasons: soap has fewer close substitutes than one brand of perfume, and buying soap is harder to postpone.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/21/O/N/24 Q1(d) · October/November 2024 · [4 marks]

Source material: Oil is a necessity and its price inelasticity of demand has brought both advantages and disadvantages to the economy.

Explain one advantage and one disadvantage of producing a product which is price-inelastic in demand such as oil.

Advantage. Because oil is a necessity with few substitutes, when its price rises the quantity demanded falls by a smaller percentage than the price rises. The oil producer therefore earns more total revenue when the price rises, for example when world oil prices increase, which boosts export earnings and profits.

Disadvantage. When the price of oil falls, the quantity demanded rises by a smaller percentage than the fall in price. Total revenue therefore falls, so a country such as the UAE that depends on oil exports can see a large drop in its export revenue whenever world oil prices fall, and it cannot sell its way out of the problem by cutting its price.

Why this scores
One mark for each effect identified and one for each explanation. Both points use the key idea, a less than proportionate change in quantity demanded, and then link it to revenue.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

Analyse [6]

0455/22/F/M/24 Q4(c) · February/March 2024 · [6 marks]

Source material: The delivery of luxury chocolate and salt, for example, was delayed. These two goods have differences in their price elasticity of demand.

Analyse the reasons why the price elasticity of demand for one brand of luxury chocolates is likely to be different from that of salt.

Price elasticity of demand (PED) measures how responsive quantity demanded is to a change in price. Demand for one brand of luxury chocolates is likely to be price-elastic, while demand for salt is likely to be price-inelastic.

Substitutes and breadth of definition. One brand of luxury chocolates is narrowly defined, so it has many close substitutes: other luxury chocolate brands and other gifts such as flowers. If its price rises, many consumers switch to a rival brand, so quantity demanded falls by a greater percentage than the rise in price (PED greater than 1). Salt is a widely defined product with almost no substitutes in cooking, so consumers keep buying it and quantity demanded falls by a smaller percentage (PED less than 1).

Necessity or luxury, and postponing the purchase. Luxury chocolates are a want, not a need, and buying them can easily be postponed or skipped if they become more expensive. Salt is seen as a necessity for cooking and preserving food, so households cannot easily cut back. This makes demand for the chocolates more responsive to price than demand for salt.

Proportion of income. A box of luxury chocolates takes up a noticeable proportion of income, so a price rise is felt and consumers react. Salt is very cheap and is bought infrequently, so a price change is hardly noticed and has very little effect on the quantity bought.

However, the difference may be smaller if consumers are very loyal to the chocolate brand, for example because of strong advertising. Loyal customers see few true substitutes, which would make demand for that brand less elastic.

Why this scores
It starts with a definition, then analyses three determinants and applies each one to both products, ending each chain with what happens to quantity demanded and the PED value. The brand-loyalty point is credited by the mark scheme and shows a deeper understanding.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/23/M/J/21 Q5(c) · May/June 2021 · [6 marks]

Source material: The demand for smartphones has become more price-inelastic as the range of functions available has increased.

Analyse the advantages of selling a product which is price-inelastic in demand.

A product is price-inelastic in demand when a change in its price causes a smaller percentage change in quantity demanded, so PED is less than 1. Smartphones are an example: as their range of functions has grown, people see them as essential and have few alternatives.

Higher revenue from a price rise. With inelastic demand, price and total revenue move in the same direction. If a firm raises its price from P1 to P2, quantity demanded falls only slightly from Q1 to Q2. The revenue gained on the units still sold is greater than the revenue lost on the units no longer sold, so total revenue rises (see diagram).

Higher profit. Because the firm sells slightly fewer units after the price rise, its total costs of production may fall at the same time as its revenue rises. Profit is the difference between total revenue and total cost, so profit is likely to increase.

Ability to pass on higher costs. If the firm’s costs rise, for example because of higher wages or an indirect tax, it can raise its price without losing many customers. It can protect its profit margins in a way that a firm with elastic demand could not.

Stable revenue and easier planning. Demand for an inelastic product changes little when prices change, so the firm’s sales and revenue are more predictable. This makes it easier to plan output, borrow money and invest in new products.

Why this scores
Defines price-inelastic demand with an example, then builds full chains of analysis: price rise → small fall in quantity → revenue rises → profit rises. The labelled diagram can earn up to 2 marks, but only if the answer explains what it shows. Stability of revenue and passing on costs are separate, developed advantages.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/25 Q2(c) · October/November 2025 · [6 marks]

Source material: The country produces a wide range of goods and services with different degrees of price elasticity of demand.

Analyse how price elasticity of demand can influence a firm’s pricing decisions.

Price elasticity of demand (PED) measures how responsive quantity demanded is to a change in price. A firm uses its PED to predict what will happen to its total revenue when it changes its price.

Elastic demand: lower the price. If demand is price-elastic (PED greater than 1), a fall in price causes a greater percentage rise in quantity demanded. Total revenue therefore rises when the price is cut. This is likely when the firm’s product has close substitutes, is not a necessity or takes up a large share of consumers’ incomes. A firm in this position is encouraged to lower its price, or at least avoid raising it, because a price rise would cause many customers to switch to rivals and revenue would fall.

Inelastic demand: raise the price. If demand is price-inelastic (PED less than 1), a rise in price causes a smaller percentage fall in quantity demanded, so total revenue rises. This is likely when the product has few substitutes, is a necessity or is a small share of income. The firm is encouraged to raise its price, because it will lose relatively few sales and its revenue will increase.

Price discrimination. If a firm sells to groups of customers with different PEDs, it can charge different prices for the same product. For example, a train company charges higher fares at peak times, when commuters must travel and demand is inelastic, and lower fares off-peak, when leisure travellers can choose another time or another form of transport and demand is elastic. This raises the firm’s total revenue from both groups.

Why this scores
Opens with a definition, then analyses both cases fully: the PED value, why demand might be that elastic, the pricing decision and the effect on revenue. Price discrimination with a real example is a separate developed point that the mark scheme credits.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

Discuss [8]

0455/22/M/J/25 Q2(d) · May/June 2025 · [8 marks]

Discuss whether or not the demand for chocolate is likely to be price elastic.

Demand is price-elastic when a change in price causes a greater percentage change in quantity demanded, so PED is greater than 1.

Why demand may be price-elastic. There are many close substitutes for chocolate, both other chocolate brands and other snacks such as biscuits or sweets. If one firm raises the price of its chocolate bars, consumers can easily switch to a rival brand, so quantity demanded falls by a larger percentage than the price rise. This applies especially to individual brands, which are much more price-elastic than chocolate as a whole.

Chocolate is also a luxury rather than a necessity. Nobody needs it to live, so if the price rises, especially for premium chocolate, consumers can cut back, buy it less often or postpone the purchase. Over a longer time period, consumers also have more opportunity to find alternatives and change their habits, so demand becomes more elastic over time.

Why demand may be price-inelastic. A chocolate bar is cheap and takes up only a small proportion of most people’s income, particularly in high-income countries. A 10% rise in the price of a bar is hardly noticed, so consumers keep buying roughly the same amount and quantity demanded falls by a smaller percentage than the price.

In addition, chocolate contains sugar and many people buy it out of habit, so for regular consumers it can be habit-forming. Strong brand loyalty, built up by advertising, also means consumers may stick with their favourite brand even when its price rises. Both make demand less responsive to price.

Conclusion. Demand for one particular brand of chocolate is likely to be price-elastic, because so many close substitutes exist. However, demand for chocolate as a whole is likely to be price-inelastic in high-income countries, because it is cheap relative to incomes and habit-forming. The answer depends on how widely the product is defined, the income of consumers, whether it is an everyday bar or an expensive luxury box, and the time period being considered.

Why this scores
This matches the Level 3 description: a definition first, two developed arguments on each side, each linked back to the size of the change in quantity demanded, and a conclusion that makes a judgement and states what it depends on (breadth of definition, income, type of chocolate, time). The examiner report praised answers that kept PED, not “more or less demand”, at the centre.
Written your own answer? Get it marked →
0455/22/O/N/20 Q4(d) · October/November 2020 · [8 marks]

Source material: It is predicted that the price elasticity of demand (PED) for cars will also change in the future.

Discuss whether or not demand for cars will become more price-elastic in the future.

Price elasticity of demand (PED) measures how responsive quantity demanded is to a change in price. Demand would become more price-elastic if a change in the price of cars caused a bigger percentage change in the quantity of cars demanded than it does now.

Why demand may become more price-elastic. Substitutes for owning a car are improving. Many cities are investing in faster and cheaper public transport, such as metro lines, and ride-hailing and car-sharing apps are spreading. If these alternatives become cheaper and more convenient, consumers can more easily choose not to buy a car when car prices rise, so quantity demanded will fall by a larger percentage.

There may also be more car producers and models to choose from, for example new firms making electric cars. With more close substitutes within the market, a rise in the price of one brand will lead buyers to switch to another. Consumers can also compare prices online easily. In addition, if car prices rise faster than incomes, cars will take up a larger proportion of income, which also makes demand more elastic.

Why demand may not become more price-elastic. For many people a car may become more of a necessity. If people move further from where they work, or live in rural areas with poor public transport, they have no real alternative, so they will keep buying cars even if prices rise. Habit also matters: people who are used to the convenience of driving are reluctant to give it up.

Furthermore, as incomes rise over time, in many economies a car takes up a smaller proportion of income than it used to. A price rise is then less noticeable, so the quantity demanded responds less. Strong brand loyalty to car makers may have the same effect.

Conclusion. Demand for cars is likely to become more price-elastic in large cities, where public transport and ride-sharing provide closer substitutes, and for individual brands as competition grows. It may become less elastic in rural areas or where incomes are rising quickly. Overall, it depends mainly on how good and how cheap the alternatives to car ownership become, and on how car prices change relative to incomes.

Why this scores
Starts with a definition, which the examiner report says helped strong candidates stay focused, and keeps every argument about the responsiveness of quantity demanded, not about whether demand rises or falls. It has two developed arguments on each side and a conclusion that depends on specific conditions (location, substitutes, prices relative to incomes).
Written your own answer? Get it marked →

Quick Checklist

  • In my answer I define PED (or elastic / inelastic) at the start of every 6- and 8-mark answer.
  • In my answer I write “percentage change in quantity demanded”, never just “demand goes up”.
  • In my answer I state whether PED is greater or less than 1 and what that means.
  • In my answer I link pricing decisions to total revenue, not to “more customers”.
  • In my answer I apply each determinant to every product the question names.
  • In my answer I end a “Discuss” with a judgement that depends on something specific: breadth of definition, income, time period or substitutes.
